# Śrīla Prabhupāda is Coming

## Dedication

**Śrīla Prabhupāda Is Coming!**  
**is an offering to**  
**His Divine Grace**  
**A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda,**  
**the pure devotee of the Lord**  
**who came to teach us to always remember and never forget**  
**the Supreme Personality of Godhead,**  
**Lord Sri Krishna,**  
**by chanting the His holy names:**

**Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna**  
**Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare**  
**Hare Rama, Hare Rama**  
**Rama Rama, Hare Hare**

## Preface

I was in the right place at the right time: I lived in India from early 1975 until mid-1977, and in those years Srila Prabhupada often stayed in India, especially Mayapur. Whenever he stayed in Mayapur, his sister Pisima visited him, and I was her personal servant.

Seeing Srila Prabhupada in America had also been wonderful, but generally I had seen him from a distance. Personal interactions were few and far between for rank and file devotees in women’s bodies. Still, I would have relished those rare moments my entire life and been satisfied with them, I suppose, had I not later lived in India and had so much personal association with Srila Prabhupada that I truly became satisfied.

In 1978 Satsvarupa dasa Goswami asked all of us who knew Srila Prabhupada to record our memories. I made four tapes, but none of my memories appeared in either *Srila Prabhupada Lilamrta* or *Prabhupada Nectar*. Eleven years later I asked Baladeva Vidyabusana dasa, who worked with Satsvarupa dasa Goswami, “Why weren’t my tapes used?”

“Did you receive transcripts of the tapes?” he asked.

“No.”

“That means your tapes were destroyed in the fire at the Gita Nagari farm. We didn’t know whose tapes burned, so we couldn’t notify you.”

I decided to make the tapes again, but five years passed before I finally made an arrangement with my good friend and Godsister Nanda-devi dasi, in Alachua, Florida, to spend one hour a week taping my memories. And by this time, 1992, when I consulted the outline I had made in 1978, some of the brief descriptions no longer meant anything to me. Thus some of my memories unfortunately perished. But with so many years behind me, I had a new perspective: the years we’d spent with Srila Prabhupada were now “the early days.” So my memories expanded into six tapes. Working from transcriptions of those tapes, I compiled this book.

Please excuse all mistakes. My memory is hardly perfect, but I tried to verify questionable details.

The story of how I came to Krishna consciousness is an embarrassing one, but it deserved to be told as part of my book because it proves that Srila Prabhupada was the greatest miracle worker. He scooped us out of the gutter and transformed us into devotees of Lord Krishna. Even the first chapters about my growing up attest to Srila Prabhupada’s greatness, for they reveal the family-and-city life I transcended later under his spiritual guidance. A reminder of this transition came while I was preparing my book for printing: my father’s life came to an end in New York.

Please excuse any offenses I knowingly or unknowingly committed at any devotee’s feet in writing this book. I especially beg for Srila Prabhupada’s mercy and forgiveness for all my offenses. My purpose is to glorify Srila Prabhupada, but it is impossible for this insignificant soul to properly glorify him because his glories are so vast. Still I made my small attempt in the hope that Srila Prabhupada will be satisfied that I tried.

I am happy to be finally sharing my memories with the society of devotees, both Srila Prabhupada’s *diksa* and *siksa* disciples, who are always eager to hear about our beloved spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada. May his glories always remain in our minds as we hanker for more of his personal association in our next lives, when we all go back to Godhead and have another ISKCON in the spiritual sky!

All glories to Srila Prabhupada!

*Your servant,*  
*Mahamaya-devi dasi*

*February 17, 2000*  
*Lord Nityananda’s Appearance Day*  
*Sridhama Mayapur*

## Introduction

Mahamaya-devi dasi, my dear friend and wife for twenty-two years, is very determined—like her namesake! Her firm desire to serve Srila Prabhupada has successfully shaped her life and character. I am honored to introduce her second book.

It is a narrative about her blissful efforts to serve Srila Prabhupada’s movement and her actual encounters with him. If you have a heart willing to be moved by the flow of a life in pursuit of a pure devotee’s mercy, you will discover an undeniable, cumulative, uplifting effect in the way her personal strivings to reach out to Srila Prabhupada resulted in his nurturing reciprocations with her.

I encouraged Mahamaya to depict her life prior to joining the Hare Krishna movement graphically, so as to contrast the person she was with the person she became in the association of the devotees and Srila Prabhupada. Srila Prabhupada’s faith moved mountains. He convinced languid, lost youths to build a spiritual movement of international scope. I am proud of Mahamaya’s part in Srila Prabhupada’s adventures, and I am sure she pleased Srila Prabhupada and made him proud of her, too.

Perhaps I should say more about who Srila Prabhupada was, but many of you are not strangers to him. And *Srila Prabhupada Is Coming!* will make you even more familiar with him. For this reason Mahamaya is very much worth knowing. So I encourage you to read on and become acquainted with both of them.

The devotee Srila Prabhupada and his devotee Mahamaya-devi dasi are spiritually father and daughter, and they share a spiritual, family business. In this, they are not easily separable, for their lives intertwine in simple loving exchanges centered on pleasing Krishna. Mahamaya deeply entered spiritual life by feeling strongly about what Srila Prabhupada wanted. She inherited his business and his struggle to perform devotional service perfectly.

The Krishna consciousness movement is a broad network of devotional friendships based on shared love for Krishna and his pure devotees. Mahamaya’s on-going, selfless work on the Srila Prabhupada Disciple Database shows me that she is in touch with this widely shared love. Her book is a further sharing of the love she has for Srila Prabhupada and Krishna with her many devotee friends as well as new readers.

Because I met my wife after almost all the events in this book transpired, I played no part in many of them. However, our paths crossed before we met. The morning I made my first appearance at *mangal-arati*, having shaved-up the night before, I was still wearing my street clothes. She remembered seeing me and wondering, “Who is that very tall man?” We didn’t actually meet at that time, and she soon went to India, but five years after that first chance meeting and just after she became my secretary in the New York temple, we were married.

A wife is described as a fortress for a husband. She protects his virtue and encourages his spiritual inclination. Those of us who are householders know that a wife’s or husband’s highest utility is simply being Krishna conscious and helping one’s spouse along the devotional path. Mahamaya has been my spiritual shelter on many occasions. It has been my pleasure to reciprocate with her by encouraging her writing efforts. I know that many devotees, as they take inspiration from the pastimes of Srila Prabhupada described here, will also feel the shelter offered by his enthusiastic follower, Mahamaya-devi dasi.

*Jnanagamya dasa*

## Prologue

**March, 1975**

Carrying boxed *prasadam* enroute to India, we devotees from the United States traveled together on Air India, stopping at London’s Heathrow Airport, where more devotees joined us, raising our number to about three hundred. We landed in Bombay but didn’t see more than the airport. Its slow-moving fans suspended from high ceilings epitomized my conception of India.

Several buses met our Bombay-to-Calcutta flight. As we endured delays, I was appalled at the number of children begging and clamoring around. Our left-over *prasadam* delighted the dirty kids.

As we headed out of the city, I felt even more dismayed seeing people who lived on traffic islands and bathed in gutters. Soon we were in the green, lush Bengali countryside, headed for Mayapur. People in the country were bathing at *pukkurs* (ponds) and washing their clothes. I thought, “Why don’t the people lured to cities by factory work return to their villages, where they can be clean, living near a *pukkur* and growing their own food?” Srila Prabhupada’s phrase, “You can’t eat nuts and bolts,” really made sense to me.

Although usually I was not openly emotional, as we got closer to Mayapur I choked up. On the verge of tears while watching the beautiful countryside from the bus window, I thought, “Soon I’ll finally be in Sridhama Mayapur, Lord Caitanya’s birthplace. It probably looks much the same as it did 489 years ago when He appeared there and started the Hare Krishna movement.” I tried to hide my emotions, but I became overwhelmed when someone shouted, “Look! Mayapur!” and I finally saw the saffron-colored Lotus Building across the rice fields. ISKCON Mayapur! It was like love at first sight, and I wanted to stay forever. A few weeks were not going to be enough.

The best part of our arrival in Mayapur was to greet and receive the person who brought us all there, a pure devotee of the Lord, Srila Prabhupada. I joined the waiting crowd soon after settling into the ladies’ simple quarters above the big kitchen; the men took rooms along the boundary wall. We all gathered at the partially constructed front gate to chant Hare Krishna while awaiting his arrival. More buses arrived, with another hundred devotees, and these devotees from various parts of the world also joined our *kirtan*. We enthusiastically chanted, “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare” for a long time in the hot sunlight, and finally we saw a speck on the road coming nearer and nearer. It was Srila Prabhupada’s car! Srila Prabhupada was coming! I started jumping and happily chanting louder. The car pulled up and stopped. Doors opened, and a diminutive golden elderly gentleman gracefully emerged: His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada. I fell to the ground and offered my heartfelt obeisances:

*nama om visnu padaya krsna prestaya bhutale*  
*srimati bhaktivedanta swamin iti namine*

*namaste sarasvati deve gaura vani pracarine*  
*nirvesesa sunyavadi pascatya desatarine*

“All glories to you, Srila Prabhupada!” I said.

\* \* \*

Why was I in this holiest of places, prostrating myself before my guru, chanting Sanskrit *mantras*? And who was this Vaisnava acarya, this great devotee of Visnu, or Krishna, who taught by his own example, with the potency to change my heart and life? How did I, a New Yorker and the daughter of intellectual Jewish parents, become transformed into an aspiring devotee of Lord Krishna?

## Part One: How I Came to Krishna Consciousness

. . . A learned scholar has been very much attracted to this movement because he has actually seen that it is turning hippies addicted to drugs into pure Vaisnavas who voluntarily become servants of Krishna and humanity. Even a few years ago, such hippies did not know the Hare Krishna mantra, but now they are chanting it and becoming pure Vaisnavas. Thus they are becoming free from all sinful activities, such as illicit sex, intoxication, meat-eating and gambling. This is practical proof of the effectiveness of the Hare Krishna movement . . . One may or may not know the value of chanting the Hare Krishna mantra, but if one somehow or other chants it, he will immediately be purified, just as one who takes a potent medicine will feel its effects, regardless of whether he takes it knowingly or unknowingly.

(*Srimad-Bhagavatam* 6.2.19, purport)

## Chapter 1 - Growing Up Without Krishna

September, 1965, was the worst month of my life. My parents got a divorce, my father immediately married his secretary, and my boy friend broke up with me. I turned nineteen, but I was miserable.

Little did I know that a special *sadhu* from India, a pure devotee of Lord Sri Krishna, docked in Boston harbor that September. I was also unaware that he later lived briefly on West 72nd Street in New York City, just around the corner from the place on 71st Street where my grandparents and two great aunts lived.

This holy man started the Hare Krishna movement in the summer of 1966, at 26 Second Avenue, a storefront on the Lower East Side. His name was His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, but his small band of followers first called him Swamiji. Later they became his dedicated disciples and lovingly called him Srila Prabhupada. Living in my birthplace, Srila Prabhupada taught New Yorkers to sing and chant the holy names of God: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. “Chant Hare Krishna and be happy,” he preached. They chanted and danced and did become happy. Taking the chanting into the streets and city parks, the devotees of Krishna performed *sankirtan*, or congregational chanting of the holy names.

Srila Prabhupada taught classes on the *Bhagavad-gita* (The Song of God), the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* (The Beautiful Story of the Personality of Godhead), and other Vaisnava scriptures. And during the very early hours of the morning, after resting only a few hours, he translated these books from Sanskrit into English, adding his purports. He fed his followers delicious vegetarian *prasadam*, food offered to God. He said he came to teach them what they’d all forgotten: their loving relationship with the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Lord Sri Krishna. His disciples opened temples in San Francisco, Montreal, Seattle, Boston and other cities within a few years.

Out of compassion Srila Prabhupada came to rescue us Westerners from a degraded life. I was not ready to hear his message. I was in illusion, *maya*, thinking I could enjoy the material world. I didn’t know I am part and parcel of God and therefore His eternal servant. Far from it! I denied God’s existence. The time wasn’t ripe for me to learn.

The first time I saw Hare Krishna devotees was in Berkeley, California, in the summer of 1968. A group of men dressed in saffron robes stood and chanted in front of the famous university: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. “They look like Buddhists,” I thought, “ready to set themselves on fire at any moment. This is probably a protest against the Vietnam War.”

My husband, Stan, and I stopped to watch the devotees. I felt attracted, wondering what they were doing. A woman, with a child in a stroller, joined their group, and this surprised me. “O.K.,” I thought, “I can understand men being renounced, but a woman?” I was impressed. We listened a while, then went away. I had no inkling that “Hare” and “Krishna” were names of Godhead, nor did I understand that this was a religious practice. I wasn’t into religion.

I was born on September 29, 1946, in New York City, because my mother’s obstetrician lived there, but I grew up in White Plains, a suburb in affluent Westchester County, forty-five minutes north of New York City, by car or train.

My parents, also born in New York City, considered themselves Jewish, but were influenced by atheism. Judaism was an ethnic identity they instilled in me in many ways, especially by sending me to Sunday school at our synagogue, where I learned about my heritage. Our rabbi’s concept of God—not very inspiring—was “a power of goodness.”

Two Catholic neighborhood kids once accused me: “You Jews killed Jesus Christ!” I was astounded, as I didn’t heavily identify with being Jewish. I did not look the part, and my family had always celebrated Christmas with a decorated tree in our living room. One year, we decided to celebrate Hannukah instead, but we hated missing Christmas and never did that again. Of course, the religious significance of Christmas meant nothing to us.

As children we all sometimes contemplate the vast night sky and feel very small, but God wasn’t a part of my life. I believed that people had concocted God as a crutch. Yet I left a small opening in my heart and mind for the possibility that God exists, just in case.

My father, David A. Boehm, a book publisher, started his own company, Sterling Publishing Company, Inc., in 1949. Sterling specialized in “how-to” books and became quite successful by the late 1950’s. Its best-selling book was the *Guinness Book of World Records*. My father invited the author, an Englishman named Norris McWhirter, to visit New York to further popularize the book. Our houseguest appeared on several popular TV shows, such as *What’s My Line?* and *To Tell The Truth*, as well as a few radio shows.

Once, Norris and I conspired: while he was on the radio, I called the talk show, got on the air and asked him to state an obscure world record—maybe something about how high frogs jump—which he did with ease, of course, because it was preplanned. After I did that, a minute later our phone rang. My sister Diana, listening to the radio show at her home, had been thrilled to recognize her little sister’s voice on the radio.

There were five of us kids in our family, but we didn’t grow up together. From the time I was an infant, my two older sisters, Suzanne and Diana, lived with our paternal grandparents, also in White Plains. Their mother, my father’s first wife, had died immediately after Diana’s birth.

I was only seven when eighteen-year-old Suzanne got married. She moved to California and rarely visited New York, so I hardly knew her. During one unforgettable visit she took me to see the circus movie, *The Greatest Show on Earth*. Her children are not much younger than me.

Diana moved back to our large, redbrick house on Cobb Avenue while she attended high school. Then she majored in elementary education at a university in upper New York State. After graduating, in 1960, she married a graduate of Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute, and I was her bridesmaid. She taught school on Long Island until she became a mother. When her husband advanced in his career, they moved to Portland, Oregon.

My younger brother, Dave, went to boarding school and came home for only two weeks each summer. At seventeen, while attending high school in Rutland, Vermont, he met a local girl, Liz, whom he fell in love with and later married.

So basically I grew up with just my older brother, Lincoln, a brilliant student. Considering that my parents valued intellectualism, it was difficult for me to grow up in his shadow. I was a good student, but I felt unnoticed. Lincoln, after only one year at Columbia University, my father’s alma mater, married Suzy, his childhood sweetheart, at a gala wedding in a New York City hotel.

Working summers, and with both parents’ financial support, they continued their studies. Soon after graduation, their first son was born. Lincoln’s career in publishing was a given.

Both my father and my mother, Peggy, were editors and authors. Although my father usually used a pen name, as the American Editor of the *Guinness Book of World Records* he used his real name. Books were everywhere at home, and I read constantly the current best-sellers and whatever else I got my hands on. Sometimes my father gave me the manuscripts of children’s books to read, and he asked my opinion whether or not to publish them. I would read galleys—the long strips of typeset paper that are almost ready for printing. Even as a young girl, I worked during school vacations at my father’s office in New York City, proofreading with the staff, stuffing envelopes, or typing invoices. By twelve, I had taught myself to type on the manual typewriter in my father’s home office.

I was raised on small doses of television because my parents didn’t own a TV until I was eight. Before that, we neighborhood kids used to gather at one family’s home at six p.m., without fail, to watch *Superman*, and on Saturday mornings, for the animal story *My Friend Flicka*. Once we had our own television, our family’s weekly shows included *Father Knows Best*; the legal adventures of Perry Mason; and the quiz show, *The $64,000 Question*.

The highlights of my childhood were the three summers I stayed at a camp called Blueberry Cove, in Maine. Children from many different backgrounds and ethnic groups came to this camp, the antithesis of a Jewish camp I later attended and hated.

The same summer as Diana’s wedding, my parents took Lincoln and I to Europe on one of their semi-annual business-and-pleasure trips. We toured England and Scotland in a rented car, flew to the Scandinavian countries, and also drove through Germany, Holland and France. At fourteen I spent a summer at a traveling camp that went through Mexico, Arizona and New Mexico. The camp leaders were a minister in the Dutch Orthodox church and his wife. While traveling in the car we would discuss the nature of life, and by the end of the summer I believed in reincarnation.

One day, when I was in high school, radio news got flashed into all the classrooms: Someone shot President Kennedy and he had been rushed to a hospital. School was dismissed early, so I ran home and turned on the television. Soon I learned that he was dead. That gruesome weekend Americans didn’t budge from the television. I wasn’t interested in politics, but Kennedy represented brilliance, vigor, youth and hope. I identified with him because, being Catholic, he was also in a religious minority. His assassination seemed to end a promising era in America.

My maternal grandparents were very much part of our family. They lived in New York City for most of my childhood, except for a few years when they lived on the third floor of our large house in White Plains. They returned to the same West 71st Street block, between Broadway and Columbus, near Grandma’s two sisters. I was close to Aunt Nora, one of my great-aunts, whom I affectionately nicknamed Lolo.

In school I liked logic and math. I was quite athletic and excelled in every sport, even though I’m only five feet tall. I became a cheerleader in my last year of high school. Hankering to be popular, I suffered because I rarely went to parties or dated.

I joined a club for teenagers at our local YMCA. My club, the M & M’s, chose me to represent them in a statewide law-making contest. We had to come up with a beneficial state law. Another girl came up with a great idea: the current law requiring one year of residency in New York to vote needed to be eliminated for voting in national elections. I wrote and presented the new bill to the other clubs at the YMCA and won the local competition. Therefore I represented White Plains in the county contest, which I also won. Then, representing Westchester County, I was sent to Albany, the state capital, for a mock session of state congress with kids representing every county.

We kids, pretending to be lawmakers, used the actual senate hall for our meetings. A bill book was prepared, including only the best bills, and I was shocked to see my bill was number two! I nervously read my presentation to the large mock senate of kids, and the bill passed unanimously. Later, I learned that an almost identical bill was in the works in the real state senate and also passed there.

Our local White Plains newspaper carried the story of these events, but I was chagrined not to see my name mentioned. Soon after that, another achievement of mine also went unacknowledged: my name and role were omitted on the printed program for our senior-class skit. I wondered, “Why am I not getting recognized for the good things I’ve done?”

In 1964, I graduated from high school as an honors student, and I was pleased to finally receive some recognition for my intelligence by scoring in the 99th+ percentile on both the Mathematics and English Composition tests in a national competition, the Scholastic Achievement Tests (SAT).

All my life, my family had expected me to go to college. That’s what “everyone” did. But I had no sure goals, no fixed ideas what to do with my life. Having worked hard for good grades in high school, I was tired of studying. So I chose the University of Massachusetts (UMass) because I had heard it was an easy school for out-of-state students. I mainly wanted to get away from my parents and have fun.

Sure enough, it was easy. The first year was just a repeat of my final year in high school, but it wasn’t as much fun as I expected. The first semester I majored in psychology, for the same reason that many others did: I thought I was going crazy and wanted to know why. But there were no pat answers. It was all too wishy-washy. Therefore I switched to elementary education, emulating my sister Diana.

## Chapter 2 - Going Beyond the Establishment

The memorable month of September, 1965: My parents’ divorce hurt me, even though I saw them constantly bicker for most of their twenty-four years of marriage; I didn’t particularly like my father’s new wife; and my boy friend, whom I wanted to marry, dumped me. I felt devastated.

I returned to UMass for another semester, with a new major in English literature, but my favorite course was calculus, because logic fascinated me. I then took a leave of absence for one semester, or so I thought.

What happened first was that a friend named Judy and I went to Boston and got clerical jobs at Campbell & Hall, a book wholesaler. Living cheap on Beacon Street, near the Massachusetts Avenue bridge, we befriended unsavory characters. Soon we were leading degraded lives, partying in the evenings and on weekends.

That’s when I met Stan, an aspiring operatic tenor. We eloped in May, 1966, and agreed to stay together until we grew tired of our relationship. While Stan studied singing at the Boston Conservatory of Music, I worked as a secretary at Harvard University Press.

Then I changed to a better-paying job with a NASA space project at Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). In January, 1968, I decided to go back to college at UMass’s new Boston branch. I switched to a part-time job at MIT, in its City Planning Department, but I had a full schedule: three days of work, alternating with three days of courses.

After one semester, however, Stan and I decided to move to California. Boston was too conservative. We had heard about Haight-Ashbury, the hip district of San Francisco, and thought we’d be happier there, with the hippies. After driving cross-country, we found jobs, and an apartment near 16th and Mission in San Francisco.

We checked out the Haight, but the good days were over, and the drug scene wasn’t for us. Our seeing the devotees at that time in Berkeley was just another unusual sight in California. We soon found what we thought we were looking for: gross hedonism. I tried to convince myself I was happy doing this, but deep in my heart I felt no satisfaction.

When I’d married Stan, we had purchased a used sports car, a light blue Triumph TR-3, which I loved. Then it was stolen off the streets of San Francisco. I was so attached to it that I cried.

However, around this time I rejected the establishment as pointless and purposeless; there had to be more to life than an education, a job, a family, and then dying. And after a year in San Francisco we were disenchanted with city life. So in the summer of 1969 we moved a hundred and fifty miles north to Mendicino, a town built high on some bluffs overlooking the Pacific. It was a small artists’ colony, and many of Mendicino’s quaint buildings were art galleries.

For several months, before it got cold, we camped in a trailer park. We became vegetarians, adopting a Zen macrobiotic diet, eating mostly brown rice garnished with tamari sauce and sesame salt. It was easy to cook on a campfire, and inexpensive.

While camping, I got a secretarial job in a lawyers’ office. When the weather turned cold, the lawyers offered Stan and I the rental of a darling, A-frame house in the woods. Stan worked at home, making collages that he’d sell to an art gallery—a new one, run by a hippie named Alfonso and three others, all members of a hippie family.

Gradually, we became members of this family, and our lives changed dramatically after they convinced us to graduate from smoking dope to taking acid (LSD) and other psychedelic drugs. I stopped going to work, and when the lawyers finally evicted us from the house, we gave away all our possessions, including our costly bedroom set and a new upright piano, brought by movers from Massachusetts. We were now homeless hippies—full-fledged! Alfonso invited us into his house, already a crash-pad for family members.

Thinking high thoughts about giving being the ultimate loving gesture, I gave away my most prized possession—a gorgeous opal ring I had designed as a teenager—which had been made in New York City’s jewelry district.

Stan and I separated. Our lives had divided, especially after my second LSD trip, when I freaked out, realizing, “I am not this body—this lump of matter.” But what was I? I didn’t know. I was trapped in my lifeless body, identifying with it. I became very withdrawn, often unable to talk or respond when people spoke to me. “They are talking to my body,” I thought, “and it’s dead.”

During that winter I lived in San Francisco and crashed at apartments with other “family” members. I think they felt responsible that I was now a zombie.

One girl named Linda had a necklace made of beads, with knots between them. She’d gotten it at the Hare Krishna temple. They were meditation beads, and she wore them like jewelry.

I heard about Hare Krishna then in other ways, too. The musical Hair had a song that included the Hare Krishna mantra—Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare—followed by “marijuana, marijuana, -juana -juana, mari- mari-,” sung to the same tune. I started singing Hare Krishna because the vibration made me feel good.

Linda talked about the great time she’d had at a commune named Banana Patch on the island of Maui in Hawaii. It sounded like paradise, and I thought about going there.

When the weather warmed up, in March, 1970, I moved to the woods on the banks of the Navarro river outside Mendicino. One day I was walking down the highway when a car pulled over. It was my mother! She was so worried about me that she’d flown in from New York. I can’t remember much about her visit, except that I offered her some marijuana. She left, probably more worried than when she arrived.

One day I got a phone call from Stan’s cousin in Massachusetts. His wife asked me for a favor: “My brother Joey is in big trouble. He’s in Berkeley right now. Would you help him out?” I agreed.

I met Joey, who was, indeed, in trouble. Although young, he dealt heroin and had killed a Mafia man. He said, “It was an accident, but that doesn’t matter—the Mafia is after me.” He was scared, running for his life.

I said, “I was thinking of going to Hawaii.”

“Please bring me along,” he begged. “Once I’m in Hawaii, I’ll travel somewhere and hide.”

First, we hitchhiked to Mendicino to get my sleeping bag, which I had stashed in a hollow tree. But while hitching back, I forgot my sleeping bag on the road. Now I had only the clothes on my back. So much for worldly possessions!

At the San Francisco airport I bought two tickets for Hawaii, with a bad check written on my former bank account. I got away with it because my driver’s license was valid.

On the airplane, Joey and I dropped acid. It was a weird trip because we were cooped up in the plane. One hippie, recognizing our intoxicated vibes, kept looking back at me. When we landed in Honolulu, Joey quickly disappeared, and I befriended the tall, thin hippie named Pierre, who also taking the on-going flight to Maui.

There, Pierre brought me to McKenna Beach. Leaving our clothes on the shore, we dove into the beautiful clear water and went swimming for a long time. After coming out, we discovered my clothes were gone. Probably the tide had washed them out. Pierre loaned me his T-shirt, which reached my knees. No problem! I needed clothes only when I went to town, because McKenna Beach was a “free” beach—everyone lived there nude.

I lived by the hippie code of ethics: “Make love, not war!” But I got tired of men taking advantage of me. Hedonism wasn’t all that it was cracked up to be. Despite this, or maybe because of it, many on McKenna Beach wanted spiritualism. One guy had a book about Jesus and fasting. I read his book, and tried it, but after three days of fasting, I couldn’t continue.

Somebody told me about the Ba’hai faith, and I went to several of their meetings. Their idea was that Krishna, Mohammed, and Jesus were all prophets and all the “chosen ones.” A Ba’hai leader reported on his recent journey to California to attend a peace festival. He glorified the Hare Krishna devotees for distributing free food there.

I visited Banana Patch, but it had mostly folded. A few people still lived on the land. “We’re farming for God,” they said.

A yogi named Sai lived in Hanna and had fifty followers. I saw them around and thought they were the Hare Krishna people, but they dressed scantily like the hippies on Maui, and the men had long hair. A friend told me: “Have you seen their car, with the Hare Krishna mantra painted on it? If you’re hitchhiking, they always pick you up. And they never stop chanting!” I did not want to get a ride from those people!

Hearing favorable reports about Sai’s Saturday “psychic sleep” program, however, I hitchhiked to Hanna, only to be turned away by a hepatitis quarantine. I never tried again.

Big Brother and the Holding Company played a concert in Lahaina that summer. Sai’s people were outside the hall chanting Hare Krishna. I stood at the back of the crowd and sang along. It was great, but I didn’t want them to see me. “They are too happy,” I thought. “I don’t want to be part of them.” After a while I went inside—after all, I had paid for a ticket. But it was unexciting after the high of chanting Hare Krishna.

Word spread in June or July that Jimi Hendrix would come to film the movie Rainbow Bridge, in the island’s crater, a sort of natural concert hall. His concert was free. The hippies streamed in, scaling the extinct volcano from every direction. Everyone around me was chanting Hare Krishna and climbing: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. It seemed like the whole world was vibrating Hare Krishna. Reaching the top, I was handed a cookie by Sai’s people, who said, “This is *prasadam*, spiritual food.” Later, I learned that Sai read Srila Prabhupada’s books and was following the instruction in *Bhagavad-gita* to offer food to Lord Krishna. Several months later, he and his followers joined the Hare Krishna movement.

In August, 1970, Stan and I reunited in Maui. After deciding to move back to Massachusetts, we stopped for several days in Honolulu. The Hare Krishna devotees were in the practice of chanting on the sidewalk in front of McDonald’s, in two lines, facing each other. To go past them, you had to walk between their two lines. Intimidated by this, we didn’t walk through the devotees’ lines into McDonald’s; we felt guilty about eating meat after a year and a half of vegetarianism. Instead, we stood at the back of a crowd that gathered, chanting Hare Krishna with them.

One devotee offered us a Back to Godhead magazine, but we refused it. He succeeded in giving us an invitation to a Sunday feast, but we didn’t go. Even though we had only five dollars to last us a week, we still didn’t go to their free feast.

Back in Orleans, Massachusetts, staying with Stan’s mother, we tried to resume “normal” life. I got a job as a nurse’s aide in an old-age home, but I hated it and quit after three weeks.

Then I was completely bored, and slept a lot, just to avoid living. In my room I set up a small altar and a plaster Buddha. Burning incense, I chanted Hare Krishna to the tunes I’d heard in Hawaii or on the *Hair* album. It just felt good, so I sang it. I especially liked George Harrison’s song, “My Sweet Lord,” in which he sings Hallelujah and Hare Krishna. I still didn’t know what Hare Krishna meant.

One day I cut my long hair quite short. I had a premonition: “I’m going to have to cut it, anyway,” as though some day I’d have to admit that I liked chanting enough to join the Hare Krishna movement. I thought the ladies shaved their heads, too.

Stan and I wanted to return to California. A friend, a local bartender, also wanted to go. In December, 1970, the three of us paid for a ride to San Francisco on a stripped-down school bus. The motor broke down somewhere in Ohio. Peter, the bus owner, didn’t have the money for repairs, so we were stuck, parked behind a popular bar. One after another, the other riders abandoned the bus to hitchhike the rest of the way. Stan and his friend also wanted to do the same, but I didn’t. So they left without me, saying, “We’ll meet you in San Francisco.” Eventually, I was the only one left. Even Peter had left to get some help.

I lived in the bus for three weeks. It was very cold, so I spent a lot of time in the bar, where I’d greet people: “Peace! Hare Krishna!” “My Sweet Lord” played often on the jukebox.

Returning with a borrowed car, Peter picked me up and drove us back to Boston to get some money for the repairs. Depositing me with some Northeastern University students, Peter said, “You can stay in my buddies’ dormitory until I’m ready to go.”

I didn’t like his friends at all, especially because they harassed me for chanting Hare Krishna. Thus, after getting stoned on New Year’s Eve, I wanted out. Leaving a message for Peter, with the phone number of Stan’s best friend since childhood, Bob, I left.

Wearing only moccasins and a blanket slit in the middle, instead of a coat, I walked all day in snow and a cold wind, determined to find Bob’s house in a Boston suburb. When I finally found it, only Carol, his wife, and their two children were home. She was shocked to see me dressed as a hippie and to hear me constantly chanting Hare Krishna. They were straight, middle-class people. Bob was a successful typewriter-ribbon salesman, and they owned their home and never took drugs.

When Bob arrived, Carol took him aside and probably said, “Get rid of her!”

Bob called the temple, unbeknownst to me, but he told me later that he said: “I’ve got a girl here who chants Hare Krishna. Will you take her?”

After getting an affirmative answer, Bob suggested to me, “Why don’t we go to the Hare Krishna temple? I just read an article about it in the magazine *Boston After Dark*.”

I refused to go: “No way!”

Why? For the same reason I used to hide when I saw the chanting party: Those people were too happy, and I was suffering but wouldn’t admit it. So Bob arranged a “date” for me with his neighbor.

“We’ll all go to a party at the Hare Krishna temple,” he said. This was acceptable, for I envisioned taking drugs and maybe having an orgy—how else were those people so happy? Sexual happiness and intoxication had produced more pain for me than lasting pleasure, but still I considered sense enjoyment my aim of life. Raw, vulnerable, and truly degraded, I always felt ready for another opportunity to “enjoy.”

## Chapter 3 - Boston—January, 1971

We drove to the temple, a large house at 40 North Beacon Street in Allston, on the edge of Boston, on Sunday, January 3, 1971. This was my first visit to a temple—two-and-a-half years after I first saw devotees. Arriving at eleven p.m., we were greeted by *brahmacaris* (celibate male students) who were cleaning the house after the Sunday feast. Kulasekhara dasa, a young Englishman, escorted me into one room, while Bob and his friend entered another room, then exited out the front door. I didn’t notice them leave.

Sitting on a bare floor in an unfurnished room, I listened to Kulasekhara talk about Srila Prabhupada as an authorized teacher of spiritual knowledge, teaching that we’re not these bodies, but spirit souls, part and parcel of God. Kulashekara said we’re unhappy because we’ve forgotten God, and that chanting Hare Krishna awakens our dormant love of God, as it cleanses the dirt from our hearts. Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare: This was the *maha-mantra*; *“maha”* means “great,” and *“mantra”* means “to deliver the mind.”

Kulashekhara said that when we admit we are suffering, we begin to become self-realized because then we ask, “Who am I? Why am I suffering?” If we are sincere, Krishna helps us meet a spiritual master, to learn the Vedic answers to our questions.

“Srila Prabhupada is such a spiritual master,” he said. “He can teach you to love God, because he loves God.”

It was news to me that these people had a guru and a philosophy. Even though he presented things clearly, I twisted everything in my mind. I thought I was God.

But what I liked best was that he was not trying to take advantage of me. That was a relief compared to what I’d been experiencing lately. I felt that everything he said was meant to help me, and I felt safe. I liked the exotic atmosphere and the aromas in the temple. It was brightly painted, and the lack of furniture was a stark contrast to ordinary life.

Kulasekhara gave me some *carinamrta* to sip—the Deities’ bath-water, mixed with yogurt and sugar. It tasted so wonderful that I thought, “This is ambrosia from the gods!” Although Kulasekhara looked like an ascetic—shaved head, simply seated on a folded *chaddar*, or large shawl, in the cold room—still I thought, “This man is really enjoying!”

I don’t remember agreeing to stay the night, but very early the next morning, some devotees found me, curled up in my blanket.

Someone then brought me to the ladies’ ashram, a room in the attic over the third floor. After bathing, I was helped into a brightly printed sari. All the other girls wore either solid yellow or solid saffron saris, depending on whether or not they were married, so this sari made me stand out. Later that day I was given a coat and boots to wear. We went outdoors to chant Hare Krishna on the streets.

Soon I was in Harvard Square with this small group of devotees singing Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare to my heart’s content. I was finally admitting that I liked to chant. We were just down the street from Harvard University Press, and I didn’t care if some employee passing by recognized me. We chanted for hours in the cold. I loved every minute of it.

“Chanting Hare Krishna on the streets is called *harinam sankirtan*,” a devotee told me. “This is new to America, but the most recent Krishna *avatar*, Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu, started the Hare Krishna movement in West Bengal, India, about five hundred years ago by chanting on the streets.”

We took turns distributing *Back to Godhead* (BTG) magazines and Simply Wonderfuls, sweetballs made from butter, powdered milk and sugar. A devotee told me, “These sweetballs acquired their name when our guru ate one and said it was simply wonderful.” If a Simply fell on the ground, we’d brush off the dirt and eat it. “It’s *prasadam*, food offered to Krishna,” went the explanation, “so it can’t be contaminated. *Prasadam* is mercy, so don’t waste it.” As for magazine distribution: “If people say they don’t have twenty-five cents for the magazine, but promise to read it, give them the BTG free.”

Back at the temple we had our main meal of hot *prasadam*. I loved the food, except for the soup made from *dahl* beans, which I found too spicy. But I thought I could learn to like *dahl*, given time. Jadurani dasi sat next to me and introduced herself as the wife of the temple president, Satsvarupa dasa, who was then in Dallas looking for a suitable building for a *gurukula*, or school. As I ate, Jadurani talked about Lord Krishna, Lord Caitanya, Lord Jagannatha and Srila Prabhupada. All these Sanskrit names were too much for me. I got everything confused. I’m a visual person, so without seeing the words, these sounds—including the devotees’ Sanskrit names—were jumbled in my mind.

Ananga Manjari dasi, who was “Elaine prabhu” at that time, a seventeen-year-old from the Boston area who had been there a few months, took charge of me. She bubbled over with so much enthusiasm that she bounced around the temple. She called me “Emily prabhu,” explaining, “We call each other ‘prabhu,’ or master, because we are all servants of the servants of Krishna.”

She asked Carol, the nine-year-old daughter of a devotee, to take me into the second-floor temple room for the first time. I thought I might do something wrong. “Just do whatever I do,” Carol advised.

She offered her *dandavats*, falling face down on the floor in full obeisances, and I followed her example. Getting up, I saw two altars. On the left altar were small Deities of Lord Krishna and His consort, Radharani. I felt a wonderful vibration emanating from Their direction. Seeing a *pujari* (priest) exit from the altar into a back room, I had a wicked thought: “She must be going into the room where they hold orgies—that’s why I feel this vibration!” What a delusion! The *pujari* was going to get incense for Radha-Krishna, who were the source of the blissful vibration.

On the right altar stood three large Deities: Lord Jagannatha, Lord Balarama and Lady Subhadra. Very unusual looking, these Deities dominated my vision. Carol informed me, “Lord Jagannatha is God.”

I didn’t believe it, and I mentally challenged Lord Jagannatha: “If you’re really God, You are going to have to prove it to me!”

Just then, it seemed, He winked at me. I thought, “I’m just hallucinating!”

That night I attended a beautiful ceremony called *arati*. Seeing the Deities, I again challenged Lord Jagannatha: “If You are God, You will have to prove it to me!” Again He seemed to wink at me! I did not doubt it after that: “O.K. I accept—You are God!”

A *pujari* stood in front of the altar and blew a conchshell loud and clear, making a magnificent sound, to signal the start of the *arati*. While the devotees chanted and danced, the *pujari* rang a bell in one hand, and with the other gracefully offered the Deities various items: incense, a burning lamp made with ghee-soaked cotton wicks, water in a conchshell, a hand-decorated handkerchief, flowers, and two fans made from a yak’s tail and peacock feathers.

The thirty-minute ceremony ended with more blasts on the conchshell. Our chanting came to a rousing conclusion, and then everyone offered obeisances on the floor while the *pujari* recited Sanskrit mantras, to which the devotees responsively exclaimed, “Jaya!” the way Christians say “Amen”:

“All glories to the assembled devotees!”

“Jaya Sri Krishna!”

“All glories to the assembled devotees!”

“Jaya Sri Krishna!”

“All glories to the assembled devotees!”

“Jaya Sri Krishna!”

“All glories to Sri Guru and Gauranga! All glories to Srila Prabhupada!”

Sitting cross-legged on the floor, we then responsively sang prayers to Lord Nrsimha-deva. Pointing, Ananga Manjari asked, “See that picture on the altar? That’s Lord Nrsimha-deva, the half-man, half-lion incarnation of Krishna, who protects His devotees.”

*Tulasi* worship was next—a similar but shorter *arati* ceremony. We danced around *tulasi*, a small, potted tree on a table at the back of temple room. When the singing ended, we all bowed down. Then the devotees got up and quickly headed downstairs to the *prasadam* room for *maha-prasadam*, or remnants from the Deities’ plate. But I didn’t get up. I was intently staring at the floor, which seemed to be alive. Ananga-manjari came back upstairs to get me.

While we ate a handful of *maha-prasadam* and drank hot, sweet milk, a devotee read from a book, *Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead*—which they abbreviated to “the *Krishna* book”—about Lord Krishna, a beautiful, blue-complexioned cowherd boy who wears a peacock feather in His hair and plays a flute, enchanting the residents of His village, Vrindaban.

As I listened, I had an unusual thought: “I’ve heard this before. It is way back in my memory, so far back that I didn’t know it was there.”

The next morning I got up at four a.m. It was tough, and the lack of sleep made me feel faint. We went to the temple room for *mangal-arati*. *“Mangal* means auspicious,” Ananga Manjari told me. “It’s the best time of day to greet the Lord.”

I don’t remember clearly everything that happened those first few days. Perhaps I was still high from New Years’ Eve, but I didn’t think so. The devotees thought otherwise.

\* \* \*

Ananga Manjari-devi dasi: One morning, just before sunrise, the devotees found a girl sleeping on the floor of the *prasadam* room. A *brahmacari* asked me to find out who she was and why she was there. I gently woke her up and asked her what her name was and how she got here. She was incoherent and spaced out, so I didn’t get much information at that time. I told her she was in the Hare Krishna temple, and that I would try to help her if she wanted me to. She was quite unresponsive to anything, but at the same time she was not resistant. I could see it was difficult for her to move much at the time—she seemed to be very intoxicated. So I gave her *japa* beads and showed her how to chant while I sat peacefully with her and chanted. She tried to chant, but not very successfully.

At breakfast, I encouraged her to eat, but she couldn’t even manage that very well, so I put some sweet burfy in her mouth and explained what *prasadam* is. Then I took her upstairs and let her sleep in my room. She seemed to need to sleep off the intoxication. Later, I gave her a towel and some toiletries and showed her where the bathroom and shower were. She seemed appreciative and happy and much more alert. She told me her name was Emily.

Then I took her downstairs and gave her breakfast. Now she was totally coherent and enthusiastic and happy about where she was. She even wanted to go out on *harinam sankirtan* with me. She was very enthusiastic. I found out that she had been taking LSD with some friends and had started chanting Hare Krishna. They couldn’t stop her, so they dropped her off at the temple in the middle of the night. A *brahmacari* let her in and let her sleep in the *prasadam* room. From then on, she was a very happy, intelligent and sincere devotee of Lord Krishna.

\* \* \*

The more I heard the philosophy, the clearer it became: Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and we are His servants, who can perform devotional service, or *bhakti-yoga*, putting Krishna’s pleasure ahead of our own. At first, it was hard to accept God as a person, though I admitted the possibility. After chanting Hare Krishna for some time, it became more than a possibility—it was logical. God is supreme, so we can’t be more than He is, and we are persons, so God must be the supreme person. The important difference is that His personality is not material like ours. Our bodies are temporary, ignorant, and prone to misery. His body is *sat-cit-ananda*: eternal, full of knowledge and bliss.

Devotees kept describing this knowledge as coming from a guru, but I couldn’t figure out who that guru was, because the Sanskrit words totally confused me. I rightly concluded that “Srila Prabhupada” meant the guru, but who was that? The main person telling me about the philosophy was Jadurani, so in my confusion I thought Jadurani was the guru.

Then, one day, walking down the temple stairs, I noticed a poster announcing Srila Prabhupada’s 1969 visit to Boston. I read the name “Srila Prabhupada” and saw his photo. Thus it finally dawned on me: *“This* is Srila Prabhupada, the guru!”

My cloud of *maya*, illusion, slowly dissipated. I realized that the happy, bright faces of the devotees came from purification, not intoxication and sex. I began to regret performing so many sinful activities and wondered how I would qualify to go back to Godhead. Devotees assured me, “Chant Hare Krishna and engage in devotional service. You’ll get Srila Prabhupada and Krishna’s mercy, and that will be your qualification for developing love of God.”

I settled into the routine starting at four a.m., when Jadurani prabhu woke us up, invariably saying, “It’s time to get up and serve Srila Prabhupada!” It was difficult to get adjusted, but hearing those words helped me remember why I was getting up so early. *Mangal-arati* started at four-thirty a.m., when it was still dark outside. The Deities were dressed in night outfits, without jewelry or garlands. A very somber tape of Srila Prabhupada singing the *“Guruvastaka”* prayer played as we swayed back and forth. The *arati* ended with a live *kirtan*, dissipating the more serious mood.

Afterward, the men stayed in the temple room to read *Srimad-Bhagavatam* aloud. The ladies trooped downstairs to the *prasadam* room to make flower garlands for the Deities, who were bathed and dressed each morning, and one of the men read to us. There were no lectures or discussions—just readings from the *Srimad-Bhagavatam*, First Canto, the original edition printed by Srila Prabhupada in India.

Garlands finished, we returned to the temple room to chant Hare Krishna on our beads. For my *japa*, or personal meditation, Ananga Manjari prabhu gave me a string of wooden beads and taught me to hold one bead between the thumb and middle finger of my right hand, twirling it as I chanted the *maha-mantra*: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. Completing the string of one-hundred-and-eight beads, called a round, I touched the head bead, or Krishna bead, to my forehead and reversed my direction on the string for the next round.

After four days I was chanting sixteen rounds, the recommended number, keeping track of them on small counter-beads attached to my beadbag. I liked chanting *japa* because it focused my attention on hearing the holy names more than singing did, as I was used to doing.

“Hearing is more important than chanting,” Ananga Manjari pointed out. “Listen to each syllable as you chant Hare Krishna and soon Krishna will dance on your tongue!”

At seven a.m. the altar curtains were opened, and we saw the Deities freshly dressed in opulent clothing fit for royalty, wearing the flower garlands we had just made. Fragrant incense billowed forth from the altar as we greeted the Deities. Then, standing around the special seat reserved for Srila Prabhupada called the *vyasasana* (“the seat of Vyasa,” the compiler of the Vedic literature, whom Srila Prabhupada represented), we sang the *“Guruvastaka”* prayers again, glorifying Prabhupada, and said the English translation.

Breakfast always hit the spot after four hours of devotional service. During the day the devotees did various services. No matter how busy anyone was, everyone stopped in the afternoon to pile into a temple van for *sankirtan*. Srila Prabhupada wanted us to go out chanting daily. “An hour a day keeps *Maya* away!” someone quipped.

Ananga Manjari taught me to be “revolutionary clean,” as Srila Prabhupada had said. She told me, “Bathe three times a day: in the morning when you get up, after going to the toilet to answer the call of nature, and in the evening. Do not bring your beads into the bathroom, and always wash your hands and mouth after eating. Sacred books, or *sastras*, should never touch the floor or be touched with dirty hands. If anything used in Krishna’s service falls on the floor, pick it up, touch it to your forehead, and say Hare Krishna!” Srila Prabhupada was quoted as saying, “Cleanliness is not next to godliness—it is *godliness*.”

I learned what the devotees said when they bowed down, the Sanskrit prayers meaning, “I offer my respectful obeisances unto His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, who is very dear to Lord Krishna, having taken shelter at His lotus feet. Our respectful obeisances are unto you, O spiritual master, servant of Sarasvati Gosvami. You are kindly preaching the message of Lord Caitanya and delivering the Western countries, which are filled with impersonalism and voidism.”

ISKCON Press, the publishing arm of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness (ISKCON), was based in the Boston temple. When I said I could proofread, the devotees gave me a small book, *Easy Journey to Other Planets*, by Srila Prabhupada, to proofread before it was reprinted. They said, “We know there’s at least one typographical error in this book, but we don’t know where it is. Can you find it?” Once I found the mistake, I kept reading this scholarly and scientific presentation of Krishna consciousness. It was great medicine for my deep-rooted cynicism and atheism.

“Is there anything else to read?” I asked.

“Yes, you must read the *Bhagavad-gita*,” a devotee told me. “But we’re out of copies at the moment. We’re not going to print any more, because the edition we had was abridged, and the unabridged edition will soon be printed.”

Seeing my desire to read the *Bhagavad-gita*, Svarupa dasa, one *brahmacari*, gave me his treasured copy. He had bound the paperback book and pasted Deity photos inside the covers. I was deeply touched by his generosity.

I delved into the *Bhagavad-gita* As It Is. Its message was so wonderful and apropos for me at that time in my life. It answered questions that I had always wondered about, and even questions that hadn’t quite formed in my mind. I read the *Bhagavad-gita* five times before I asked if there was another book to read. Each time I reread the book, it seemed entirely new. I began to understand the description of *transcendental literature* as ever fresh.

Most of the sixty or so devotees in Boston personally knew Srila Prabhupada, but I was so cynical I doubted that he really existed. “The *Bhagavad-gita* As It Is is so transcendental,” I thought, “how could the author even be on this planet?” Refusing to be convinced until I saw him myself, I began to desire to meet Srila Prabhupada. He was in India, but he wrote the devotees letters, which were shared with everyone, saying he was coming to America soon.

I learned about the four regulative principles: no meat-eating, no illicit sex, no intoxication, no gambling. “Wow! I can’t possibly follow these!” I thought. I still had a roach clip for smoking marijuana joints, and I was just waiting for the opportunity to use it. But within a short time I thought, “I’ve been following these principles, and if I continue associating with the devotees, I can follow them.”

Many devotees encouraged to me to stay at the Boston temple, but I still had plans to meet Stan in California. “Relationships are temporary,” I was told. “Either he’ll leave you, or you’ll leave him; he’ll die, or you’ll die. One way or another, the relationship will end. But your relationship with Krishna is eternal. You can give your heart and soul to Krishna, and you’ll never get hurt. Krishna can be your eternal husband. Depend on Krishna, and you’ll be happy.”

“This is reasonable, but am I ready to surrender?” I asked myself. I understood that if I stayed in the temple, I might never see Stan again. But the possibility of a relationship with Krishna as my eternal husband was deeply comforting.

Passing me on the temple stairs one morning, Hridayananda dasa brahmacari, the temple commander, casually inquired, “How are you doing?”

I poured out my heart: “I like chanting Hare Krishna, and I’d like to stay, but I’m just waiting for my ride to California. I’ll probably go to a temple there.”

“You really should stay here and become fixed in devotional service,” he advised. “Then you can travel anywhere and bring your Krishna consciousness with you. We’ve already wandered throughout this universe, lifetime after lifetime, in material bodies. Now we have a chance to go back home, back to Godhead. Experiment! Try Krishna consciousness for just this one lifetime and see for yourself if the process works. You’ve got nothing to lose and everything to gain.”

He convinced me. I was already getting feedback that the process worked. Srila Prabhupada taught, “Chant and be happy,” and I was happy chanting sixteen rounds. I decided: “I’ll make a week-by-week experiment. If I get positive feedback each week, I’ll stay another week.”

Tracking me down through Bob, Peter called to say, “I’m ready to go back to Ohio and on to California.”

I said, “I’m not going. I’m staying here at the temple.” I told him I would pick up the things I’d left at his friends’ dormitory, but I never did. Everything I needed was provided at the temple—sleeping bag, clothes, winter coat, boots and so on.

Soon I was allowed to dress in saffron saris like the other *brahmacarinis* (single women). I was told it pleased Srila Prabhupada to see the ISKCON girls dressed in spiritual clothing, so I liked doing that. The girls had a policy of sharing clothes, but I didn’t like that, because, being very short, I had a problem: the saris were so wide that I had too much cloth to tuck in. So I claimed two saris for myself and cut off several inches, thus making sure that no one else would wear them.

Bob visited me whenever he was in the neighborhood. I thanked him for tricking me into going to the temple. Someone always chaperoned me when he visited, so I got the impression the devotees expected Bob to kidnap me.

After Satsvarupa prabhu returned from Dallas, Jadurani asked me several times, “How old are you?” I had the feeling they thought I was only thirteen or something, and didn’t believe me that I was twenty-four.

I became happier daily, eventually fully emerging from my withdrawn state. Reading Srila Prabhupada’s books continually, I developed trust that he was not cheating me by giving me some man-made philosophy. He was an authorized guru, coming in a long line of disciplic succession. I developed the desire to dedicate my life to his mission. To please Srila Prabhupada and to avoid wasting precious time, I began literally running from one service to another.

I was surprised to learn that the worst month of my life—September, 1965—was the very month Srila Prabhupada arrived in Boston. He came out of his compassion to save us, and he definitely saved me. If it weren’t for Srila Prabhupada, I would have either committed suicide or ended up in a mental hospital.

When the devotees found out I could type, I began typing letters for Satsvarupa prabhu and re-typing the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* manuscripts.

After one week at the temple I had phoned my mother and said, “I’ve joined the Hare Krishna movement!”

“You’ll be doing something different next week,” she predicted.

But she was wrong. I never left the movement after the day I joined.

## Part Two: Śrīla Prabhupāda in America

## Chapter 4 - Initiations from Srila Prabhupada

## Settling Down to Temple Life

“Srila Prabhupada is coming!” Talk about nectar! Hearing these four words proved the ultimate high, and I kept hearing them the first few months I lived in the Boston temple. Anticipating Srila Prabhupada’s arrival gave us hope and enabled us to do extra hours of devotional service we might not have otherwise wanted to do. Actually, every devotee except me wanted Srila Prabhupada to come to Boston soon. I hoped Srila Prabhupada wouldn’t come until I’d been in the movement six months, so I could be initiated by him in person.

During my first few months in ISKCON, I attended the morning and evening temple programs and during the day typed for ISKCON Press. Every day I also went out for an hour of *harinam sankirtan*. We chanted Hare Krishna and danced in the streets and collected donations for *Back to Godhead* magazines. The days of freebies were soon over—we had to cover our printing costs. On Sundays I always helped cut veggies or make *samosas*. I loved the action-filled kitchen. Sometimes pungent aromas of the *chaunces*—made by frying spices in *ghee* (clarified butter)—forced me to run out of the kitchen sneezing, with tears in my eyes.

Extra classes on *The Nectar of Devotion* and *Sri Isopanisad* for new devotees helped me solidify my Krishna consciousness. *The Nectar of Devotion* was ISKCON’s devotional law book, containing the do’s and do-not’s, and lots of sweet pastimes of Lord Krishna, which made it clear what our potential spiritual relationships with Him are. I liked chanting in unison with other neophytes the nineteen *slokas*, or verses, of *Sri Isopanisad*, and memorizing them. I also learned by rote the Sanskrit and Bengali prayers we sang daily, by carrying around slips of paper with the words and learning to pronounce them. Most important, I never missed a day of chanting sixteen rounds on my *japa* beads: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. Living in the temple, I felt sheltered from the material world’s onslaughts.

One of my favorite activities was taking a break from our daily routine, jumping in the van, and bringing books, posters, *prasadam* and musical instruments to classrooms in schools and universities. Various teachers invited us, and the students always paid attention and often responded to our enticements to chant with us.

Satsvarupa prabhu, one of the first devotees to join the movement in 1966, taught a weekly evening seminar at Boston University in the Adult Education department, and many times I, and sometimes another devotee, accompanied him. His seminar differed from the classroom visits. Ten or eleven students sat around a conference table and listened to his discourse and then asked questions. I could often relate better to their questions than to his answers because my knowledge of Krishna consciousness wasn’t much beyond rudimentary. Once, while traveling back, I said, “I felt your answer to the girl’s question was way over her head,” because I had not understood his answer myself.

“You could have answered her question,” he said humbly. “Next time that happens, you answer.”

I was astounded by his humility. I felt unqualified to answer any question, what to speak of answering one in the presence of such a senior devotee.

Another evening Satsvarupa prabhu and I emerged from the seminar into a blizzard that had covered Boston with more than a foot of snow. It stopped the trolley cars, our transportation home, and knowing that our sole temple vehicle was unavailable, we had to hitchhike back. Few cars were out in the snow, but one old car stopped and offered us a ride. We climbed into the back seat and found ourselves among friendly hippies willing to share their marijuana. We refused of course. After they dropped us off, I thought, “I do identify more with the devotees. I’ve come a long way.”

One Sunday in March, Ananga Manjari got married to Hridayananda prabhu. I loved seeing the Vedic wedding, which included a *yajna*, or fire sacrifice. A small fire was lit in a sand-filled wok in the sacrificial arena. While chanting Sanskrit mantras, the priest poured ladles of ghee over the fire, and the couple and the audience tossed into the fire small handfuls of raw rice and sesame seeds. With each addition of *ghee*, grains and seeds, the flames rose higher. At the end, the couple carefully placed two bananas in the fire, and the knot was literally tied—with the corners of her sari and his *dhoti* (the wrapped, outer cloth worn by men on the lower part of the body), and it stayed tied the rest of the day. We celebrated the happy occasion with a big feast. Not long afterward, the newlyweds moved away to open new temples. I looked forward to seeing them again.

## Learning to Typeset Srila Prabhupada’s Books

Palika dasi, the wife of Bhavananda dasa, the president of the Brooklyn temple, was in Boston typesetting Srila Prabhupada’s books. “Do you want to learn composing and move to New York with ISKCON Press?” she asked me.

“Yes,” I said, because I wanted to be more involved. Publishing was in my blood. Besides, I’d heard about the delicious *halava*, a buttery cereal made from farina, that Bhavananda prabhu made every morning in New York.

Palika oriented me to the IBM composing machine: “Before you start your service, offer your obeisances to Sri Composer,” she said, using a Sanskrit honorific. “Paraphernalia used in Krishna’s service becomes spiritualized and should be respected.”

“The composer justifies lines,” Palika taught. “This means it straightens the right margin. You start by typing a rough draft on plain paper to measure the length of the lines. The gauge gives you a color/number code, which you type at the end of a line, for example ‘b5’ for blue 5 or ‘y3’ for yellow 3. When the page is complete, put glossy paper in the composer and retype the final page. Set the little dial to the line’s code. If you typed accurately both times, the lines will be justified.”

She also explained that for the different type faces—regular and italic—we had to switch the fonts manually, by alternating the small metal spheres peculiar to IBM selectric machines.

The most difficult part was putting in diacritic marks—the dots, dashes and other marks above and below certain letters. The diacritics were also on two separate fonts, because Sanskrit words were in italics, but Sanskrit names were in regular. It was especially tricky doing the synonyms, which alternated between Sanskrit words and their English equivalents.

“Proofread your work and type corrected lines at the bottom of the page for the layout men to strip in,” Palika said. “But if you have more than nine mistakes per page, layout will reject it; so retype the entire page.”

To avoid retyping whole pages, I and another composer named Kathy (who became Nara-devi dasi) often scratched out our smaller mistakes on the glossy paper with a razor blade and retyped over them. One blade was accidentally dropped inside a machine, and a serviceman had to come to retrieve it. We were warned never to do that again, so we avoided using razor blades for some time, but eventually we used them again, and the cycle was repeated.

I loved this challenging service. Not only was I helping to produce books for distribution but also I got to read Srila Prabhupada’s books before practically everyone else. We were composing the unabridged edition of *Bhagavad-gita* As It Is, which the Macmillian company had agreed to publish, and the monthly editions of Back to Godhead, which we composed six months in advance because they were printed in *Japa*n.

My first contact with Srila Prabhupada was in connection to my composing. Either Palika or Radhaballabha dasa, the production manager, wrote to him: “Emily prabhu is now composing.” According to Palika, Srila Prabhupada wrote back, “Tell the composers to slow down.” Too many typing mistakes were slipping by the proofreaders and getting printed. Srila Prabhupada was particular about the appearance of his books. He wanted them to be first-class, so that scholars and readers would accept them as highly authoritative.

## Typesetting in New York

In April, 1971, the composing department moved to New York, ahead of the other ISKCON Press departments in Boston. Two attached brownstones in a residential area of Brooklyn served as the temple, which was larger and more populated than the Boston temple.

I was brought to the large *brahmacarini* ashram and given a room to share with others. In this temple, to my relief, the devotees didn’t share clothing. I put my few possessions on my shelf. Then I heard guitar music downstairs. “Wow! In Boston the devotees said guitars are *maya*,” I thought. “This temple is less conservative.” I became doubly convinced when I learned that the music came from Bhavananda prabhu’s office. New York was a happening place!

As in Boston, the devotees stood anywhere they liked in the temple room during *aratis* and *japa—*front or back. Being short, I liked to stand in the front, near the Deities. On the left side of the altar was a medium-size oil painting of Panca-tattva—Lord Caitanya and His four principal associates. A large Lord Jagannatha and His brother and sister stood on the right side, and in the middle of the altar were small brass Deities of Sri Sri Radha-Krishna, with large lotuslike eyes and curvy hips, beautifully dressed by Rukmini dasi.

New York *kirtans* always sounded lively, especially when Bharadraja dasa led, although a few ladies were also good at leading. Once, even I led a *kirtan*, as we walked through the streets of Brooklyn, returning from *sankirtan*.

Sundays were my favorite days. We’d start with a maha-cleanup early in the morning. During *japa*, several men disassembled Srila Prabhupada’s *vyasasana*, a series of graduated platforms, setting them in the hallway, and Bhavananda prabhu mopped the floor. The *pujaris* similarly stripped down the altar, behind a closed curtain, after placing the Deities on tables in the adjoining *pujari* room, and they thoroughly cleaned the altar room.

After breakfast on Sundays, a large group of devotees took the subway into Manhattan, to their favorite spot in Central Park, for *sankirtan*. After they drew a large crowd, a spokesman briefly introduced the people to Krishna consciousness and invited everyone to the temple for the feast. People followed us back, swelling the regular number of guests at the *arati* and lecture. Sitting with guests to eat the feast was always fun, especially when they welcomed hearing about Krishna consciousness.

After the feasts, we swung into another, not-quite-as-thorough maha-cleanup of the temple, and engaged any guests willing to help. When the guests had gone home, Bhavananda began our weekly *istagosthis* (meetings) by singing a wild rendition of the Nrsimha-deva prayers—which was immortalized on tape.

Awaiting the completion of the renovation of the new press offices, Palika set up the IBM composers in a small dungeon beyond the steamy laundry room in the sub-basement. Yuck! Another devotee, Debby, joined the department—she became Mamata dasi—and we ate a lot of *halava* (stashed at breakfast) to keep up our spirits in that dank, dark room.

Once the new ISKCON Press quarters were ready, we moved the composers to Tiffany Place, four blocks away, and the other departments from Boston arrived soon after. The name Tiffany Place was deceptive; the street was strewn with broken glass, not diamonds.

It’s a good thing I understood that my relationship with Krishna surpassed the importance of material relationships because Stan visited me one day in April with a girlfriend. They stayed overnight but left quickly the next morning, and I never saw them again.

I donated to the temple my shares of stock that my paternal grandfather gave me as an investment. “I don’t need them,” I reasoned, “because the temple meets all my needs.” I was satisfied to receive a set of hardbound Krishna books in return. A broker informed my grandfather of the stock transaction. He became upset, and so did my father. After that, our relationships were strained, but my brother Lincoln and his wife and my young nephew occasionally visited me.

I loved composing, and I was committed to it, but I broke Palika’s rule not to accept any other service. It was tantamount to betrayal when she caught me in the composing room embroidering a handkerchief for the Deities. Not accepting my excuse—I had promised to have it done by a deadline—she traded me with a girl from Philadelphia.

“Since you are attracted to Deity worship,” Palika reasoned, “you can be the head *pujari* in Philadelphia.” There was no court of appeal.

## The Philadelphia Temple

Bill prabhu (who became Rabindra Swarupa dasa) drove me to the Philadelphia temple in his Volkswagen bug. In the back seat rode new Deities, which Jatila dasi had carved, of Lord Jagannatha, Balarama and Subhadra. When we arrived at a large house in a residential area in Germantown, a suburb of Philadelphia, the devotees carried the Deities inside. Everyone offered obeisances, but the Deities were promptly placed in a closet. The fifteen local devotees weren’t ready to install them.

However, I immediately took up my responsibilities as the *pujari*. I was told to offer *arati* to a picture of the Panca-tattva. I knew only the basics: how to ring the bell with my left hand while offering the articles with my right hand in graceful circular motions. And I knew the order of offering the articles: blowing the conchshell, then offering incense, a *ghee* lamp, water in another conchshell, a handkerchief, a flower, the *camara* fan, the peacock fan, and finally, blowing the conch again. I could also chant the *“jaya om”* prayers that *pujaris* recited aloud at the end.

What I didn’t know was the numerical system of offering the items. In the Deity kitchen Patita Uddharana dasa quickly trained me: so many circles to the lotus feet of the Deity, so many to the waist, and so on, up to the head. I felt exhilarated offering my first *arati*, and I looked forward to doing several a day.

Things changed the next day, however, when a letter arrived from Srila Prabhupada. He initiated three devotees by mail. Even without a fire sacrifice to formally accept these disciples, we immediately considered them initiated.

“Now that we have a total of five initiated devotees,” said Nayanabhirama dasa, the temple president, “all the *aratis* should be offered by them.”

That left me out of the picture, and I was crushed. I couldn’t offer any more *aratis*. Instead, I set up the *arati* paraphernalia for the *pujari* and cleaned up afterward.

## The Washington and Ocean City Temples

Six weeks later I took part in a *maha-sankirtan* day in Ocean City, Maryland. This meant that the devotees from several temples (Philadelphia, Washington, D.C. and Baltimore) came together to do *sankirtan*. We gathered at a small house just off the boardwalk rented by Nanda Kisora dasa and Jahnava dasi, his wife, for summer preaching at this popular beach resort. “Chanting parties” walked up and down the beach, singing, playing instruments, distributing Back to Godhead and handing out homemade caramel-coated popcorn and other sticky sweets that we spooned into outstretched palms.

Regrouping at the house, I met another new devotee, who was the *pujari* in Washington, D.C. She got to perform *aratis* and even cook. I wanted to go to Washington, and she wanted to go to Philadelphia, so our temple presidents agreed to let us switch places.

In the Q Street temple, near Dupont Circle in Washington, I was happily offering all the *aratis*. I became proficient by practice. I also learned the basics of cooking for Krishna and the devotees. The daily fare included rice, *dahl*, *subjees* (vegetables), and *chapattis*. Helping to cook the Sunday feast, I learned how to make specialties like sweet rice, *samosas*, *pakoras*, *halava*, *chutney*, *puris* and other preparations.

We worshiped a picture of Panca-tattva on the altar, but in the temple room were small Jagannatha Deities kept in a box and covered by a curtain, which curious guests sometimes opened to peek inside.

During the summer I went back to Ocean City with a few Washington devotees to spend a whole month preaching to the crowds at the beach. We distributed lots of *prasadam* daily and held programs nightly. Passersby on the warm evenings became attracted by the sound of our chanting, the aroma of incense drifting outside and the exotic altar visible inside. We invited them in to chant and to hear our *Bhagavad-gita* classes.
