The Sunday feasts often drew a crowd. Nanda Kisora gave us hardly any *ghee* to cook with during the week, saving it for Sunday, when he cooked huge batches of *lugloos*—sweetballs made with crisp *bundis* (deep-fried, chick-pea-flour pellets) and dried fruits and nuts, all held together with a syrup of dates cooked in sugar. Nanda Kisora, after restraining us from eating the leftover *lugloos*, packaged them in small plastic bags, stapled on labels and sold them in some local shops for twenty-five cents each.

Back in Washington, I was cleaning the *pujari* closet one day and found medium-size, brass Deities of Radha-Krishna standing on the floor, covered by a cloth. I offered my obeisances and admired Their beauty. After cleaning around Their lotus feet, I searched out Damodara dasa, the temple president, and asked, “When are you going to install these Deities?”

“It will be years before we can worship Them,” he answered. “There aren’t enough *brahmanas* here to worship even our small Jagannatha Deities.”

About a dozen devotees lived in Washington, and only a few had received their first initiation. Only *brahmanas*—those with second initiation—could worship Deities other than the picture of Panca-tattva.

Damodara prabhu brought the Deities to New York, where the ISKCON Press artists painted Their feet, hands and faces. There the Deities sat in the *pujari* room, waiting to be picked up by Damodara.

## Going to New York to See Srila Prabhupada

Krishna fulfilled my desire. By the time Srila Prabhupada arrived in New York, in July, 1971, I was eligible for first initiation. I had been chanting sixteen rounds and following the four regulative principles for six months. I simply required the recommendation of my temple president.

“Why do you want to get initiated?” Damodara prabhu asked.

“Because I’m a servant of Krishna, and I need a guru.”

“Who is Krishna?”

“Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead.”

“O.K. I’ll recommend you to Srila Prabhupada.”

Whew! I passed the test.

When Srila Prabhupada arrived in New York, devotees from all over the East Coast joyfully greeted him, but not the Washington devotees. Damodara prabhu’s thinking was that our business wasn’t to gawk at the spiritual master; but we were meant to serve him and to go out on *sankirtan*. So we were going to New York only long enough to get initiated.

Mrganetri dasi, Damodara’s wife, stayed back because she had just had a baby, whom Srila Prabhupada named Gulab dasi. Before we left, Mrganetri asked me, “What do you think Srila Prabhupada will name you?”

I jokingly said, “Srila Prabhupada is probably going to name me *Maya*, because I’m always in *maya*.”

I didn’t really think I was in *maya*; I thought I was fixed up. I was just being falsely humble and repeating a standard joke among the twenty-five uninitiated *brahmacarinis* back in the New York temple.

On the way to New York we stopped at the Baltimore temple to get new *japa* beads for our initiation ceremony. The devotees there had bought a big box of dark walnut beads, and those of us getting initiated each counted out one hundred and eight beads. We already had our end bead—the Krishna bead—because a couple of the men had carved enough for all six of us. In the car we memorized the ten offenses to be avoided while chanting the holy names, because we heard that Srila Prabhupada expected us to recite them.

## The First Time I Saw Srila Prabhupada

Arriving at the Brooklyn temple, I experienced a new atmosphere. Just being in the same building as Srila Prabhupada was ecstatic. Someone informed us, “Be very quiet! Otherwise, you’ll disturb Srila Prabhupada.” His quarters were directly below the stairs to the *brahmacarini* ashram, where we visiting ladies were staying. To minimize noise, that stairway was blocked off, forcing us to take a detour up through the *brahmacari* ashram and down another staircase into our ashram. Although I tried not to look, I observed a substandard level of cleanliness in the men’s ashram. Hare Krishna!

I quickly found out the schedule. Each evening Srila Prabhupada would be giving the *Bhagavad-gita* class, because in the mornings he was holding initiations instead of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* classes. As Srila Prabhupada could chant on ten sets of new initiates’ beads in one hour, the number receiving first initiation each day was limited to ten.

Before the first evening class I sat with the other Washington devotees in the empty *prasadam* room, stringing my beads, making a knot between each one. Our initiation was the next morning.

Then we went to the temple room for class. Bhavananda prabhu had painted the temple room bright yellow and had put several other bright colors on the trim along the ceiling. The room was packed to the brim and beyond! *Kirtan* was going full blast, and everyone was sweltering. The two window fans were ineffective in cooling the room on this hot July evening.

Sitting in the temple room, anticipating Srila Prabhupada’s entrance, I wondered cynically, “Is Srila Prabhupada really going to walk through that door?” By serving Srila Prabhupada for six months I’d become purified, but some of my deep-rooted skepticism remained. As I sat, I watched Maha Dave (who became Kirtiraja dasa) make his way through the crowd and ask some devotees to leave, to make room for others in the hall who had never seen Srila Prabhupada give class. I was shocked when he asked me to leave, as though I had already seen Srila Prabhupada. Maybe he didn’t know I had moved out of the New York temple months earlier. I sat there defiantly, thinking, “No way I’m going to budge!”

Finally, Srila Prabhupada walked through the door, and in that first glimpse I had of him I felt I was seeing an old friend I hadn’t seen for a long, long time. All my doubts about whether the author of the *Bhagavad-gita* could really be on this planet vanished. I offered my obeisances to Lord Krishna’s pure devotee and felt happy to see him.

## First Initiation

The next morning, on July 23rd, Srila Prabhupada accepted me as his disciple at a fire sacrifice. The ladies being initiated were loaned new saris—colorful polyester saris, with fake metallic designs, complements of the New York temple. Well, they were almost new. The ladies initiated the previous day had also worn them, and the following day they’d be worn by the next group, and so on throughout the week.

Upon entering the temple room, Prabhupada sat on his new *vyasasana*, a Bhavananda creation that consisted of a red-and-white op-art, cloth-covered base, topped by two smaller platforms and pillows covered in checks and stripes of various pastel colors. Srila Prabhupada liked the *vyasasana*, calling it “daring.”

The ten initiates and those receiving second initiation that day sat in the sacrificial arena. While we chanted *japa* for an hour, we watched Srila Prabhupada chant on our ten new strings of beads. Holding the beads in both hands, he rolled them between the thumb and middle finger of his right hand as he chanted a round on each string.

Our new *japa* beads were hanging on the microphone stand, in the same order that we sat in the arena. Therefore I knew exactly when Srila Prabhupada was chanting on my beads. Once he chanted on our walnut beads, they were as good as *tulasi*, Lord Krishna’s dear devotee in the form of a plant, whose sacred wood is used for *japa* beads in India.

Seven men were first go get initiated. Srila Prabhupada didn’t ask them to say the ten offences, but he asked the first few devotees to recite the regulative principles. After that he just asked others, “Do you know the four regulative principles?”

Srila Prabhupada named the four men from Washington Pancaratna, Saptaratha, Astaratha and Dasaratha, giving short definitions, like “*panca* means five, *ratna* means jewels” or “one who has eight chariots.” Then a pretty twelve-year-old, a blue-eyed blonde from the Brooklyn temple, whose mother had received the name Kansa dasi the previous day, came before Srila Prabhupada to be initiated. Srila Prabhupada became jovial, smiled at her and asked, “How many rounds do you chant?”

She answered, “Sixteen.”

“Your name is Sasi-mukhi. *Sasi* means moon, and *mukhi* means face. Fortunate name,” Srila Prabhupada said.

I was next, coming before Saptaratha’s wife, who received the name Hemalata dasi.

After offering full obeisances to Srila Prabhupada, I sat at his lotus feet to receive my beads and spiritual name. I felt that Srila Prabhupada looked right through my body, penetrating layers and layers of false ego. He saw the real me—spirit soul. It was one of the most exhilarating moments of my life.

Aravinda dasa, Srila Prabhupada’s servant, read my name from the list: “Mahamaya.” I was stunned. Srila Prabhupada repeated it into the microphone for everyone to hear. There was a shocked silence in the packed temple. Srila Prabhupada then started to laugh, and everyone naturally followed suit, including me. We all cracked up!

“Mahamaya, Mahamaya, the illusory energy,” Srila Prabhupada said. “So illusory energy is not bad. Just like there are phases of the moon, the illusory energy is another phase of Radharani. Those who do not want to serve Krishna, for them the illusory energy is there. Do you know the rules?”

I said, “Yes.”

Srila Prabhupada handed me my beads. I again offered *dandavats* and returned to my place in the sacrificial arena.

It seemed he talked for such a long time about my name—it felt like ten minutes—but he said only four sentences. It seemed that long because of Srila Prabhupada’s intensity—his seeing through my bodily identification to the soul. He didn’t talk about Maya-devi, the personality, but instead talked about *mahamaya*, Krishna’s energy. Well, I asked for this name! And it’s perfect, because Srila Prabhupada named me. Besides, “*dasi*” is the most important part of my name—”servant” of Lord Krishna’s energy.

My initiation was a most serious commitment. Srila Prabhupada accepted me as his disciple, and I, in turn, accepted his guidance as my spiritual master, swearing to strictly follow vows my entire life.

The Washington devotees drove back early the next morning. Therefore I didn’t see any other initiations, but they continued.

## Srila Prabhupada’s Airport Departure

A few days later, right after *mangal-arati*, Rupanuga prabhu called from New York. He told Damodara prabhu: “You devotees missed Srila Prabhupada’s arrival, and you hardly stayed while he was here. I insist that you come back to New York for his departure. It’s today at the airport.”

Damodara told us, “Jump in the van right now! We’re all going to New York!”

I was just about to make a garland for Srila Prabhupada’s picture. I had a few roses and some big carnations. I thought, “These flowers are for Srila Prabhupada, so I’ll give them to him personally.” I quickly made a little bouquet by wrapping something around the stems.

We drove for five hours, straight to the airport. When we arrived, Srila Prabhupada was already there, sitting in a row of attached seats. Hundreds of devotees were gathered around him on the floor of the terminal lounge. Bharadraja prabhu was playing *mrdanga* and leading a rousing *kirtan*.

I felt too scared to hand the bouquet to Srila Prabhupada, and I tried to get other ladies to give it to him. I asked Kalindi prabhu, Rupanuga’s wife. She refused, saying, “You do it.” I also asked Rukmini prabhu, Bharadraja’s wife, but she also said, “No, no, no, you do it.”

So I had to do it myself. I frantically formulated words to say when I handed the bouquet to Srila Prabhupada. But first I had to figure out how to get near him. The only way was from behind the line of attached seats. Once there, I reached out my arm from behind Srila Prabhupada and handed him the bouquet, blurting out inarticulately, “This is from the devotees in Washington Vaikuntha.”

As Srila Prabhupada took the flowers, he looked back at me and said something, but I was too shy and bewildered to listen. I was totally focused on hitting the floor to offer obeisances and then getting back into the crowd as fast as I could!

If only I’d had the presence of mind to speak with Srila Prabhupada, or at least to hear and understand what he said to me. Maybe he said, “Thank you,” and I could have answered politely, “You’re welcome.” Maybe Srila Prabhupada didn’t even understand what I had mumbled. Anyway, who ever heard of “Washington Vaikuntha”? “Vaikuntha” means the spiritual sky, but “Washington Vaikuntha”? I had just made it up!

This small, clumsy incident was a typical example of a new disciple attempting to please her spiritual master but being too nervous to follow through with the exchange in a satisfying way. I learned that serving the spiritual master in person is quite different than serving his picture.

When Srila Prabhupada’s flight departure was announced, he walked to the gate, still holding the bouquet. All the devotees followed him, dancing wildly. The *kirtan* roared. Just before departing, Srila Prabhupada handed Bharadraja the bouquet as a gift, to thank him for his wonderful *kirtan*. Then our beloved guru entered the gate to board the plane.

## Janmastami and Vyasa-puja, 1971

Located in the hills of West Virginia, near Moundsville, the New Vrindaban farm community (named after Lord Krishna’s sacred hometown in India) was started by Kirtanananda Swami and Hayagriva dasa. Srila Prabhupada enjoyed six peaceful weeks there in 1969. Every year, New Vrindaban hosted the East Coast devotees during Janmastami, Lord Krishna’s appearance day, and on the following day, Srila Prabhupada’s birthday. The guru’s appearance day is called Vyasa-puja day because he represents Vyasa, the compiler of the Vedic literature.

I traveled to New Vrindaban with the Washington devotees. This year, large marble Deities, Sri Sri Radha–Vrindaban-candra, would be installed. The temple was at the main farm, called Vrindaban, two miles down a dirt road. It was difficult for cars to drive there, but not impossible.

So much service was required to make the festival a success that all the devotees had to pitch in and help the overworked New Vrindaban devotees—like Yamini dasi, who, ignoring her rather large belly, tirelessly organized the festival Deity worship in the large open-air barn. Eagerly volunteering, I got to do many services—some I wasn’t really qualified for. For example, during the installation of the Deities, four *brahmanas* were required to sit in the corners of the arena and read Srila Prabhupada’s books aloud. Although just first-initiated, I was thrilled to be picked to do this.

Back in Washington after the celebrations Damodara prabhu gathered everyone together to read a letter that had arrived from Srila Prabhupada in response to our collective Vyasa-puja letter, sent before the July initiations:

London  
August 15, 1971

My dear Damodara, Patita Pavana, Michael, Tom, Emily, Tosan, Roset, Mrganetri-devi dasi, Gulab, Patrick, etc.

Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you very much for your kind feelings of appreciation of my humble service unto you. You are all helping me in pushing forward this mission of Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, coming down by disciplic succession to my Guru Maharaj. So whatever you have spoken, it is simply due to them, on behalf of my Guru Maharaj, His Divine Grace Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Goswami Maharaj Prabhupada.

Hoping this meets you all in the best of health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

ACBS/adb

Not accepting our praise for himself, Srila Prabhupada instead gave credit to his guru and the disciplic succession. This humility endeared Srila Prabhupada to me. He took many risks for our sake—crossing the ocean at seventy, when others would have retired; leaving behind his beloved Vrindaban-*dhama*, just to save us. And when we glorified and thanked him, he saw himself as our servants—“my humble service unto you”—and the servant of his guru. Srila Prabhupada was the perfect disciple as well as the perfect guru!

## Guru-daksina Letter

A newly initiated disciple gives a gift, or *guru-daksina*, in reciprocation for the guru’s giving him or her *diksa*, initiation. While in New York I gave Srila Prabhupada two gifts-a blue-satin eyeglass case, trimmed with white lace, and an opulent Deity handkerchief for the worship of his Radha-Krishna Deities, both handcrafted by me. Monetary *guru-daksina* was also required. It took me a few months to collect twenty dollars because I rarely went on *sankirtan*. Damodara instructed me to mail the *laksmi* (money) to Karandhara dasa, the ISKCON treasurer in Los Angeles.

In early September, 1971, I wrote a letter to Srila Prabhupada to inform him I’d sent *guru-daksina*. I followed the recommended etiquette for writing letters to the guru: three-quarters for glorification and one-quarter the point of writing. Beyond that, I remember only thanking him for pulling me out of the gutter of material life.

To save the price of one stamp, Damodara said he’d mail my letter along with one of his, which wasn’t yet written. After waiting for weeks and weeks, finally in October he was ready to send his letter, so I dated my letter at the end. It was a strange place to put the date, but that’s what I did.

## Hearing About the Road Show

For months I was lonely, being the only *brahmacarini* in Washington, but in autumn three girls joined. Later they became Vilasi, Manakumari and Bhagyavati dasis. Still, once I heard about a phone call from Kirtanananda Swami to Damodara prabhu, I didn’t want to stay in Washington.

Kirtanananda Swami had conceived of a Transcendental Road Show. Needing Deities, and being on the lookout for some, he spied Washington’s Radha-Krishna Deities still standing in the New York *pujari* room. Kirtanananda Swami called Damodara on the phone: “May I borrow your Deities for about two years?”

“Sure,” Damodara prabhu responded. “We won’t be installing them for at least that long.”

The Road Show left New Vrindaban in a few buses and went to Atlanta. After an installation ceremony for the Deities, the Road Show continued south, to Miami, I was told. Devotees from all over were congregating in Florida, and I wanted to join them. It sounded like fun.

With Damodara’s permission to join, I asked my father for one hundred dollars, for the airfare to Miami. Dad refused, so I had to collect the *laksmi* myself. With permission to keep a percentage of my collection, I was so highly motivated that in one single day I distributed enough pamphlets (“On Chanting Hare Krishna”), at ten cents each, that I could afford a ticket to Miami-a bus ticket!

## The Miami Temple

Surviving the long bus ride of about fifteen hundred miles, I arrived at the Miami temple, a house on Mary Street, only to learn that I was misinformed. The Transcendental Road Show was in Tampa, three hundred miles northwest. Discovering that Hladini dasi, whom I knew from New York, was the Miami *pujari*, I enjoyed her association so much that I temporarily forgot about joining the Road Show. Sridhama dasa, the temple president, happily let me stay there to assist Hladini.

The Mary Street house contained a kitchen, *prasadam* room, *brahmacari* ashram and office. The floors were stained red and the house reeked of sweet smells as a result of devotees packing Spiritual Sky incense there-a temple business. The *brahmacarinis*—there were only a few—lived in a favorable girl’s apartment, a short walk away, until the day we discovered her boy friend stayed there sometimes, and we moved somewhere else. The temple room was in a separate building, an old church, a block away. We had to carry Krishna’s plate through the streets to make the offerings. I don’t remember the altar, but I do remember the love of Hladini’s life, her Lord Jagannatha Deities.

## A Letter from Srila Prabhupada

While in Miami I received two letters. One was from my father, who relented and sent me one hundred dollars. I donated the money to the Miami temple. The other was from my spiritual father in response to my *guru-daksina* letter. The Indian aerogram read:

Tridandi Goswami  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Date: November 23, 1971  
Camp: 7-D Kamala Nagar, Delhi 7

My dear Mahamaya dasi,

Please accept my blessings. I have received your letter undated and have noted the contents carefully. I have very pleased by your attitude to surrender yourself completely by engaging cent per cent in Krishna’s service. Somehow or other always to be thinking about Krishna, that is the goal of Krishna consciousness process. So you please help me to spread this information all over this earth planet, especially by helping to distribute my books and literatures as many as possible. Then you will get the credit and know it for certain that very soon you will go back to Home, Back to Godhead.

I hope this finds you in good health and cheerful mood,

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

ACBS/sda

As was customary in ISKCON—all the devotees in any temple read any letters from Srila Prabhupada-the Miami devotees all read my letter. It was a wonderful, inspiring letter. I immediately noticed the phrase: “your letter undated”—obviously, he didn’t notice the date, wrongly placed. The next line really struck me: “I am very pleased by your attitude to surrender yourself completely by engaging cent per cent in Krishna’s service.” What had I written that Srila Prabhupada saw in me an attitude of complete surrender? That stunned me. He was seeing something I didn’t see.

Srila Prabhupada asked me to distribute the message of Krishna consciousness, “especially by helping to distribute my books and literatures as many as possible.” Although this instruction did not transform me into a book distributor, I did feel I should again compose and proofread Srila Prabhupada’s books for producing books is requisite to distributing them. Srila Prabhupada called ISKCON Press the big *mrdanga*, *Brhat Mrdanga*, because although one can play a *mrdanga* drum and those nearby can hear the small *kirtan*, the printing press produced books, which spread the transcendental message of Krishna consciousness—the big *kirtan*—all over the planet.

Srila Prabhupada assured me (or any follower) that by helping him distribute Krishna consciousness, “then you will get the credit and know if for certain that very soon you’ll go back Home, Back to Godhead.”

Who else but Srila Prabhupada taught that even the most sinful, like me, can go back to Godhead in this lifetime? Who except Srila Prabhupada so fully understood Lord Caitanya’s magnanimity in freely distributing the contents of the storehouse of love of God? Who but Srila Prabhupada was so determined to reach the conditioned souls outside the Vedic system and teach them that they could transcend an unimaginable quantity—countless lifetimes—of karma, simply by chanting Hare Krishna and performing devotional service? The Godbrothers of Srila Prabhupada and various saintly persons in India didn’t have his clear understanding or brave resolution.

## The Transcendental Road Show

After three weeks in Miami, I decided I really did want to join the Road Show. I phoned Tampa to request permission. Kirtanananda Swami wondered, “Can you get along with other women?”

Every temple had its problem devotees, but in general I was a sociable person, so I answered, “Yes,” especially since this was his condition for my acceptance.

I got a ride to Tampa and thus joined ISKCON’s first traveling *sankirtan* party. The Transcendental Road Show was one of the most ecstatic experiences of my life. We traveled around in the buses with beautiful Radha-Krishna Deities, preached at colleges and universities, distributed massive amounts of *prasadam*, held ecstatic *kirtans*, performed well-rehearsed dramas, heard philosophical lectures by *sannyasis*, enjoyed Krishna-conscious guitar music, and more. We had a tie-dyed parachute backdrop for our rock band. It was unique.

The Road Show’s thirty-five devotees more or less took over the Tampa temple, which boasted only four devotees, with Pusta Krishna dasa in charge. Everyone squashed into the tiny house. The Road Show’s old school buses, undergoing renovation, lined the street.

The Radha-Krishna Deities were lovingly cared for by Silavati dasi, an older lady—meaning, perhaps, forty-five—whom Srila Prabhupada trained in Deity worship in Los Angeles. He named her the “world *pujari*,” with the responsibility to train others. (Her two sons were elsewhere, but she said they had received both first and second initiation very young—before reaching their teens.)

The small temple didn’t have a proper altar with a curtain. Therefore Silavati prabhu bathed and dressed the Deities every morning in view of the devotees chanting *japa*. This proved too intense for most devotees, and they spilled out of the temple to chant elsewhere. I was fascinated. Silavati or sometimes Kirtanananda Swami rubbed the Deities with scented oils. Either she was spontaneous in her devotion or she was experimenting, but she bathed the Deities in a variety of ways. For instance, she poured water over Them while They stood in large stainless steel containers, or bathed Them by mantra or by wiping Them with a warm wet washcloth. Bathing complete, Silavati dressed the Deities beautifully, despite Their lack of many varieties of clothes. She sang throughout in her deep-throated, melodious voice.

Those uninvolved in the bus renovations gave classes at the University of South Florida or did *harinam sankirtan* and book distribution. We tried selling the Krishna book for the whopping price of seven dollars, and Narada Muni dasa distributed three of them in one day. We were amazed!

One day Labangalatika, Prsni and I were driven across town to distribute at a state fair. Once there, we went our separate ways on the fair grounds. After a few hours I met two men extraordinarily interested in the books and the Spiritual Sky incense, and once their money had changed hands, they said, “You’re under arrest!” They brought me to their temporary police station on the fairgrounds.

“We’ve got your two sisters down at headquarters,” they said. “You can’t sell things at the fair—it’s illegal.”

I protested: “I was asking for donations, not selling. You think you’re serving our country by stopping us, but we’re trying to spread love of God, which really benefits people.”

Preaching up a storm, I talked my way out of being taken to police headquarters and being booked, but the other ladies were already in jail.

Released, but too scared to call the temple, I walked back. I told Kirtanananda Swami what happened. Even with a lawyer’s aid, he couldn’t get the other ladies released until the next day.

We practiced our repertoire of dramas: *The Age of Kali*, The *Fish* Play and others. Puskara dasa painted large backdrops of Lord Caitanya and Lord Krishna. Jamadagni dasa and Mangalananda dasa wrote songs: “Svetadvipa,” “Oh Govinda,” and our theme song, “Lord Caitanya’s Moon is Rising”:

Lord Caitanya's moon is rising  
And it "ain't" at all surprising  
That we're singing in the street  
Telling everyone we meet  
We're going home—Back to Godhead!

Another favorite song:

Hey, you don't have to worry  
All your cares will soon all be gone  
Try to understand the problem  
There's no place for you to put your love

So take this simple message  
It will carry you so very far  
Back to Home, Rack to Godhead  
Chant these names and give yourself to God

Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna  
Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare  
Hare Rama, Hare Rama  
Rama Rama, Hare Hare

Many of our neighbors in Tampa were nice, even letting us use their ovens to cook our Sunday feasts, but we were unpopular with some of them. The unfriendly ones complained to the police, who asked us to leave. Since the buses weren’t ready, most of us went to Orlando, where we stayed in someone’s empty airplane hanger. We got a little sentimental on Christmas day and sang Hare Krishna to various holiday tunes.

Renting a house there, we established another temple. Going on *harinam sankirtan*, we roamed the residential areas and received boxes and boxes of donated oranges and grapefruits.

Our biggest Road Show engagement in Florida was at the Orlando Youth Center. Devotees from Gainesville and Tallahassee temples came to see our show, which included a performance of *The Age of Kali* so powerful that one young man in the audience ran out screaming.

## Rolling with the Road Show

After two months the buses were ready. One was a temple room, another a traveling kitchen, a third was the equipment bus. All were gaily painted. Our friends Bob and Norma owned a fourth bus. We were down to fourteen devotees because many devotees grew tired of waiting and left. The Deities of Radha-Krishna were tied to the altar, which, in turn, was strapped down over one of the rear wheels. Silavati and I traveled in the temple bus to make sure the Deities didn’t fall. Other devotees traveled in the buses or Kirtanananda Swami’s van. A three-man crew drove ahead to set up engagements for us.

We three ladies slept in the kitchen bus, behind a huge water tank. The space was barely big enough for us and accessible only through the rear door. I was getting along fine with the other ladies until these close quarters promoted tension. Silavati prabhu started giving me a hard time and favoring Prsni, whom she had brought to the Road Show from Los Angeles. I felt I couldn’t reveal to Maharaja that I wasn’t getting along with her, and she was senior to me by initiation and age, so I kept quiet and absorbed myself in kitchen service, play production, and book distribution. Once in a while Silavati was nice to me and gave me some sewing for the Deities.

While driving north, we stopped at the Gainesville temple. There we performed *The Fish Play* before an appreciative audience in an unused church behind the temple. A film crew shot us, and later some segments of our play were aired on local TV This comical yet pointed drama concerns Mr. Fish failing miserably to enjoy life on land after being lured out of the water by Maya-devi. A few times I played one of Maya’s agents, Fish’s wife, and Harikesa dasa played Mr. Fish. Norma was Maya-devi, and Mangalananda played some of Maya’s other agents—the boss, the professor and the waiter. A devotee named Lambodhara dasa was Mr. Euphoria, Maya’s ultimate agent, who totally bewilders poor Fish. Fortunately, in the end Fish

understands his frustration and learns that he must surrender to Lord Krishna, the “Big Fish,” to become happy again.

*The Fish Play* became our main theatrical production. Eventually Mangalananda played all Maya’s agents—even the wife—with only minor props and extra costume pieces. A well-rounded person, Mangalananda cooked well, too. He introduced deep-fried breadsticks. Breadsticks and nutritious *kittri* (rice and *dahl* stew) became our main meal—easy to cook while traveling.

We drove next to Florida’s capital, Tallahassee. We parked the buses near the temple and performed at a university, Florida State. By this time we had a reputation for stealing both kitchen equipment and devotees who caught our traveling fever. I helped in the kitchen and can verify we didn’t intentionally take anything. Cooking went on in both the temple and the bus, so things just got mixed up.

Driving through Alabama, we did stints in colleges and universities and once even in a hippie bar. People seeing devotees for the first time in their lives stared incredulously wherever we went.

## Visnujana Swami Joins the Road Show

Visnujana Swami, hearing the glories of the Road Show, came to check it out. He was based in Texas, running preaching centers in San Antonio and Austin. He loved our breadsticks and *kittri* and said we had the best *prasadam.* He immediately jumped into service. He taught us dramas, including one about Mrigari the hunter, performed with handheld tin masks that warbled when shaken. The Deities needed a better altar, and he built a new one.

Somewhere in the deep South we performed our full Road Show production in a university auditorium one evening. Silavati prabhu, sitting mid-stage in dim light, started the show by slowly and meditatively singing the “*Govindam*” prayers and *Sri Isopanisad* mantras, accompanying herself on a tamboura. Behind her was the rock band-guitars, drums, amplifiers. The tie-dyed parachute hung pinned to the ceiling in poufs like an Arabian tent. Visnujana Swami further set the mood: “Just relax, let your mind quiet down and listen to these vibrations from the spiritual world.”

We performed *The Fish Play*, Kirtanananda Swami gave a talk on Krishna consciousness and there was plenty of *kirtan*. Visnujana Swami wound up this performance with one of his heart-penetrating speeches, asking people to realize their true identities and join us in chanting Hare Krishna. The whole audience chanted with us and danced in bliss. While *prasadam* was being served, I manned the display of Srila Prabhupada’s books.

## Sri Sri Radha-Damodara

Since the Deities were tied to Their altar for safety, Kirtanananda Swami wrote to Srila Prabhupada to request They be named Radha-Damodara, in connection with a childhood pastime of Lord Krishna’s in which mother Yasoda tied Him up. Srila Prabhupada’s reply caught up with us:

Jayapur  
20th January, 1972  
Orlando, Florida

My dear Kirtanananda,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated January 2, 1972, and I am very very pleased to hear from you again and to hear that your roving party is having great success and opening many temples. Actually that is the process, traveling, preaching, taking opportunity wherever Krishna offers it and leaving behind solid centers where propaganda will continue to go on nicely. You are doing very good work and I am very much pleased upon you for such sincere effort. Your process of speaking in colleges and other places and getting paid is very much liked by me; in this way try to penetrate every school and college

and introduce our books profusely. It is nice you are taking advantage of every opportunity for spreading Krishna consciousness by speaking on radio, attending engagements, etc. That is the method recommended by my Guru Maharaja—now you are practically demonstrating this fact with good results. So go on like this, you have all of Krishna’s blessings.

It is very nice that even the Mayor was convinced by you to buy our Krishna book. That is a very good sign.

Yes, you may name your naughty boy Radha-Damodara because He is always being tied up with ropes.

Hoping this will meet you in good health and happy mood. Your ever well-wisher,

A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada

ACBS/sda

P.S. For the last one week we are having festival in Jayapur (Rajasthan) .

We were all happy to hear that our preaching efforts pleased Srila Prabhupada. And he named Sri Sri Radha-Damodara: “You may name your naughty boy Radha-Damodara.” Not many Deities in ISKCON had special names at this time. Unfortunately, the Orlando temple didn’t last long after the Road Show left.

## The Deputy Sheriff in Houma, Mississippi

In Houma we stopped for a day to sell books. It wasn’t uncommon for us to jump out at gas stations and sell books to pay for the gas, but in Houma we even stopped cars driving down the streets. Dressed in our saris and *dhotis*, we were nearly taking over the town. In the late afternoon, however, Kirtanananda Swami and a few others were nowhere to be seen. A few of us just sat around on a curb waiting for them.

A local sheriff drove up and announced: “Ya’ll got four minutes to git out a’ town! Ya’all hear me? Four minutes!” Radha Kanta dasa, one of our drivers, revved up the temple bus as Visnujana Swami, Silavati and I jumped into it. The other buses filled with devotees, and we headed down empty country roads toward the next town, where we had an evening engagement.

I overheard Visnujana Swami and Silavati’s anxiety-filled conversation: “They tolerated us all day, and now they’ve run us out of town.”

“That redneck deputy was pretty upset. Do you think it’s because here in the deep South they hate Northerners?”

“Maybe they didn’t like us because we’re devotees.” “He sure wanted us out of town fast.”

“Maybe it’s a trick. Maybe they wanted us out of town so they could ambush us in the middle of nowhere.”

“If that happens, how will we protect the Deities?”

“Well, it’s our duty to protect Radha-Damodara, but we’ll be really helpless if they attack us.”

“If something happens to the Deities, we’ll probably go to hell!” Fortunately nothing happened. Krishna protected us!

Arriving at our destination, we learned that the sheriff had arrested the missing devotees, and the show went on without them. Visnujana Swami soon made a decision to join the Road Show full-time. He temporarily left us to close his preaching centers, and he said he would meet us in Houston.

We then traveled to Louisiana and visited the New Orleans temple, another building smelling of Spiritual Sky incense. Nityananda dasa, the president, didn’t want us to stay nearby because our reputation had reached him before us. He directed us to park in some swamp out of town, which was quite inconvenient, especially when one bus sank into the morass. After a lot of effort, we extracted it.

## The Houston Temple

When the Road Show rolled into Houston, we got a warm reception from the temple president, Hridayananda prabhu, and his wife, my friend Ananga-manjari dasi. The ladies were welcome to stay in the temple while we prepared for a theater engagement in a month’s time. Visnujana Swami took over the Road Show to some extent, transforming *The Fish Play* into a rock opera. I felt the old school being replaced by the new and wasn’t all that inspired. Besides, Silavati prabhu was giving me an especially hard time. After a week or so I decided to quit the Road Show and become a Houston temple devotee.

I was anxious to take second initiation because only *brahmanas* could worship Lord Jagannatha and Radha-Krishna. I had asked Kirtanananda Swami to recommend me, and he had, but his letter went unanswered. That was normal; many letters to and from India got lost. Now I asked Hridayananda prabhu, who wrote to Srila Prabhupada, but no answer came immediately.

Hridayananda and Ananga Manjari prabhus, I thought, were the most attached couple I knew; they were inseparable. They were also aware of Srila Prabhupada’s disgust with the break-up of many devotee marriages, and they wanted to prove to him that some householders could please him by their service. He had written to them on July 5, 1971, “Recently too many couples have been drifting into *maya's* waters, and it is very discouraging.” More recently he had written letters to several devotees, refusing to bless marriages. Wanting to please Srila Prabhupada by showing him that some disciples could be exemplary *grhasthas,* in early May Hridayananda and Ananga Manjari decided to travel and preach and distribute books. They just lacked a car.

“If we go see my parents in Los Angeles,” Hridayananda prabhu said, “they will give us a car.”

Pusta Krishna prabhu, who was now vice-president of the Houston temple, was left in charge, and they flew to Los Angeles to see his parents. I don’t think it was a coincidence that Srila Prabhupada was also there.

While they were gone Satsvarupa prabhu visited Houston, in his position as Governing Body Commissioner (GBC) for mid-America. Srila Prabhupada had set up the GBC body in 1970. Dividing the world into zones, he had appointed twelve GBCs to oversee the temples and maintain the spiritual standards.

When I revealed to Satsvarupa prabhu that I missed composing for ISKCON Press, he recalled my typing abilities and instead asked me to become his secretary in Dallas, his new base. I accepted.

## Dallas Temple-May, 1972: Second Initiation

The Dallas temple was formerly an impressive church—a stone-clad edifice with a large, adjacent building for Sunday school. The immense temple room, previously a gym, had concrete-block walls and a linoleum floor. The stage, at one end, where the altar was placed, dwarfed the small Panca-tattva painting.

The main focus of the temple at this time was ISKCON’s only *gurukula*. It was small and well organized then. The schoolboys and teachers lived on one side of the huge basement, under the mostly unused church sanctuary, and the little girls lived on the other side. Although I wasn’t involved with the school, I carefully avoid talking *prajalpa* (nonsense), knowing that little ears could be hearing every word I said.

Since Saha-devi dasi, the wife of Stoka Krishna dasa, the temple president, had a newborn son, she had her own room in the large basement, and she let me share it.

Several householder couples lived in temple-owned houses in the neighborhood. The first night I was there; a devotee took me to visit the home of Indira and Vamanadeva prabhus, who had full-scale Deity worship—three altars. Vamamadeva, freshly bathed after working as a carpenter all day, offered *arati*, while Indira sat at her sewing machine, making a new outfit for their Deities. Their young son was asleep in the bedroom. I was witnessing *grhastha* life at **its** best. Dallas ISKCON was more than a temple—it was a community of devotees.

After three weeks, Satsvarupa prabhu announced he was traveling to Los Angeles to take *sannyasa* from Srila Prabhupada. The night before he left, I made one more effort to get recommended for *brahmana* initiation. He astounded me by responding, “I’m sorry! I forgot to tell you—a letter came from Srila Prabhupada, and you’re getting initiated tomorrow!” He gave me a photocopy of the letter:

CENTER: ISKCON Los Angeles  
DATE: May 17, 1972

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have received several letters from Hridayananda requesting sacred thread initiation for Pusta Krishna and Mahamaya dasi in Houston centre. So now he has come to Los Angeles so I am sending you their threads and two copies of *gayatri* mantra enclosed. You may hold a fire yajna and give them both *gayatri* mantra. You know well how to do it.

Another thing, I am giving sannyas to Rupanuga, Karandhar and Bali Mardan and as you have also asked me, I shall also be pleased to give you sannyas. The others are coming to Los Angeles by the 25th May, 1972, and the ceremony will be held on the 27th May. However, as I am most probably coming to Dallas on my return from Mexico City, it is more practical to give you sannyas order of life at that time.

Hoping this will find you in good health,

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Satsvarupa das Adhikary,  
c/o ISKCON *Gurukula*, Dallas Texas  
ACBS/sda

\[*Handwritten by Srila Prabhupada:*\] If possible you can also come and take sannyas along with others.

As Srila Prabhupada had sanctioned, Satsvarupa prabhu was the priest at a fire sacrifice in Dallas on May 25th, the Ekadasi before Nrsimha-caturdasi. During the ceremony Pusta Krishna prabhu and I listened via earphone, in the right ear, to a tape of Srila Prabhupada chanting the *gayatri* mantra. We followed the words printed on our slips of paper.

Before and during the ceremony I felt terribly sick. But fortified by several glasses of hot *brahmastra* juice (made from ginger, red chillis and a sweetener) that Jahnava dasi mercifully forced down my throat, I was there—barely. At every moment I felt as though an unseen force was pushing me out of the sacrificial arena.

Rupanuga prabhu was visiting his son in the *gurukula* before taking *sannyasa*, and he gave the lecture. Somehow I managed to stay put through the ceremony and lecture.

Getting second initiation meant I vowed to chant the *gayatri* mantra three times a day—morning, noon and night. Liking this silent meditation, I eagerly memorized the words on the slip of paper. Now I could worship Deities!

After our Ekadasi feast, Satsvarupa and Rupanuga prabhus left for Los Angeles. Later we heard that four men took *sannyasa* on Nrsimha-caturdasi, but not the four named in the letter. Instead of Karandhara, Hridayananda accepted *sannyasa* from Srila Prabhupada. I was amazed!

## Studying the Srimad-Bhagavatam

Srila Prabhupada’s secretary circulated an important letter around the Society concerning the structure of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class. It was dated June 16, 1972, and written to Madhudvisa Swami:

I am very much stressing at this point that all of my students shall be very much conversant with the philosophy of Krishna Consciousness, and that they should read our books very diligently at least one or two hours daily and try to understand the subject matter from varieties of angles. We are holding our morning class here in Los Angeles in the temple and I am speaking from 7 to 8 am, and the process is that we are going through some chapters of Srimad-Bhagavatam by taking one sloka each day, and reading the Sanskrit aloud, each word is pronounced by me and repeated by the students and then altogether we chant the sloka several time until we have learned it. And then we discuss the subject matter very minutely and inspect it from all angles of approach and savor the new understandings. So you introduce this system in all of the centers in your zone, and you will discover that everyone becomes very much enlivened by these daily classes. Read one sloka and discuss and then go on to the next sloka on the next day, and so on, and even you discuss one verse each day it will take you 50 years to finish Srimad-Bhagavatam in this way. So we have got ample stock for acquiring knowledge. And if the students get knowledge more and more, they will automatically become convinced and very easily perform their duties for tapasya or renunciaton of the material bondage, and that will be their successful advancement in Krishna Consciousness. So I want that advancement amongst all of my students, so you are responsible that the standard will be maintained.

Following Srila Prabhupada’s instruction, the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class became an in-depth study of one *sloka* per day. First we chanted the verse in Sanskrit many times, collectively, then individually. The lecturer read the synonyms, the translation of the verse and Srila Prabhupada’s purport. After lecturing on the verse and purport, including his own realizations, he ended the class with questions and answers.

The *Bhagavad-gita* classes also followed this format. Serious study of the *sastras*, or scriptures, was now part of our daily schedule. We were eager for that spiritual advancement Srila Prabhupada promised.

## Sri Sri Radha-Kalachandji

Once Satsvarupa Maharaja took *sannyasa*, he traveled a good deal. As he needed my secretarial services only rarely, I became the head *pujari* for Panca-tattva. The temple devotees wanted to worship Radha-Krishna Deities and were waiting for a set they had ordered from India.

During the summer of 1972 a group of *gurukula* students and their teachers traveled to San Francisco to attend America’s only Rathayatra parade. Srila Prabhupada had inaugurated this festival in 1967 by instructing the devotees to make huge, colorful, hand-drawn carts, fashioned after the ones in Jagannatha Puri, which carry Lord Jagannatha, Baladeva and Subhadra. The San Francisco parade attracted thousands of people to pull the carts and follow the parade through Golden Gate Park. It ended at Hippie Hill, the site of a festival that included entertainment, *kirtan* and *prasadam*. I asked to go, too, but it wasn’t possible.

“You’ll be able to go to the Janmastami and Vyasa-puja festival in New Vrindaban this year,” I was told. “Srila Prabhupada is going there, and all the devotees—teachers, kids, and temple devotees are going. Afterward Srila Prabhupada will visit Dallas.”

If the truck bringing two sets of Radha-Krishna Deities—one set for Dallas, another for the Detroit temple—hadn’t broken down in Ohio, the Deities would have arrived before we left for New Vrindaban. Having seen the Deities, I might have wanted to stay in Dallas. But it wasn’t meant to be. Laksmi-Narayana dasa convinced the temple authorities that his wife, who was about to arrive from another temple and who was experienced in dressing Deities, should replace me as the head *pujari*, even though no one expected Srila Prabhupada to install the new Deities for a year or more. I couldn’t take being demoted. I asked permission to stay in New Vrindaban after the festival. Stoka Krishna prabhu agreed, and I was confident that Kirtanananda Swami would accept me in New Vrindaban.

Later, I heard that both sets of Radha-Krishna Deities were delivered while we were in New Vrindaban. By some fluke, the two Radharanis got switched, and therefore the Detroit Radharani is taller than Krishna, who stands on a pedestal to compensate, and the Dallas Radharani is petite compared to Her Krishna.

When Srila Prabhupada visited Dallas after giving what were called the *Bhagavat Dharma* Discourses in New Vrindaban, he surprised everyone by immediately installing the Deities. He appropriately named Them Radha-Kalachandji. *Kala* means black, and *chandji* is the moon. Krishna was a huge black-marble Deity with a big round fatherly-looking face.

## Chapter 5 - New Vrindaban Days

## The Bhagavat Dharma Discourses—September, 1972

About a thousand devotees and guests gathered at New Vrindaban to meet Srila Prabhupada and attend his lecture series, the Bhagavat Dharma Discourses. Devotees came from all over the country, but mainly the northeast and mid-America. Guests came by the carloads. People heard about it and came. It was a happening, like a Rainbow Festival or Woodstock.

Srila Prabhupada drove in from Pittsburgh, where he saw the Road Show perform the rock opera. We heard he didn’t like it; he didn’t even recognize the performers: “Are these our men?” That was the kiss of death for the Road Show. After one more performance it folded. I’m glad I quit before this ignominious ending.

Everyone waiting for Srila Prabhupada to arrive chanted Hare Krishna together outside the temple-farmhouse at Bahulavan, the new main farm, located on a winding country road. Some ladies had prepared a new red-velvet *vyasasana* in the shade of a large tree. I made garlands to decorate the overhead canopy. Devotees from Washington, D.C. brought many large potted tulasi trees, which were placed all around the *vyasasana*. The *kirtan* was going strong for quite some time before we finally heard shouts: “There’s Srila Prabhupada’s car! Srila Prabhupada is coming! Haribol!”

The devotees offered *dandavats*, and our *kirtan* surrounded Srila Prabhupada as he emerged from the car, walked across the lawn and sat down on the *vyasasana*. Srila Prabhupada looked wonderful on this sunny day, sitting on a red velvet *vyasanana* in a *tulasi* grove.

After giving an arrival address, Srila Prabhupada went to his new quarters a mile down the road in an old farmhouse at Madhuban, another New Vrindaban farm. During his previous visit, Srila Prabhupada stayed at Vrindavan, the original farm. Now New Vrindaban was expanding and owned many acres of land on several separate farms.

The visiting devotees and guests camped in tents, up a hill, but I stayed in a small building with New Vrindaban devotees because I easily received permission to join the farm. Silavati prabhu, now the head *pujari* at Bahulavan, amazingly accepted me as her close friend, forgetting all the hard times of the past. It seemed I passed some kind of private test.

I was put in charge of flowers during the festival. Garlands and floral arrangements in vases had to be made for Srila Prabhupada and all the Deities in the temple and festival tent. I organized many helpers daily. We soon ran out of store-bought flowers, and we picked wild flowers, especially wispy white Queen Anne’s Lace.

I served along with those I engaged in this service, but I saved a most nectarean service for myself—throwing flower petals on the path before Srila Prabhupada’s lotus feet. During the weeklong festival whenever Srila Prabhupada walked up the hill to the big tent, I walked backwards directly in front of him and tossed petals from a basket toward his feet. The devotees were offering their obeisances all around Srila Prabhupada as he proceeded, but I watched him the whole time. How I relished seeing Srila Prabhupada so close! As I threw petals, Srila Prabhupada many times folded his hands and nodded his head. Thrilled to have my service acknowledged, I felt he was blessing me by his pranams.

Once, as Srila Prabhupada walked up the hill, a guest grabbed some of the flower petals and threw them on Srila Prabhupada’s head. That didn’t please Srila Prabhupada. He brushed them off and shaking his head said, “No.” I regretted not guarding the basket better, and after that I was more careful.

Arriving at the tent, Srila Prabhupada mounted a simple stage. His *vyasasana* was on the left side as you faced the stage, and Deities were on the right: Radha-Damodara, straight from the Pittsburgh performance, and big, local Jagannatha Deities. Srila Prabhupada’s Bhagavat Dharma Discourses were brilliant and thought-provoking.

One night it rained heavily, and all the campers got soaked. Several devotees stayed up all night to protect the Jagannatha Deities from the rain. They held the tent flaps secure to keep the Deities dry. Radha-Damodara spent the night in a van or bus. It became muddy everywhere, true to the farm’s nickname—Mudsville—a twist on Moundsville.

Since the previous year’s installation of Sri Sri Radha–Vrindaban-candra, the dirt road to the remote Vrindaban farm had become impassible except by foot, on horse or in a four-wheel drive Jeep. Therefore, some months before, the Deities had been moved to Bahulaban. During the festival Srila Prabhupada presided over the opening of the new temple room, a devotee-built extension of the old farmhouse. The marble floor was especially beautiful—multi-colored, with an indented path from Srila Prabhupada’s *vyasasana* to the marble altar where Radha–Vrindaban-candra now presided.

One day an opportunity to offer *arati* came my way. I was scared of making a mistake yet honored to render such intimate service to the Lord during the festival. It was both intimidating and exciting to be a *pujari* with hundreds of devotees chanting and dancing.

One morning, during the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class in the new temple, Krishnamayi dasi chanted the Sanskrit verse so well that Srila Prabhupada said, “Very nice!”

## Janmastami

During our festival we celebrated Janmastami, the appearance day of Lord Krishna, a fast until midnight for us, but a feast day for the Lord. Outdoor kitchens were set up for the cooks from various temples. Wonderful foodstuffs were prepared and offered every hour all day long by *pujaris*, at special *aratis*.

A large initiation ceremony at Bahulaban got started late. When Srila Prabhupada entered the temple room, nothing was ready—not the sacrificial arena, the paraphernalia—nothing. And devotees had packed the sole hallway leading to and from the temple room. No one could move in or out—total gridlock. Silavati prabhu asked me to help her set things up for the fire sacrifice, but how? We had to use the only unblocked route: This route—from the *pujari* room, through the altar room and into the temple room—was prohibited, but we had to do the needful. We hauled in sand, firewood and all the paraphernalia and set it up while Srila Prabhupada watched from the *vyasasana*. I felt pressured to do this quickly because we were late, and it was nerve-racking to sense Srila Prabhupada and hundreds of devotees watching us. Finally everything was ready.

About forty-five were receiving initiation, so Srila Prabhupada asked a few senior devotees to help him chant on the initiates’ beads. I stayed in the temple room to see everyone receive beads and a spiritual name from Srila Prabhupada.

In the evening Srila Prabhupada wanted to hear the story of Lord Krishna’s advent. Devotees packed into the temple room to listen to various devotees read from the Krishna book for hours and hours. Many, including me, listened from outside through the windows. Some stood on piled up crates reaching the high windows on one side of the temple. The reading continued until the midnight *arati*.

A feast took place at one a.m. Afterwards many of us stayed up to chant our rounds. We didn’t want to sleep for just a few hours at the risk of missing *mangal-arati*, because the next day was another auspicious event, Srila Prabhupada’s seventy-sixth appearance day.

## Vyasa-puja

Vyasa-puja day was celebrated in the festival tent. We were so happy to be with Srila Prabhupada on this day. Our happiness turned to dismay, however, because we didn’t know how to offer the ceremony properly, and Srila Prabhupada became upset and displeased with us. He immediately gave us another chance though, after instructing us how to do it: In order of seniority the disciples already on the stage approached the *vyasasana* one at a time and offered flower petals and full obeisances three times. Srila Prabhupada also explained that the *sannyasis* should go first, then GBCs and temple presidents.

After many rank-and-file men in order of seniority had also offered their flowers, Srila Prabhupada indicated that Silavati prabhu should come forward. Older and worthy of our respect, she was the only lady who made an individual offering of flowers. Time did not allow for every single devotee to do the same, but Srila Prabhupada was satisfied.

Srila Prabhupada often emphasized seniority. This Vaisnava etiquette was the way of temple life from the day I joined ISKCON. We knew who was most senior and which devotees had joined around the same time and who was junior because of joining later. We respected seniors, made friends with equals, and preached to the junior devotees. *Sannyasis* were shown the most respect because they had accepted the renounced order. The proper relationships flourished when Vaisnava etiquette was followed, so I appreciated Srila Prabhupada’s impeccable adherence to etiquette during formal ceremonies like Vyasa-puja.

I gave Srila Prabhupada a letter containing fifty dollars that I collected with much effort on *sankirtan* in Dallas. I also gave him a handmade Deity handkerchief decorated with several rows of trim, for his Deities. However, being unable to travel with all his gifts, Srila Prabhupada left it behind for Radha–Vrindaban-candra.

## The Vrindaban Farm

After the festival I moved to the Vrindaban farm, where I began to take care of the big Lord Jagannatha Deities. Silavati prabhu needed another *pujari* at Bahulvan to help with Radha–Vrindaban-candra, but Hladini dasi was selected because she was more experienced. It seemed backwards to me because Hladini was attracted only to Lord Jagannatha Deities—she even looked like Lady Subhadra—while I wanted Silavati prabhu’s expert training.

The Vrindaban farm’s temple room had many windows, affording a view across a forest toward a ridge. Upstairs was the small room where Srila Prabhupada stayed in 1969. The basement kitchen was dark and damp and was probably the only ISKCON kitchen where the devotees wore boots.

The devotees lived in wood cabins scattered across a hillside. Deep in the woods, my cabin was equipped with a wood-burning stove made from a fifty-five-gallon oil drum, which barely heated the place during the freezing winter. My roommate, Cintamoni dasi, worshiped her own small Jagannatha Deities on the temple altar. Her Deities were previously worshiped in the Tokyo temple, which she and Sudama dasa, her former husband, and Bali Mardan dasa had opened years earlier. After Sudama took *sannyasa*, Srila Prabhupada told her twice: “Make Lord Jagannatha your husband, Lord Balarama your son and Lady Subhadra your daughter.”

Only the temple building had running water; life was austere. We certainly lived by Srila Prabhupada’s motto of “simple living and high thinking.”

“You think it’s austere?” an old New Vrindaban hand asked me. “You should have been here last winter. It was so cold that a cow froze to death, and all we had to eat was rice and popcorn.”

One day someone walked up the dirt road to inform us that the ladies should now be addressed as “Mother So-and-so.” We giggled as we stuck the word “Mother” before ladies’ names, thinking it sounded strange. Only “Mother Silavati” sounded right. There were other changes—ladies having to stand at the back of the temple room and ladies offering obeisances only on our knees, not fully prostrated. Whether or not we liked the changes, we had to accept them. We were told, “This is Vedic.” We assumed that Srila Prabhupada approved of them.

## A Second Letter from Srila Prabhupada

A letter from Srila Prabhupada got sent from temple to temple until it reached me in New Vrindaban:

September 9, 1972  
ISKCON Dallas

My dear Mahamaya,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you very much for your kindly remembering me on Vyas Puja birth day, and I have duly received your gift of $50.00, and I have appreciated your warm sentiments with great satisfaction. I am very much encouraged by your progress in Krishna Consciousness. Now go on in this way always striving to please Krishna and you will feel yourself becoming happy more and more. That I want. Thank you once again,

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Mahamaya devi dasi  
ACBS/sdg

Because Cintamoni prabhu had already received a similarly worded letter, I understood this was a form letter sent to those who gave Vyasa-puja gifts. Still, it was personal. I especially liked the concise sentence: “That I want,” in reference to the increasing happiness I would feel by serving Krishna.

## The Madhuban Farm and Marriage

Once the roads thawed enough in early 1973 to accommodate a four-wheel-drive vehicle, Hayagriva dasa arranged for our big Jagannatha Deities to move to Madhuban farm. I went along.

In my opinion, Madhuban was the most austere farm. Most of the devotees who lived there were *grhastha* couples with kids. The old farmhouse always looked dirty, even though we scrubbed everywhere, even the walls. The temple room, formerly Srila Prabhupada’s bedroom during the Bhagavat Dharma Discourses, was so cold that we wore socks on the altar. Hladini prabhu also moved to Madhuban, worshiping her own Jagannatha Deities on the altar. She spent her days in the kitchen cooking on wood stoves, without the luxury of running water. Water was hauled from a deep well some distance away. When she finished cooking, both the kitchen and her sari were disasters. The *prasadam* was cooked with devotion, but it was austere fare—no fresh fruits or vegetables. We practically lived for the spectacular Sunday feasts at Bahulaban. We bathed and washed clothes with water from a cistern well that collected rainwater. Because the roof was full of coal dust from the furnace, even our clean clothes looked gray. You could easily spot Madhuban devotees at the feasts. We looked gray, too!

Kunti dasi, the single mother of little Kham dasi (later Karnamrta), volunteered to wash everyone’s clothes in an ancient washing machine in the basement. Hauling bucket after bucket of water from the cistern well to the washer was unbelievably difficult, but she did the service with a smile.

The men dragged dead trees out of the forest during the summers and sawed them into sections, and the ladies helped split logs for the kitchen stove. Even in extremely cold weather, I’d throw off my outer clothing while swinging the axe. The exertion worked up a sweat. After forty-five minutes I would be exhausted and careless—and get smacked by flying wood fragments, indicting it was time to stop.

I wasn’t very tolerant of the kids in Madhuban and wanted to move to another farm. At the Sunday feast I told Kuladri prabhu, Kirtanananda Swami’s right-hand man, “I want to move back to the Vrindaban farm. I heard the *brahmacarinis* just moved up there.”

“Actually, we want you to get married,” Kuladri said.

“To whom?”

“That doesn’t matter. You talk to the other ladies at your farm and make up your mind.”

Naturally, the other ladies, themselves *grhasthas*, encouraged me to get married, even though we couldn’t figure out to whom. Was it to the cowherd at Bahulaban? I did not want to get married. I was trying to escape from the *grhastha* scene at Madhuban. But I agreed, not wanting to disobey the authorities.

I felt upset when I learned that Kirtanananda Swami wanted me to marry Tribanga dasa, a Spiritual Sky salesman working Indiana, lured to New Vrindaban by promises of a wife, house, *maha-prasadam* and whatever he wanted. They chose me for two reasons. I was the only one shorter than him (he was very short) and close to his age (I was twenty-six, he twenty-nine). Although especially not wanting to marry him, I could see no way out.

The next Sunday we were married at a New Vrindaban “flower ceremony,” in which we exchanged flowers on the temple porch at Bahulaban. Everyone cheered and considered us married. We were given the Deities’ *maha-prasadam* afterward.

The marriage went downhill from that point.

“If your marriage lasts a year,” I was told, “you’ll qualify for a real wedding at a fire sacrifice.” I didn’t have a modicum of hope for a bright future. He had different expectations than me and ridiculed me for being staunch about the regulative principle not to have illicit sex. It was embarrassing to be married to this man. My unforgiving feelings are most accurately described by a crude expression I learned while growing up: I hated his guts.

## “Honeymoon”

Once married, I survived six weeks on the road with Tribanga selling Spiritual Sky incense and eating only his homemade version of trail mix. His recipe called for a couple of tins of mixed nuts and salted peanuts, a package of dates, and a bag of shredded, sweetened coconut, all mixed together in a paperbag.

During our travels, Tribanga got a huge order for incense in Louisville, Kentucky, so we returned to New Vrindaban to fill the order. There we saw Dharmatma dasa, a *sankirtan* leader from Vancouver, who was looking for a place to repair an old Road Show bus he’d just bought. Tribanga invited Dharmatma prabhu and his group to come with us to Louisville, because there he knew a favorable couple, whose house we’d stayed in.

Dharmatma drove his bus to Louisville and parked on the street in the couple’s neighborhood. We filled our order and left town, but he and the other devotees with him stayed three weeks longer. One *brahmacari* befriended a neighborhood girl, who showed up at the bus with packed bags just as the bus was leaving for a festival at New Vrindaban. Dharmatma refused to bring her along because she was underage and her father was the leader of a motorcycle gang. When the devotees left, she ran away from home.

Her father thought she was also going to New Vrindaban, and he drove there to look for her during the weekend festival. The festival attracted more than two hundred devotees, and Visnujana Swami led the most beautiful *kirtans* during the festivities. Not only was an Ananta Sesa Deity buried under the cornerstone of a future temple, but also two sets of small, brass Radha-Krishna Deities were installed, one set for the Vrindaban farm and the other for Madhuban. It was an open-house festival, and the girl’s father showed up in his old convertible—one of those really huge ones. He came with another man and the local sheriff. Devotees escorted them around, to convince them his daughter wasn’t here. The father returned a second time, with his friend and two ladies, to see more and ask more questions. However, the matter wasn’t settled that weekend.

## The Bahulaban Farm

After my six weeks on the road with him, Tribanga traveled and sold incense alone, returning occasionally, while I lived at the Bahulaban farm, following a tight schedule. I went on the altar mornings and evenings, helped to roll hundreds of chapatis for lunch and baked the four p.m. offering. I came to Bahulaban too late to get Mother Silavati’s training—she had moved to New York—but Kutila dasi, Kuladri’s wife, was an excellent head *pujari* and a stickler for kitchen cleanliness. I loved the *pujari* service. Following a rotating schedule in the mornings, I dressed Radharani once a week, Vrindaban-candra once a week and either tiny Radha-Krishna or Lord Jagannatha the other days.

However, every evening for nine months, I dressed the same Deity: Srimati Radharani. I took off Her day clothes and jewelry and put on Her nightclothes. I became quick and adept, because we had little time. One night, in the usual rush to finish on time, I looked at Radharani’s face and froze, thinking, “This is Radharani!” But I had to set aside that thought to continue my service. I knew a passage from The Nectar of Devotion about Daruka feeling ecstasy while fanning Krishna, but setting aside his feeling to continue his service. I wasn’t on Daruka’s platform of devotional love, but I felt something similar that day by recognizing Radharani and then continuing to serve Her under time pressure.

Later, I heard a class that helped me better understand the Deities and Their feelings and interactions with *pujaris*. When Srila Prabhupada himself installed Deities for the first time in ISKCON—small brass Radha-Krishna Deities, in Los Angeles, on July 16, 1969—at the end of his class he said:

If you think it is a brass-made doll, I mean to say, idol—*ye yatha mam prapadyante tams tathaiva bhajamy aham*—if you think this is a brass-made idol, then it will remain a brass-made idol to you forever. But if you elevate yourself to a higher platform of Krishna consciousness, then Krishna, this Krishna, will talk with you. This Krishna will talk with you.

There is a Vamsidas Babaji Maharaja, he was talking with his Deity. And the Madana-mohana Deity—He was talking with Sanatana Gosvami. Sanatana Gosvami at that time had no temple; he was hanging his Deity on the tree. So Madana-mohana was talking with him: “Sanatana, you are bringing all these dry *chapatis*. And it is stale, and you don’t give Me even little salt. How can I eat?”

Sanatana Gosvami said, “Sir, where shall I go? Whatever I get I offer You. You kindly accept. I cannot move; I am an old man.”

You see. So Krishna had to eat that \[*chuckles*\]. Because the *bhakta* is offering, He cannot refuse. *Ye mam bhaktya prayacchati*: real thing is *bhakti*. What you can offer to Krishna? Everything belongs to Krishna. What you have got? What is your value? And what is the value of your things? It is nothing. Therefore real thing is *bhaktya*, real thing is your feeling. “Krishna, kindly take it. I have no qualification. I am most rotten, fallen, but \[*begins to cry*\] I have brought this thing for you. Please take it.” This will be accepted. Don’t be puffed up. Always be careful. You are dealing with Krishna. That is my request. Thank you very much.

## Thugs Attack Bahulaban

In June, 1973, during the week following the open-house festival, an armed motorcycle gang attacked the temple. It was a local gang hired by the distraught Louisville father, who was with them, still looking for his runaway daughter. Surrounding the temple in the early morning, as the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class was being held, they fired shotguns through open windows and doors, hitting two men, to gain control.

I was on the altar bathing the Deities. Kutila prabhu and I managed to place the small Deities—Jagannatha and Radha-Krishna—out of sight behind the altar before we were ordered into the temple room. There I saw Bidhan Candra and Krishna Katha prabhus lying bleeding on the floor.

We chanted the Nrsimha-deva prayers for the Lord’s protection, but a gang member demanded, “Stop your chanting!” and shot another bullet through the ceiling.

“If you don’t give us the girl, we’re going to kill all of you!” one of the villains threatened.

Terrified, I truly thought we were all going to die because the girl wasn’t there and had never visited New Vrindaban. The previous weekend, when her father had looked for her at our festival, he’d seen a cornerstone-laying ceremony, at which a Deity of Ananta Sesa was buried in a deep pit. He thought we had sacrificed the girl, and was convinced the pit was the girl’s grave.

The father demanded: “Who is your leader?” Kirtanananda Swami identified himself.

“Who was the leader down in Louisville?”

“I was,” Dharmatma prabhu admitted.

“We’re taking you both to dig your own graves where you cut up and buried my daughter. I’ve heard about cults like yours!”

We helplessly watched Kirtanananda Swami and Dharmatma being taken away. Miraculously, the girl’s father did not recognize the *brahmacari* who’d befriended his daughter, though he was right there, in the corner of the temple room, fearing for his life.

We softly chanted *japa*, praying fervently to Lord Krishna.

“What’s behind the curtain?” one of the thugs asked. Tearing down the Deity curtain, he saw the marble forms of Radha–Vrindaban-candra, still not dressed because Their bath was interrupted. This monster forced one of our men to push over Srimati Radharani. Then he himself pushed over Lord Vrindaban-candra! The Deity broke slightly as He hit the lower shelf of the marble altar, which cracked and broke beneath Him. Standing on the altar, this envious demon declared—just like Hiranyakasipu—”Now I am God. Worship me!”

The loud sounds of the two Deities hitting the marble floor frightened the gang members walking up the hill. While the first crash was startling, the second sounded like a gunshot, and they thought the devotees had gained control of the situation. They began to run away, yelling at those inside to join them. Thus the ordeal suddenly and unexpectedly ended.

The wounded men were taken to the hospital and Radha–Vrindaban-candra were carried to Kirtanananda Swami’s house. Kutila and I retrieved the small Deities. She hid Radha-Krishna somewhere safe, while I and others carried the Jagannatha Deities to Talavan, a more secluded farm.

We prepared for the gang’s return because one thug had threatened, “We’ll be back tonight to burn the place down.” The police refused to protect us, so by nightfall the devotees had armed themselves. Several devotees stayed up all night to guard the farm, hidden from sight with all the lights out. But by Krishna’s mercy neither the father nor the gang ever returned.

Hearing about these unfortunate events, Srila Prabhupada wrote to Kirtanananda Swami on June 14, 1973:

I am in due receipt of your letter dated June 6, 1973 and have noted the contents with great care and concern.

The Deities may be immediately repaired and worshipped. Now we must take precaution as you have suggested. You may get some guns and some of the boys may be trained as ksatriyas \[warriors\]. Such gun shots could have taken place long ago since we are challenging everybody. So we must be prepared to fight. We cannot stop this movement. It is actually a fight against Maya. So Maya may also sometimes cause casualties in our camp. So, we must only thank Krishna and seriously dedicate everything for Him. So, you are Kirtanananda Maharaja, take pleasure in performingkirtana. That is our real weapon againstMaya.

On June 22nd, Srila Prabhupada wrote again:

After the shooting affair what precaution have you taken? Bharadraja is here and he gave report that the devotees were very frightened. I further understand that the attack was for the second time. Here in Mayapur there are reports of dacoits at least once or twice in a month surrounding our place. So we have now taken two guns under regular license from the government. So when New Vrindaban has been attacked twice, thrice, why are you not keeping guns? We are not advocates of non-violence; when there is aggression we must kill them. So I think you shall immediately arrange for guns and at least 10, 12 men should be trained up so when there is again attack you can properly reply the aggressor.

. . . In the meantime I shall be glad to hear from you what defense measures you have taken to protect the life and property of New Vrindaban. This is very important and you must take all steps. Actually the government should have arrested all the gang and punished them properly for their atrocious behavior on unarmed people. Is there an attempt to have the government punish the gang who are well known to your country? We just take measure against occasional attacks by criminals.

To add insult to injury, these gang members were tried in the local court but not convicted, despite the testimony of many devotees. Justice, it seems, would be attained only on a higher plane, not in the material world.

## Leaving New Vrindaban

I left New Vrindaban on January 3, 1974, three years to the day since I had joined ISKCON. After eight months of hell, married to Tribanga, I decided to leave him, and chose to go to Los Angeles because Srila Prabhupada was there and because the Los Angeles temple, being opulent and cosmopolitan, was the antithesis of New Vrindaban. When Tribanga learned I was going, he said, “Don’t leave me here! I also want to go to Los Angeles.”

I agreed to travel with him, thinking I could use his protection. It was better than traveling alone, and it worked out. We hitchhiked to Moundsville, and with two rides from there we reached Indianapolis, Tribanga’s previous sales territory. We stayed overnight with a couple he had cultivated in Krishna consciousness. I cooked a feast. We loaned them devotional clothing, and we had a *kirtan* and *arati*.

The next morning they dropped us off at the highway, where we again hitchhiked. Our first ride was going to Los Angeles. After riding twenty-four hours a day for two and a half days, we were dropped at the door of the Los Angeles temple.

## Back to Brahmacarini Life

Srila Prabhupada had been in Los Angeles for five weeks, but he was leaving in a week. For his *Srimad-Bhagavatam* classes many devotees packed into the temple room, and after classes the children walked to the *vyasasana* to receive cookies from Srila Prabhupada’s hand.

There was some ceremony I can’t recall, but Pradyumna prabhu, who traveled with Srila Prabhupada as his Sanskrit editor, went around to the ladies saying, “Srila Prabhupada wants you to make the ululating sound the ladies make in India.” We didn’t know how to do it. However, we wanted to please Srila Prabhupada, so we tried. Without an example to follow, it sounded like an American Indian war cry or a sick cow bellowing.

Three weeks after Srila Prabhupada departed, my “flower-ceremony” husband left the movement, or “blooped,” as Srila Prabhupada would say. “Bloop” was the sound of the soul falling back into the material pool. I was glad to be a *brahmacarini* again.

I stayed in Los Angeles several months doing Deity worship. Mostly I hand-beaded outfits for Sri Sri Rukmini-Dwarakadish, which Tunga Bhadra dasi had designed and sewn. I also made garlands and cooked, and dressed the big Lord Jagannatha Deities at night. I worked directly under Manjuali dasi, the head *pujari*.

While I lived in New Vrindaban, cut off for a year and a half from the rest of ISKCON, *sankirtan* had changed. In the earlier days everyone went on *harinam sankirtan* daily. Now, only small *harinam* groups went out daily from most temples, and large groups went out only on weekend nights—in Los Angeles, to the corner of Hollywood and Vine.
