The distribution of big books had become more prominent. Srila Prabhupada was repeatedly emphasizing that book distribution pleased him. Thus many devotees dedicated themselves entirely to this important service.

The experienced distributors taught others their techniques. In Los Angeles I saw how fired up Ramesvara and Tripurari prabhus were about the Los Angeles temple winning the transcendental competition to distribute more books than other temples. Their enthusiasm was reflected in the *sankirtan* devotees.

I started feeling the pressure: “Go out on *sankirtan* and don’t be in *maya*.” It became intense. The whole *sankirtan* party, men and women, took their morning *prasadam* in the ladies’ *prasadam* room, with us. If they’d had the same *prasadam* as us, there would have been no problem. But they got lots of *maha-prasadam* milk sweets, and fruit and yogurt, whereas we got little or no *maha-prasadam* and were served hot milk and cooked cereal, even though it was spring. I got the feeling the *sankirtan* leaders were purposely trying to make us envious so that we’d join them.

However, taking care of the Deities and cooking were also vitally important. I finally heard Ramesvara prabhu admit this, comparing *sankirtan* to a bucket brigade: Water buckets pass from hand to hand to the last person, who throws water on the fire; similarly, the *sankirtan* devotees get the credit for distributing books, but they couldn’t do it without the back-up of the temple devotees. Despite his acknowledgement, I felt uncomfortable in Los Angeles.

One weekend I attended a blissful Caitanya Festival, sponsored by the San Francisco temple, managed then by Bhakta dasa. Near the San Francisco Bay in Berkeley, where I’d first seen devotees in 1968, a nine-foot-high paper-mache form of Lord Caitanya was pulled through the streets. The *kirtan*, expertly led by Madhudvisa Swami for eight hours straight, was so ecstatic that I couldn’t stop chanting the entire time. Afterward, I told Bhakta dasa I wanted to move to San Francisco, but Manjuali nixed my idea when I returned to Los Angeles.

One day, at the end of our *japa* time, the devotees chanted a new prayer, “Guru Vandana,” a Bengali song glorifying the spiritual master. A formal *guru-puja* also took place: One devotee offered *arati* to a picture of Srila Prabhupada on the *vyasasana*, while everyone else individually offered flowers to him, then obeisances. Srila Prabhupada had recently requested the devotees in India to begin this *guru-puja*, and now it had come to America. This worship of Srila Prabhupada became an important part of my spiritual life.

In May I attended a festival in San Diego, this one wonderfully led by Visnujana Swami. Again I became inspired to leave Los Angeles, and at a meeting with Jayatirtha dasa, the temple president in Los Angeles and a GBC, I explained bluntly: “I don’t want to live in Los Angeles any more because the householders are all in Maya. When I ask ladies to sew or cook for the Deities, they refuse, but they’re always willing to cook a potluck feast with their friends. I want to move to another temple.”

Jayatirtha prabhu, himself a householder, didn’t appreciate my remarks, got angry, and replied just as bluntly: “You want to move? All right! Move today, right now! I want you out of here by tonight!”

I moved to San Diego, just one hundred miles south of Los Angeles. Gunagrahi dasa, who had just become the temple president there, invited me. Short one *pujari*, the temple had plenty of services to cover. The *prasadam* was so opulent I felt like I was on a heavenly planet. Unfortunately, after a few weeks Jayatirtha prabhu sent Cinmayi dasi to fill the *pujari* post, leaving me without service.

Hoping to compose Srila Prabhupada’s books once again, I wanted to move back to New York. My mother agreed to send me an airplane ticket, but I needed to collect my taxi fare tothe airport. That was a memorable day—going to the San Diego zoo with a small group, led by Mahatma dasa, who was such an enthusiastic *sankirtan* devotee that his enthusiasm almost rubbed off on me.

## Chapter 6 - Back to ISKCON’s Birthplace

## The Temple Grows in Brooklyn

After visiting my mother when I returned to New York, I went to the Brooklyn temple. Nara-devi dasi, an ISKCON Press composer, was about to have a baby, so I was able to resume my old service, and felt very happy about that. The composers were now working on the third and fourth cantos of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam*, and the Adi-lila (first part) of *Sri Caitanya-caritamrta*.

The seventh chapter of Adi-lila, “Lord Caitanya in Five Features,” was being published as a paperback, before the other chapters came out in hardcover editions, because it was packed with philosophy and nectar about the storehouse of love of God being distributed to everyone indiscriminately. The purport to Text 4 was particularly amazing:

Our obeisances to Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu are complete when we say, *sri-krsna-caitanya prabhu nityananda sri-advaita gadadhara srivasadi-gaura-bhakta-vrnda*. As preachers of the Krishna consciousness movement, we first offer our obeisances to Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu by chanting this Panca-tattva mantra; then we say, Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. There are ten offenses in the chanting of the Hare Krishna *maha-mantra*, but these are not considered in the chanting of the Panca-tattva mantra, namely, *sri-krsna-caitanya prabhu nityananda sri-advaita gadadhara srivasadi-gaura-bhakta-vrnda*. Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu is known as *maha-vadanyavatara*, the most magnanimous incarnation, for He does not consider the offenses of the fallen souls. Thus to derive the full benefit of the chanting of the *maha-mantra* (Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare), we must first take shelter of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, learn the Panca-tattva *maha-mantra*, and then chant the Hare Krishna *maha-mantra*. That will be very effective.

When the composers read this purport, we felt that *japa* in ISKCON could be revolutionized. Before each round, we devotees would now chant the Panca-tattva mantra to offer obeisances to Lord Caitanya in His five features and thus receive His mercy to avoid offenses. The New York temple had some great *kirtan* leaders—Agni-deva, Jaya Sacinandana and Visnugada prabhus—and they were already chanting the Panca-tattva mantra in the *kirtans*.

I liked adding this to my *japa*, this begging for the mercy of the most munificent avatar, Sri Krishna Caitanya. Already I felt connected to Srila Prabhupada, as if holding his hand, because I was chanting *japa* on beads he’d chanted on for me. And taking shelter of the discipline of chanting my daily rounds, no matter what, I felt relief from a lot of anxiety.

Palika prabhu had long since gone to India with Bhavananda prabhu (who later took *sannyasa*), so we composers were no longer restricted from Deity worship. I became enthusiastic to serve under Mother Silavati, who was now the head *pujari* in Brooklyn.

Since mid-1971, when I’d left New York, the temple had been blessed with more Deities. Small new brass Gaura-Nitai Deities now stood on the left altar. On the middle altar, large Radha-Govinda Deities were standing. Lord Govinda was made from black marble, Srimati Radharani from white—both with intense gazes. Little Lord Jagannatha Deities had replaced the large ones on the right altar.

Nara-devi asked me to take over another of her services—bathing and dressing the little Jagannatha Deities every morning and dressing Them at night. I asked her what had happened to big Lord Jagannatha.

“He had a huge crack that we couldn’t fix,” she said. “We placed those Deities in the river without thinking about replacing them. Only later we realized we needed another set to worship. Remember the Jagannatha Deities the kids had in their classroom?”

“Yes,” I said.

“Well, that’s little Lord Jagannatha! There weren’t any other Deities around, so now we are worshiping them,” Nara-devi said.

At first I was horrified, remembering how the kids had played with those Deities on the floor during the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* classes. But before long I accepted that Krishna does whatever He wants, even allowing us to worship Him after children have dressed and undressed Him as though He were a doll.

I got up daily at three-forty, twenty minutes earlier than everyone else, to wake the Deities, with the help of Birsingha dasa. If the *mangal-arati pujari* didn’t show up—and because of their rotating schedule, this happened a couple of times a week—we’d alternate, one of us doing the *arati* ourselves.

The *pujaris* who dressed the Deities had a running contest: Which Deity will steal the show today? Gopijanaballabha dasa brahmacari, our popular temple president, also took part. He dressed Radha-Govinda along with Janardana dasa. After the dressing was complete, we’d run into the temple room to greet the Deities. Enthusiasm was high—all the devotees eager to see the Deities. The altar curtains opened to a tape of Yamuna-devi dasi singing the “*Govindam*” prayers—blasted from loudspeakers—and everyone threw a handful of flowers onto the altar before offering obeisances. Then, leaving the *kirtan*, we *pujaris* regrouped to clean up the *pujari* room and to get a consensus which Deity of the Supreme Personality of Godhead had stolen the show that day. Lord Balarama often won.

Jadurani dasi and Jayadvaita dasa, an editor, dominated the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class schedule. When Jadurani gave a class, it wasn’t possible to remain a wallflower because she called on devotees to recite the appropriate *Bhagavad-gita* slokas that confirmed her points. Macmillan had published the unabridged Gita by then, which included the Sanskrit *slokas*, and I memorized the most important ones. This was satisfying and rewarding—especially if I was called on to quote them in class!

Our New York temple, as always, was a happening place. So many people had joined the movement. And because living outside the temple was considered to be the influence of *maya*, most of the newcomers had moved in. The temple now had so many more residents than when I was last there, and five or six *grhastha* couples lived in nearby apartments.

I often took *prasadam* at the ISKCON Press offices with Jadurani prabhu. While doing oil paintings, she listened to tapes of Srila Prabhupada’s lectures, and during lunch she’d fill my ears with what she’d heard or else tell stories about Srila Prabhupada. My favorite anecdote was this one: She was cleaning the temple floor grudgingly, thinking she’d rather be painting. Srila Prabhupada saw her and said, “Oh, you are cleaning the floor? You are cleaning my heart!”

I finally got the training from Mother Silavati that I’d always wanted. She held *pujari* meetings weekly. She’d explain: “The *pujari* must always be aware of the time. If you are so absorbed in decorating the Deities that you are late, you’ve broken the first principle of Deity worship—punctuality. Cleanliness and sincerity are the other principles.”

“Don’t think you are trimming a Christmas tree,” she said. “The Supreme Lord is dressing Himself, and you are His instrument. Try to be receptive to what He wants. And don’t pin flowers on the Lord in inappropriate places. He’s a person!”

An unofficial, transcendental competition existed between the American temples. Each temple wanted Srila Prabhupada to say its Deity worship was the best or its Deities were the most beautiful or his favorites. The contest especially concerned New York and Los Angeles, because in these temples the main Deities were dressed a second time each afternoon. That kept *pujaris* on their toes. It required more manpower and the sewing of more outfits.

I loved helping other ladies sew elaborate Deity outfits. Besides hand-sewing in the temple room during the morning classes, we sometimes used one or two large rooms in Srila Prabhupada’s empty quarters. Before each of the five or six annual, major Vaisnava holidays that we celebrate, invariably we’d pull off an all-nighter to finish new outfits on time. Our camaraderie in the sewing room inspired girls who helped us sew to join the movement.

I had always been attracted to small Radha-Krishna and asked to bathe and dress Them.

“But who will take care of Lord Jagannatha?” Silavati asked.

“You’re right. I have to take care of Him,” I admitted.

Still, I really wanted to dress Radha-Krishna, and They fulfilled my desire. Occasionally, I would dress Them in the afternoon.

Sometimes my family members visited me, now that I lived nearby. My mother loved the delicious mango-yogurt *prasadam* she ate. When my brother Dave and his wife, Liz, visited, he reminded me: “You first heard the name ‘Krishna’ from me! Remember when Liz and I had cats named Zeus and Krishna? And we had a poster of Maha-Visnu, like the Jimi Hendrix album cover.” Although he dubbed his cats with divine names, he thought back then Krishna was a goddess.

Lincoln and Suzy also visited, now with two young sons, Geoff and Greg, who happily danced in the temple room. Geoff liked chanting Hare Krishna, despite his parents’ disapproval, and chanted all the way home. At one Sunday feast Lincoln and Suzy witnessed a devotee licking up sweet rice spilled on the floor. They never forgot it or comprehended it, no matter what I said.

## Srila Prabhupada’s Visit

When Srila Prabhupada visited New York for a few days in June, 1974, large groups of devotees from temples all over the place welcomed him at the airport, as usual. Seeing Srila Prabhupada enlivened us, and we were so happy to see him.

We raced back from the airport on the bumpy Brooklyn-Queens Expressway (BQE) to greet Srila Prabhupada again as he arrived at the temple, but an axle broke on the old, overloaded car I was in. We were stuck, until some policemen mercifully drove us to our temple at Henry Street. We arrived at eleven p.m., but we were in for another disappointment.

Normally, seeing Srila Prabhupada in his quarters happened only for the leaders—”the big guns,” as Srila Prabhupada called them. For a woman, it was nearly impossible to gain entry, unless you were a temple president’s wife. Therefore, when we returned to the temple that night, I was greatly disappointed to hear that we’d missed a special *darshan* (audience). Srila Prabhupada had invited all the *brahmanas* to come to his room. I was shattered. “My one and only chance to see Srila Prabhupada in his quarters,” I thought, “and I was stuck on the BQE!”

Srila Prabhupada came to greet the Deities and give the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class every morning. One day the Deities wore a purple and white outfit, accented by a fabulous, white-ostrich-feather backdrop. Word spread fast that Srila Prabhupada really liked how the Deities looked. Gariyan dasa, a Press photographer, took photos, and immediately developed the film. By that evening, he’d presented Srila Prabhupada with a framed photograph of the Deities, and Srila Prabhupada was very pleased.

One evening I and some other ladies were blocked from entering the temple room for *arati*. The *brahmacari* guarding the door proclaimed: “Srila Prabhupada said women ruin *kirtans*, so you can’t go in.” He pointed to a sign posted on the door that said the same thing. We were furious with the *brahmacaris* for this stunt. More women gathered. We sat down in the hall and waited while some *grhastha* men went to ask Srila Prabhupada if he was being quoted correctly.

Returning with Srila Prabhupada’s answer, these men said it was a misunderstanding. Harikesa dasa and other *brahmacaris* had complained to Srila Prabhupada earlier that women were getting in the way of the men’s dancing. The *brahmacaris* came out of that meeting with the misunderstanding that Srila Prabhupada had agreed to their idea that women be excluded from the *aratis*. It took only thirty minutes to solve this problem, and in that time we missed the *arati*, but it was a small price to pay for stopping this erroneous “Srila Prabhupada said” from becoming a policy.

## Srila Prabhupada at Pan Am

Bhumi dasi and Sikandini dasi, devotees who distributed books daily at Kennedy Airport, also bathed and dressed Gaura-Nitai. One morning in the *pujari* room, they invited Silavati and me to be part of their special plan for Srila Prabhupada’s departure: We would be in their car, before Srila Prabhupada left; this would enable us to follow his car, and on the way to the airport, after pulling ahead to beat his car there, we’d go to Pan Am’s back entrance—they knew the terminal inside-out from doing *sankirtan* there—and dash through the building, to greet Srila Prabhupada at the front door.

On the morning of Srila Prabhupada’s departure, we four ladies headed to our car early. I saw Bhaktin Miriam (who later became Dhriti dasi). She was holding a big full plastic bag. I said, “Hey, come with us!” She agreed and hopped in. As Srila Prabhupada’s car pulled away, we were right behind him. All the other devotees, who saw Srila Prabhupada off at the curb, were still running to their cars as we took off. We knew they’d be caught in city traffic and never catch up to Srila Prabhupada’s car.

We stayed with Srila Prabhupada’s car through the city and onto the highway, delighted to see the back of his head through the rear window. Someone asked: “Hey, Miriam, what’s in the plastic bag?”

“I don’t know,” she said. “Someone asked me to hold it.” She looked inside and announced, “It’s flower petals.”

Great! We’d shower Srila Prabhupada with flower petals at Pan Am.

We came to the point where we had to pull ahead of Srila Prabhupada’s car to execute our plan. We hesitated: “It’s not right to go ahead of the spiritual master. We should follow in his footsteps.”

However, we had a purpose in mind, and now we had lots of flower petals, which made us extra determined. So we picked up speed to pass. Clearly seeing Srila Prabhupada in the back seat as our cars were side by side, we held up our folded hands and said his *pranam* mantras aloud. It was embarrassing—we passed Srila Prabhupada’s car—but we did it.

Entering into Kennedy Airport’s maze of roads, the *sankirtan* girls easily headed to the Pan Am terminal. Srila Prabhupada’s car was now following us. Not taking the ramp to “Departures,” as Srila Prabhupada’s car would do, we took another route to the rear of the building.

“Oh, no!” our driver said, “Srila Prabhupada’s car is still right behind us! Maybe his driver thought we knew something he didn’t, so he followed us to the back door.”

As Srila Prabhupada got out of the car, we five ladies greeted him by offering our obeisances. Miriam and I, and probably some of the others, grabbed flower petals and tossed them at his lotus feet. Using the rear entrance, Srila Prabhupada had to walk through the whole building to the front lobby—and it was a huge building. Walking backward, Miriam and I showered petals in front of Srila Prabhupada the entire way. Thinking it was nice that Srila Prabhupada had a flower-strewn path to walk on, we feasted our eyes on our beloved spiritual master’s features.

Srila Prabhupada sat down in an airport chair, and the small group of us who accompanied him sat on the floor. We were not surprised that no other devotees had arrived yet.

We were enjoying this moment with Srila Prabhupada, when an angry airport official approached us and demanded: “You threw flower petals all over the floor and made a big mess. You have to clean it up.”

Srila Prabhupada calmly asked him, “You do not like flowers?”

Turning to us, Srila Prabhupada said, “They throw cigarette butts all over the floor, and nobody says anything, but flower petals they do not like.”

We cracked up, appreciating Srila Prabhupada’s keen sense of humor. Needless to say, we didn’t clean up the petals.

## The Paramahamsa Letter

By mail Gopijanavallabha prabhu recommended several devotees for initiation, and Srila Prabhupada’s reply, dated September 19, 1974, contained important instructions that we read again and again:

. . .You must see that they strictly follow the regulative principles, the four prohibitions as well as the devotional practices of arising early, taking morning bath, putting on tilaka, attending mangala arati, chanting japa 16 rounds, and attending Srimad-Bhagavatam class. This is the duty of all my initiated disciples, whether they are big or they are small. Actually in our movement there is no such thing as big or small. Everyone is a devotee. So everyone is expected to follow the devotional practices. Here in Vrindaban I am enjoying my disciples how they are speaking. So two times daily everyone must attend the classes. During the morning and evening times there should be no business, simply kirtana, chant, dance, and be purified. Do not be paramahamsa that now I haven’t got to hear Srimad-Bhagavatam and Bhagavad-gita. Don’t be a better paramahamsa \[topmost, swanlike devotee\] than your Guru Maharaja. I go and sit and I enjoy the lectures and how they are performing kirtana. So as I am doing, you must also do.

Everyone can become a first class speaker. Simply cram the purports of my books. The references are there, the philosophy is there. Everything is there. So if you do it, everyone will be pleased with your speaking. . . .

This letter altered the lives of the New York devotees. We were all regularly attending the morning classes, but now Srila Prabhupada was emphasizing the importance of also attending the evening *Bhagavad-gita* classes: “Don’t be a better *paramahamsa* than your Guru Maharaja.” All the devotees—even *sankirtan* devotees—had to wind up their daily activities, bathe, dress in clean clothes and attend the evening program. Who would want to be accused of trying to be a “better *paramahamsa*” than Srila Prabhupada?

## Lugloos

New York was blazing hot in the fire of book distribution. We were competing against temples our size, especially Los Angeles. Coming back early from *sankirtan* to attend the evening programs would mean a decrease in distribution. Or would it? Gopijanavallabha prabhu figured out a perfect solution: Increase *prasadam* distribution by packing *lugloos* while listening to the now mandatory *Bhagavad-gita* classes.

It was a team effort. The kitchen crew cooked large pots of sweet sticky *lugloos*, chock full of *bundis*, almonds, cashews, raisins and dates. Nanda Kisora, famous in Ocean City for *lugloos*, was one of those in charge. They poured *lugloos* into rectangular stainless steel containers to cool. Each evening, devotees sat around these tubs, many hands shaping the round sweetballs, others slipping them into small plastic bags. The bags were stapled shut with an invitation-and-ingredients label. The devotee lecturing on the *Bhagavad-gita* sat speaking into a microphone, and we kept our talking at a minimum so we could hear the class.

By combining class and *lugloo*-packing, we increased the quantity of *prasadam* distributed—up to ten thousand *lugloos* per week. Srila Prabhupada heard about it:

**Tripurari dasa:** Now in New York they’re distributing a hundred thousand Back to Godheads every month. You saw the *lugloo* from New York?

**Srila Prabhupada:**\[*chuckles*\] Yes.

**Tripurari:**They are making *lugloos* and packaging them in New York, distributing them on the streets with Back to Godhead magazines. Many people come back for more. They like the taste.

**Srila Prabhupada:**And for each *lugloo* there is magazine? If anyone wants *lugloo,* then he has to take one magazine? \[Prabhupada mimics refusing to give a *lugloo*.\] No! \[*Laughter*\] One *lugloo* presentation for one magazine.

To fit the schedules of some temple devotees, Maha-Saturday *Sankirtan* was introduced: a seven-hour day of *sankirtan* weekly, as opposed to one hour a day for seven days. I rarely went out on Maha-Saturday *Sankirtan*. Instead, I stayed back to serve the Deities. But when I did go out to distribute, at a concert or a football game, I consumed a fair share of the *lugloos*. It’s true people asked for them. They were so good, they were more addictive than intoxicants!

## “The Toyota Queen”

Bali Mardan dasa, a temple leader, had given up *sannyasa* to marry Taittiriya dasi,an Oriental lady who claimed to be the heir to the Toyota fortune. Taittiriya dominated the New York temple and had everyone calling her Maharani—”Queen.” If devotees didn’t get along with her, they were kicked out. I didn’t trust Taittiriya and avoided her. She was very critical of devotees. Then things got out of hand. It became obvious to some that she was drastically deviating from the principles of Krishna consciousness. Many devotees complained to Srila Prabhupada, who wrote to Kirtanananda Swami on October 5, 1974:

Recently there has been some provocation in our Society regarding Bali Mardan and his wife. Jayatirtha has received a bunch of complaints. Similarly Brahmananda Maharaja has also received, so far so that his wife is eating chicken in the temple. This has hampered me very much, so I wish to form an investigation committee of three members, namely Tamala Krishna Goswami, Rupanuga Prabhu, and yourself. I am informing them also in this connection with a copy of this letter. So you combine together and investigate about the charges against Bali Mardan and his wife . . .

A few days later Srila Prabhupada wrote to Karandhara dasa in Los Angeles: “. . . I have received complaints against Bali Mardan and his wife, so seriously so much so that the girl has declared that Bali Mardan is an incarnation of Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakur on my name.”

Bali Mardan and Taittiriya left the temple before the investigation committee arrived. A few sentimental devotees cried when they understood that we’d all been duped—she was no Toyota queen—but the vast majority were relieved. We held a big fire sacrifice to purify the temple and danced jubilantly while chanting Hare Krishna. I felt like Dorothy in The Wizard of Oz singing: “The wicked witch is dead!”

One of Taittiriya’s claims was that she was buying a new temple on Fifth Avenue for ISKCON. Srila Prabhupada very much wanted the temple to move to a prestigious Manhattan address, and he had reason to hope Taittreya might help: George Harrison had recently donated a manor outside London. Thus Srila Prabhupada wrote to Gopijanaballabha dasa on December 12, 1974:

I never said that what Taitiriya was doing should be overlooked because of getting the buildings. Anyway where are the buildings? . . . I was tolerating because she was to give the buildings. Bali Mardan said she was this and that, and so I believed. Anyway somehow or other they are out. This is nice. So you all have acted very sensibly. So go on with your Krishna consciousness business. Rupanuga and Kirtanananda Swami I understand are going there, so organize things nicely. We shall try to save Bali Mardan from this fall down. He is a good boy, but he has been misled.

## The Redactron

In 1974 ISKCON Press bought its first phototypesetter, a Redactron that used a magnetic tape for storing information. While Duhkhahantri dasi continued using the IBM composer, Ragatmika dasi and I traveled into Manhattan to Redactron’s offices for a few classes on how to use the new machine. Afterward, Jayadvaita dasa, our department head, trained us further, especially on how to use codes for the diacritical marks on Sanskrit words.

I loved composing on this machine. It was so much easier to use than the IBM composer, even though I had to memorize countless codes—worse than learning DOS, the first computer language. Still, those were dinosaur days—before computers. We could not see what we were going to get because we ended up with undeveloped film. Arcita dasa ran the film through a developing machine that filled an entire darkroom, and thus we got the final proofs.

The first book we composed on the Redactron was Volume One of Madhya-lila, the middle part of the *Caitanya-caritamrta* series. After composing the three volumes of the mostly philosophical Adi-lila, I felt like I was tasting sweeter and sweeter nectar as I composed the amazing pastimes in Madhya-lila. Simultaneously, we composed a small book, *Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers*—a set of conversations in India between Srila Prabhupada and the Peace Corps worker Bob Cohen (who later became Brahma-tirtha dasa).

For the next few months I blissfully composed Srila Prabhupada’s books and continued my service of bathing and dressing the Deities. I looked forward to the next visit from Srila Prabhupada, and that meditation made time fly.

## Chapter 7 - New York—March, 1975

## Srila Prabhupada Arriving at the Airport

Srila Prabhupada’s next visit to New York included a memorable arrival. Not only was he greeted by hundreds of joyful devotees, chanting and dancing, but also Srila Prabhupada stopped to talk with reporters. We all listened. One reporter wanted to pin down exactly when Srila Prabhupada had become God realized. The reporter got more than he bargained for:

**Reporter:**Have you realized the highest truth yourself?

**Srila Prabhupada:**Certainly.

**Reporter:** You have?

**Srila Prabhupada:** Oh, yes.

**Reporter:**At what age did you realize God?

**Srila Prabhupada:** I can deliver to you also.

**Reporter:**Pardon me?

**Srila Prabhupada:** I can deliver to you also if you want.

**Reporter:**My question to you is at what age did you realize God?

**Srila Prabhupada:** God is a person like you and me. The difference is that we are many, and God is one—leader. Now, what is the difference between this one and we many? He maintains all these many, and we are maintained by Him, but He is also a person like you and me. Do you follow?

**Reporter:** Yes, I do, but Swamiji, my question to you was a bit more pointed than that. I appreciate the answer you just gave me, but at what age did you realize the highest truth? At what physical age?

**Srila Prabhupada:**There is no question of age. Realization of God should be educated from the very beginning of life.

**Reporter:**Oh, I understand that, Swamiji. My question to you was at what age did you yourself in this physical incarnation realize the highest truth?

**Srila Prabhupada:**Of course, we were born in a very nice family. My father educated me in this way. So practically from the very beginning of our life we were educated in this way.

**Reporter:**Oh, no, I understand that. I mean at what time did you have your own personal realization, Swamiji? At what age?

**Srila Prabhupada:**Well, that I can say from the age, say, four or five years.

**Reporter:** At the age of four or five years?

**Srila Prabhupada:** Yes. That’s a fact.

Srila Prabhupada was revealing something about himself as a special soul who always remembers Krishna. Here he made one distinction—that he took birth in a very nice family, different from ordinary families. This rare public display of Srila Prabhupada’s enlightened position increased my faith. I felt so proud to be his disciple. Hearing Srila Prabhupada admit to this insistent reporter that he was always Krishna conscious enlivened me very much. So many other gurus promise to enlighten their disciples, but without their having pure love of God, it’s a hollow claim, like saying, “I’ll give you a million dollars.” If you don’t have it, it’s a farce. Srila Prabhupada wasn’t that kind of guru. He was the genuine article, a bona fide spiritual master.

A female reporter asked some typical questions about women’s liberation, and Srila Prabhupada’s answered with a comparison of men’s and women’s brains:

**Reporter:** Are men regarded as superior to women?

**Srila Prabhupada:** Yes, naturally. Naturally, woman requires protection by the man. In the childhood she is protected by the father, and youth time she is protected by the husband, and old age she is protected by elderly sons. That is natural.

**Reporter:**That goes against the thinking of a lot of people in America now. Do you know that?

**Srila Prabhupada:**No. America, maybe, but this is the natural position. Women require protection.

**Reporter:**Who decides what’s natural?

**Srila Prabhupada:** Natural means—just like in psychology, it is said that woman, the highest brain substance of woman is thirty-six ounces, whereas the highest brain substance of man is sixty-four ounces. So there is difference by nature, of the brain.

**Reporter:** Well, to get to something else \[*laughter*\], what do you do for fun when you’re in New York?

**Srila Prabhupada:**What is that?

**Srutakirti dasa:** You have defeated her.

**Reporter:** \[*laughs*\] But I’m not going to pursue your line of logic. And what do you do for fun?

**Srila Prabhupada:**No, that is natural defeat. You cannot avoid it. \[*laughter*\]

**Reporter:** I can’t what?

**Devotee:** You cannot avoid it.

**Reporter:**Again, what do you do to have a good time in New York?

**Srila Prabhupada:** A sixteen-year-old boy can travel all over the world, and it is very difficult for a sixteen-year-old girl to travel all over the world.

**Reporter:**Thank you, Swamiji.

**Srila Prabhupada:** Thank you. Hare Krishna.

**Devotees:**Haribol!

When Srila Prabhupada spoke like this, I didn’t get upset or take it personally. I knew he wasn’t talking about his female disciples. He didn’t put us in the category of mundane women of this material world. Jadurani once told me something Srila Prabhupada said that impressed me deeply: She asked him, “Can women go back to Godhead?” and Srila Prabhupada answered, “Not if they think ‘I am a woman.’”

In the early days in ISKCON we ladies didn’t especially think “I am a woman.” The men and women stood wherever they liked in the temple room during *arati* and *japa*. Both men and women led the *kirtans* and gave the classes. We didn’t mingle freely or talk with each other unless it was related to devotional service, but neither did we dwell on our bodily designations. That wasn’t emphasized.

However, when our leaders changed policies—women standing in the back of the temple room out of sight and not offering full *dandavats*—thinking the women in ISKCON should behave more like their conception of chaste ladies in India, one unfortunate result was that we all became more bodily conscious instead of more Krishna conscious.

Still, Srila Prabhupada encouraged us so much, telling us that we can go back to Godhead in this very life, regardless of our present situation. Women are spirit souls yet have a natural dharma as women. Jadurani once made this point by repeating what Srila Prabhupada said in a lecture: “Men have a big-shoulder mentality. They think, ‘I have big shoulders. I am a big man. Everyone must listen to me!’” Women don’t have a “big-shoulder mentality.” They are naturally subservient. Thus a woman’s body is a blessing in disguise because everyone—men and women—must learn to submit to the spiritual master and Krishna, and women naturally practice this daily.

The same female reporter had more questions:

**Reporter:** Swami, is dressing in this fashion, and playing drums and dancing in the streets, the only way to be spiritual?

**Srila Prabhupada:**No. We have got about sixty books. If you want to learn about this movement through science and philosophy, we have got our books. You have not seen our books? \[*laughter*\]

**Reporter:**Can’t people be spiritual without dressing in this fashion and dancing in the street?

**Srila Prabhupada:**Oh, yes, oh, yes, you can become spiritual in your dress. Simply you have to learn what it is from the books. The dress is not very important thing, but still in the material field this girl is dressed in a different way, you are dressed in a different way.

**Reporter:** The way we dress let’s us move in all circles.

**Srila Prabhupada:** No, the thing is, dress is not very important.

**Reporter:** But you have your disciples dress in this way . . .

**Srila Prabhupada:** But just to draw a particular—just like the policeman, he is differently dressed. One can understand that he is policeman. Similarly, we are also differently dressed so that people may understand we are Hare Krishna people.

**Devotees:** Jaya! Haribol!

“We are Hare Krishna people!” I loved this—we all did—and it really brought a lot of cheering! We were happy to be the Hare Krishna people!

## Arriving at the Temple

After being driven to Henry Street, Srila Prabhupada entered the temple room. Mother Silavati and I, her assistant, were standing on either side of the altar fanning Radha-Govinda and the other Deities with yak tail fans. Srila Prabhupada, followed by hundreds of devotees, offered his obeisances to the Deities. He stood for a while gazing at Them.

Whenever I saw Srila Prabhupada in America it was always for such a short time—one week here, another week there. The time went by quickly, and there were so many other devotees and guests around. And year after year, the policy continued: I and many others were limited to seeing Srila Prabhupada in public. Yet I yearned to be noticed, singled out of the crowd. Perhaps many devotees also wanted this. Therefore they’d dance like crazy or do many special things to attract Srila Prabhupada’s attention.

This occasion was very special to me because that day I wasn’t part of the throng standing behind Srila Prabhupada in the temple room. I was standing on the altar, hoping Srila Prabhupada noticed my service to Radha-Govinda. I hankered to receive his favorable glance.

Srila Prabhupada walked through the crowd to his *vyasasana*. The temple was so packed that Silavati and I, to listen to his arrival address, sat down where we had been standing.

## A Question in the Srimad-Bhagavatam Class

One morning, I asked a question after *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class. It was a bit nerve-wracking to stand up in the crowded temple to ask Srila Prabhupada my question: “Is it Lord Visnu or Lord Krishna who is in our hearts?”

Devotees always said, “Krishna is in your heart.” Yet the painting of the Lord in the heart was of Lord Visnu.

Srila Prabhupada answered, “Krishna is in the area near the heart.” Srila Prabhupada misunderstood my question, because I probably spoke inarticulately, just as I did in 1971, handing him a bouquet at his departure from the New York airport.

## Preparing to Go to India

ISKCON Press was far behind in publishing Srila Prabhupada’s editions of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* and the *Caitanya-caritamrta*. Nevertheless, most of the Press devotees had received permission to attend the second annual, international Gaura Purnima Festival in Mayapur that month. They had been using a little time each day to collect money for their airfares and festival fees.

I was not included because the composers were pushing to catch up with Srila Prabhupada. To make matters worse, Ragatmika prabhu had joined the New Vrindaban *sankirtan* party, leaving me as the only composer on the Redactron. Yet I really wanted to go to India and wasn’t happy I would be left behind.

I was also upset that the Press was scheduled to move when everyone returned from India. It would relocate to Los Angeles. Ramesvara dasa, newly in charge, was based there and had found a better building than the one we used in New York. I wasn’t at all enthusiastic to move back there.

Somehow I convinced Radhaballabha prabhu, the production manager, to let me go to India. He said, “Don’t stop composing; the other Press devotees will collect your fare. You keep composing until the last minute.”

Shortly before I left for India, Balai dasi arrived, ready to learn the Redactron. She was one of the first ladies to join ISKCON in New York, and one of the first composers on the IBM machine. Already familiar with the dynamics of composing, she quickly picked up the use of the Redactron well enough to compose Srila Prabhupada’s books while I was away.

When the long-awaited day of departure came, I was prepared. I had a hundred dollars spending money, which my father graciously gave me when I made a rare visit to his office. Before leaving the temple I took a long shower because we would be traveling for the next two days.

Devotees from all over the country were checking in at the airport. Then they gathered in a lounge where Satsvarupa Maharaja was answering questions. I asked the same question I had put to Srila Prabhupada, this time speaking articulately: “Who is in our hearts—Krishna or Visnu?”

Satsvarupa Maharaja said, “Visnu is in the heart of every living entity, but when we become devotees, then Krishna manifests Himself in our hearts. And when we become pure devotees, both Radha and Krishna will manifest in our hearts.”

I was satisfied with this answer.

As I got on the huge airplane, I put aside all thoughts about composing or moving the Press and simply wondered, “What is India like?”

## Part Three: Śrīla Prabhupāda in India

## Chapter 8 - The Gaura Purnima Festival in 1975

## March, 1975

Carrying boxed prasadam enroute to India, we devotees from the United States traveled together on Air India, *stopping* at London’s Heathrow Airport, where more devotees joined us and raised our number to about three hundred. We landed in Bombay, but didn’t see more than the airport. Its slow-moving fans suspended from high ceilings epitomized my conception of India.

Several buses met our Bombay-to-Calcutta flight. As we endured delays, I was appalled at the number of children begging and clamoring around. Our left-over *prasadam* delighted the dirty kids.

As we headed out of the city, I felt even more dismayed seeing people who lived on traffic islands and bathed in gutters. Soon we were in the green, lush Bengali countryside, headed for Mayapur. People in the country were bathing at *pukkurs* (ponds) and washing their clothes. I thought, “Why don’t the people lured to cities by factory work return to their villages, where they can be clean, living near a *pukkur* and growing their own food?” Srila Prabhupada’s phrase, “You can’t eat nuts and bolts,” really made sense to me.

Although usually I was not openly emotional, as we got closer to Mayapur I choked up. On the verge of tears while watching the beautiful countryside from the bus window, I thought, “Soon I’ll finally be in Sridhama Mayapur, Lord Caitanya’s birthplace. It probably looks much the same as it did when He appeared there and started the Hare Krishna movement.” I tried to hide my emotions, but I became overwhelmed when someone shouted, “Look! Mayapur!” and I finally saw the saffron-colored Lotus Building across the rice fields. ISKCON Mayapur! It was like love at first sight, and I wanted to stay forever. A few weeks weren’t going to be enough.

The best part of our arrival in Mayapur was to greet and receive the person who brought us all there, Srila Prabhupada. I joined the waiting crowd soon after settling into the ladies’ simple quarters above the Big Kitchen; the men took rooms along the boundary wall. We all gathered at the front gate to chant Hare Krishna while awaiting his arrival. More buses arrived, with another hundred devotees, and these devotees from various parts of the world also joined our *kirtan*. Our international gathering of devotees, most of us first-timers in India, enthusiastically danced and chanted for a long time in the hot sunlight. Finally, we saw a speck on the road coming nearer and nearer. It was Srila Prabhupada’s car! Srila Prabhupada was coming! I started jumping and happily chanting louder. The car pulled up and stopped. Doors opened, and Srila Prabhupada gracefully emerged. The Gaura Purnima festival officially began with Srila Prabhupada’s arrival at the front gate.

## ISKCON Mayapur

ISKCON had many hectares of land but few buildings. The very first structure in Mayapur, originally Srila Prabhupada’s thatched-roofed hut, stood just inside the only partly constructed, but nevertheless functional front gate. An unaesthetic *godown* (storage room) faced the hut. A boundary wall ran parallel to the road-footage of the ISKCON property and served more purposes than just security. Small rooms along its entire length were used for housing, *godowns* and work rooms.

Inside the gate, walking to my right for two minutes, I found the *prasadam* pavilion, which held five hundred people at a sitting. It adjoined the Big Kitchen, where cooks prepared daily meals for the residents and guests, and free meals of nutritious *kittri* on weekends for the hungry—Mayapur’s Food for Life program.

Walking straight along the main path from the front gate, I came to the Lotus Building. This contained the temple room and a guesthouse. Saurabha dasa, a Dutch architect, had designed it, and Srila Prabhupada made some vital changes, including wide verandahs around each upper floor of the building. It was painted saffron, and lotus flowers in bas-relief adorned both sides of the many sculpted arches along the wrap-around verandahs. The building effectively caught Mayapur’s cooling breezes.

The ground floor housed the temple room for the presiding Deities, Sri Sri Radha-Madhava, beautiful medium-size brass Deities, and a slightly smaller Lord Caitanya, also brass, all standing on a large, silver lotus flower on the altar.

The GBC meeting room was on the first floor of the Lotus Building, as was the Deity kitchen. The second and third floors contained guestrooms and Srila Prabhupada’s quarters, and the flat roof was partially covered by two rooms occupied by the co-directors of Mayapur, Jayapataka and Bhavananda Maharajas.

Beyond this, there were only rice fields, flower gardens, banana groves, a *pukkur* and other fields and trees. Several cows were kept at the temple *goshalla* (cow shed). Mayapur was rural. Outside the gate were just more temples and ashrams.

The holy Ganga (Ganges River) flowed powerfully past Mayapur. Lord Caitanya had bathed in her purifying waters, and following in His footsteps, I took my *snana* (bath) there. To reach the Ganga I needed to walk for twenty minutes along narrow, raised dirt paths between rice and jute fields.

The confluence of the Ganga and the Jalangi (Sarasvati River) was at Hular Ghat, a boat-dock down the road from ISKCON. Coming together at right angles, the green Jalangi waters gently mixed with the faster brownish waters of the Ganga.

Mayapur is a holy *dhama*, a special abode of the Lord, so special that I felt it was another planet—in the spiritual world.

## The Gaura Purnima Festival

Every morning Srila Prabhupada took his morning walk around the compound accompanied by *sannyasis* and senior men, and often Visakha dasi went along to take photographs. Returning to the temple just in time to greet the Deities, Srila Prabhupada offered obeisances to Sri Sri Radha-Madhava and Lord Caitanya when the curtains opened, and everyone else also fell flat on the marble floor. Standing up, he gazed lovingly at the Deities while the “*Govindam*” prayers played over the speakers.

After again offering obeisances, Srila Prabhupada rang a bell that hung to the side of the altar, then walked behind the altar room, and emerged on the other side, where he rang another bell. Next, he walked down the length of the temple room, behind the *vyasasana*, then back to the altar—a complete circumambulation—always followed by the lead singer, the devotees playing *mrdangas* and *kartals*, and the horde of devotees.

I liked to stand on the sidelines to watch the fun as Srila Prabhupada came around the corner from behind the altar, where he stopped to ring the bell. The *kirtan* was roaring, and everyone danced around Srila Prabhupada.

Occasionally he rang the bell more than the usual three times, and we’d really go wild. The *kirtan* would reach an even higher point, with so many devotees, including me, leaping into the air. Srila Prabhupada encouraged us by waving his free hand upward: “Jump! Jump higher!” This increased the ecstasy even more! Feeling the loving reciprocation between our beloved spiritual master and us, his spiritual children, we used all our youthful energy to dance and jump high to catch his eye and please him. Smiling, he continued walking, letting go of the bell’s cord, and many devotees competed to catch it.

After circumambulating three times, Srila Prabhupada sat down on his *vyasasana*, and the mood became more grave. A senior devotee offered him *guru-puja*, while we sang the *Guru-vandana* prayers and offered flowers.

After *kirtan*, Srila Prabhupada gave class, but he lectured on the first verses of the *Caitanya-caritamrta* instead of the *Bhagavatam*. It was appropriate that we understood the glories of Lord Caitanya while visiting Mayapur, His birthplace. Because we are preaching widely, Srila Prabhupada explained, our ISKCON temple is Lord Caitanya’s *karmastana*, or place of activities. It is even more important than the Yogapitha, His *janmastana* (birthplace). Lord Caitanya predicted that from Mayapur, the birthplace of the *sankirtan* movement, the chanting of the holy names would spread to every town and village in the world.

One morning while lecturing about Lord Caitanya, Srila Prabhupada suddenly stopped speaking. He paused a long time, spiritually entranced. A devotee started singing the Hare Krishna mantra. Others joined in, and Srila Prabhupada returned to external consciousness. We felt privileged to see his highly advanced state of spiritual absorption.

No one wanted to miss Srila Prabhupada’s classes, but the resident Mayapur devotees required help in the Big Kitchen to prepare the breakfasts. Following a rotating schedule, some of the visiting ladies had to miss classes to cut fruits. It was sad enough missing a class, but the craziest thing was that we all preferred to eat whole fruits. Devotees who had attended the previous year’s festival warned us not to eat cut fruits if we wanted to stay healthy, because fruits go bad quickly, especially in the heat. Or flies may have landed on them, or the knives and bowls may have been unclean or contaminated. (With *subjees* you also have these problems, but *subjees* are later cooked.) Despite our objections, the kitchen manager insisted on fruit salad, so we went along with him.

We weren’t the only ones who had to perform such duties: The visiting men followed a rotating schedule to maintain a twenty-four-hour *kirtan* in the temple. For some, that required sleepless nights.

The ladies from New York—Sunita, Alarka, Bhumi, Sikhandini and I—shared a room above the Big Kitchen. It was so hot that we slept on the roof. One night we woke up to a huge windstorm! We had to move into our room, but it was too hot to sleep. One day I discovered why it was so hot in our room: it was directly above the huge mud-and-cow-dung-covered cooking stoves, which radiated heat day or night.

The majority of devotees attending the festival were Americans, representing nearly every American temple. Groups of devotees also came from South America, Australia, Europe and other locations. I particularly remember the ladies from Germany and Australia because there were really hard working. While the rest of us were flat on our backs, suffering from the extreme heat and humidity, they seemed impervious and volunteered to do various services. They really ran circles around us Americans.

In the evenings we were entertained on a temporary bamboo stage set up in the rice field between the temple and the main gate. Lohitaksa dasa and his wife, Rasa-jna dasi, headed a drama-and-dance troupe from Los Angeles that performed *bharat-natyam* dance and dramas depicting Lord Krishna’s pastimes.

## Cleaning Srila Prabhupada’s Quarters

While Srila Prabhupada took his morning walk, a small group of ladies assisted Srimati dasi, an American living in Mayapur, in cleaning Srila Prabhupada’s rooms. One morning I clearly saw Srila Prabhupada’s mustard-oil-marked footprints near the doorway between his rooms. We took turns reverently offering obeisances and placing our heads directly on his footprints. I didn’t want to wash them away, but duty forced me to clean the floor.

On the floor by Srila Prabhupada’s low desk stood a large glass jar with a lid, which sometimes contained sweetballs made from fresh, grated coconut and *gur* (dehydrated sugar-cane juice). They were for Srila Prabhupada’s guests. If any were left from the previous evening, Srimati rewarded each of us with one. These Bengali sweets were extremely delicious!

## Parikramas

Every day leading to Lord Caitanya’s appearance day, I joined the devotees going on *parikrama* (pilgrimage). We left after breakfast and returned by two or three, in time for lunch.

When we visited the nearby holy places, our colorful group carried flags on bamboo poles, and we streamed out the main gate together, the *sannyasis* in front, our *kirtan* party following, its lead singer accompanied by many *kartals* and *mrdangas*. The holy names were our international language.

Walking down Bhaktisiddhanta Marg, we went to the temple at the Yogapitha, where we circumambulated the neem tree under which Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu appeared. After offering respects to the many Deities on the three altars, we chanted for a long time: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.

It felt to me as though the five hundred years that had elapsed since Lord Caitanya’s appearance were just like one day. When *sannyasis* and leading devotees told the pastimes that happened right there, I felt that our activities now were part of Lord Caitanya’s activities then.

Walking further down the road, we stopped at Srivasa Angam, Srivasa’s home, where the Lord performed His nocturnal pastimes of privately chanting Hare Krishna in *kirtan*, before He brought chanting to the streets. The homes of Advaita Acarya and Gadadhara Pandita, two of Lord Caitanya’s principal associates, were in the same compound. A bit off the road we visited the house of Murari Gupta, another associate of Lord Caitanya.

The furthest point we went was Caitanya Matha, the Mayapur home of Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakur, Srila Prabhupada’s guru. His *samadhi* (tomb) was there, beside a temple housing Sri Sri Gandharvika-Giridhari, his Deities, and so was the *samadhi* of Gaura Kisora dasa Babaji, his guru.

Mayapur is nondifferent from Vrindaban, Lord Krishna’s place of childhood pastimes. Thus Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, with his transcendental vision, had seen from his house at Caitanya Matha several holy places of Vrindaban: Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda, two sacred lakes, and Govardhana, a rocky hill that Lord Krishna held up like an umbrella with His left pinkie to protect the residents of Vrindaban from devastating storms. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta’s disciples excavated the *kundas* and built the Govardhana that we saw there.

Sometimes the *parikramas* went to more distant places by bus. Other days, to see the many temples in Navadvipa, we crossed the Ganga in boats. Boatmen pushing long bamboo poles into the water moved the boats far upstream close to shore before our crossing. This offset the pull of the current as we traversed the river. It seemed like an art how they always landed exactly at the boat ghat.

One day we crossed the Jalangi to visit the house and *samadhi* of Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati’s father. We were allowed to go inside and stand on the upper verandah of the house, which overlooks the Jalangi. In the early years of the twentieth century, Bhaktivinode Thakur stood on this verandah and predicted: “People from all over the world will congregate in Sridhama Mayapur to chant ‘Jaya Sacinandana! Gaura Hari!’” Our *kirtan* included these words glorifying the names of Lord Caitanya, and thus we fulfilled the Thakur’s prophecy, just as ISKCON devotees had done the previous year during the festival. It was no coincidence that straight across the river stood our ISKCON temple.

## My Personal Kartals

During free time one day, I went shopping in Navadvipa, which had a certain mystical atmosphere. I’d heard a story, alleged to be true, about a devotee who’d gone to Navadvipa and eaten a cursed *rasagula*, a round milk sweet, which made him sick; then a yogi removed the *rasagula*—still whole—through his navel. True or not, the story set a mood about the extraordinary nature of Navadvipa.

I wanted to buy a pair of brass *kartals*. Seeing devotees occasionally handing their own *kartals* to Srila Prabhupada to play, I also desired to have Srila Prabhupada play mine. Arriving at the brass shop, I found a devotee couple, Uttama-sloka dasa and his wife, shopping there. Uttama-sloka prabhu helped me select *kartals*, hitting various pairs of the small brass cymbals gently together to hear their rings until he found a pair with a sweet sound. The brass-walla (shopkeeper) engraved my name on them.

I awaited the opportunity to hand my *kartals* to Srila Prabhupada. I watched him very carefully play his own. He wrapped the cord around his index fingers and played the traditional one-two-thrrreee rhythm by pressing his thumbs against the *kartals* and then releasing them to let them vibrate on the third beat. He kept his other three fingers on each hand outstretched straight, raising and lowering them just like his thumbs.

After watching Srila Prabhupada play for some time I was able to imitate him and thus learned how to play the *kartals* and make them sound like bells.

Noticing that sometimes Srila Prabhupada didn’t bring his *kartals* for *guru-puja*, I placed my pair on the *vyasasana* cushion one morning. I hoped he wouldn’t have his own to play. Sure enough, he picked up my *kartals* and played them during *guru-puja*. I was in bliss!

## Pisima, Srila Prabhupada’s Sister

We called Srila Prabhupada’s sister, younger than him by three years, Pisima (meaning “Auntie,” or the father’s sister). She arrived in Mayapur shortly after Srila Prabhupada. Her face resembled her brother’s—a female version of him. She was shorter than Srila Prabhupada—even shorter than me!—and was very round, the sign of a good cook. And Pisima was famous for her Bengali cooking.

Of all Srila Prabhupada’s immediate family members, only Pisima respectfully accepted him as *jagat-guru*, the guru of the universe, as totally as his disciples did.

I was so thrilled to meet Pisima. She stayed on the first-floor verandah of the Lotus Building because all the rooms were filled with life members. I was appalled that she was sleeping there, on a mattress on the floor, but she was content to be there, near the ladies’ bathroom, because it was hard for her to walk. She wasn’t in good health.

One evening a sizable group of ladies and I joined her on the verandah for *bhajans*. Pisima had a sweet, childlike voice and expressed a desire to sing “*Manasa Deha Geha*,” her favorite *bhajan*. We became so absorbed in singing that we missed the evening *arati*. After one or two more days of our following the same schedule, Srila Prabhupada came to hear about it and asked us to stop the *bhajan* on time, so that we would not miss the *arati*. Whoever delivered this message said a few other things about Pisima that Srila Prabhupada supposedly said, and we got the point that he didn’t want us to make too big a fuss about her.

We then heard something informal: that Srila Prabhupada engaged in brotherly teasing, telling Pisima that she was fat, and that she was so good-natured that she laughed with him!

We also heard a story about how incredibly chaste Pisima had been to her late husband, who was in many ways a rascal: She never let her guests know why her husband wasn’t home, but she knew he was out gallivanting around.

Srila Prabhupada said that Pisima was not only his sister, but also his Godsister because she also took first initiation from Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakur.

However, when she asked Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati for second initiation, he asked her, “Do you follow the regulative principles—no meat-eating, no intoxication, no illicit sex, no gambling?”

She answered, “Yes,” and voluntarily added, “but my husband doesn’t,” which resulted in Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati’s refusing her second initiation. Not long after that, he left this world, and Pisima took her brahminical initiation from Bhaktisaranga Swami, her Godbrother, and thereafter considered him her guru and considered his disciples her Godbrothers and Godsisters. Only because Srila Prabhupada told some devotees did we find out about her first guru.

Pisima was so humble that she came to hear Srila Prabhupada’s classes, though she didn’t understand English. Once, she tried to communicate in Hindi with Cinmayi dasi, whose long black hair made Pisima think she was Indian. Lokamangala dasa experienced the same confusion because he also looked Indian, being half black.

*Gayatri* dasi was her personal servant, and carried Pisima’s famous pint-sized bottle of Ganga *jal*, Ganga water. Pisima doused anyone and everyone with handfuls of this water, especially putting it on our beadbags.

Unbeknownst to Srila Prabhupada, some of the ladies took up a collection to pay for Pisima’s train fare to Vrindaban so that she could attend the second portion of the festival. Srila Prabhupada came to know about it only when he saw Pisima in Vrindaban. He wasn’t pleased that we’d paid for her ticket, but by then it was too late.

## Gaura Purnima Day

On Gaura Purnima day, starting early in the morning, many thousands of pilgrims poured into Mayapur. For the devotees, it was a fast day, until evening.

An announcement was made right after *mangal-arati*: “There’s a huge parade in Calcutta today for Holi, which the local people celebrate on the same day as Gaura Purnima. Srila Prabhupada wants ISKCON represented at this parade. A bus is leaving for Calcutta right now, returning tonight in time for the feast. Who will volunteer to go?”

I raised my hand, thinking, “I’m going to have a hard time just hearing and chanting all day. I’d better stay active.”

The only problem was that a thief had stolen my shoes the previous day. Dinanatha dasa, the organizer, assured me I could get another pair in Calcutta.

Off we went on the bus. Arriving in Calcutta, I found that the whole city was shut down for the holiday and parade. So I had to walk the streets of Calcutta barefoot.

We chanted Hare Krishna and danced before huge crowds. We had no float, no Deities, no banners or flags. Spotting another group’s *murtis* of Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityananda, we situated ourselves directly behind them.

Guess who missed the feast that day? Yes, we returned long after dark, and the feast was entirely consumed. Someone found a few puris, and that was our feast.

Being very exhausted, I didn’t sufficiently scrub the filth of Calcutta off my feet, and as a result I was very sick the next day. For two days I had diarrhea, and I was running from the ashram to the round bathroom next door without caring one bit who saw me in my nightgown. This was a good lesson on the importance of being revolutionary clean, especially in India.

## Leaving Mayapur

The Mayapur segment of the festival ended with a big feast on the day after Gaura Purnima. As I packed, I realized how much I liked Mayapur and wanted to stay. “Ever since I arrived,” I thought, “I’ve felt a deeper identification with being a devotee of Krishna.”

After I told Vrindaban-based Visalini dasi about my desire, she said, “Wait till you see Vrindaban! I’ll bet you’ll want to stay there even more!”

I said, “Maybe I will. But I really love Mayapur.”

During a big *kirtan*, Srila Prabhupada left Mayapur for Calcutta. He was flying to Hyderabad in a few days, where a *pandal* (tent) program was scheduled. While about two hundred and fifty devotees went to Vrindaban, I was among the hundred and fifty who chose to go to Hyderabad, by train.

## Chapter 9 - A Week in Hyderabad—March, 1975

## Traveling to Hyderabad

Our train ride to Hyderabad was memorable. The devotees managed the reservations in such a way that we fully occupied two whole *bogis* (train cars). Bhagavat dasa arranged for our *prasadam*, and we did not starve. We had lots of *samosas* (vegetable pastries) at every meal. The best part of our journey was having the association of Dina Bandhu dasa and Nitai dasa. They (and their wives) sat in the ladies’ section and entertained us with story after story about Krishna.

Nitai prabhu was the Sanskrit editor who normally traveled with Srila Prabhupada, but on this trip he traveled with us because his wife had come to the festival. He told us not only about Krishna but also about his experiences with Srila Prabhupada. Dina Bandhu prabhu was a gifted storyteller. We became so absorbed in their talks that we didn’t mind the excessive heat or the rain of soot pouring in the windows from the coal-burning engine. We tolerated the austerities of the two-night-long ride. When they tired of talking, we’d have a *kirtan*. Then we’d go back to the stories. As many girls as could fit on the top bunks and in the hall crowded around the whole time to hear all the nectar.

## The Hyderabad Festival

After putting our luggage in a *dharmasala*, a simple guesthouse, we went to the airport to greet Srila Prabhupada. *Kirtan* was going strong when he arrived. Sitting down in the terminal, Srila Prabhupada chanted Hare Krishna with us and clapped his hands.

“Srila Prabhupada doesn’t have his *kartals*,” I noticed. I passed my pair to his servant, and he gave them to Srila Prabhupada. I was delighted!

When Srila Prabhupada finished playing them, he looked around as if to ask, “Whose are these?” I was too far back in the crowd to claim them, so he passed the *kartals* to someone, and I swiftly got them back before they disappeared.

The temple in Hyderabad was small, but housed six-foot-tall, white marble Deities—Sri Sri Radha–Madana-Mohana. Mr. G. Pulla Reddy, a wealthy life member, had donated a good-size parcel of land just behind the temple, where construction of a larger temple was just beginning. Mr. Reddy’s sweet shop stood just across the street from our temple. He advertised that his sweets contained pure cow’s milk and ghee.

Our evening *pandal* programs took place in a large grassy park. Each night, after a *kirtan* welcoming Srila Prabhupada, we would all sit on the stage with him as he spoke.

One night when Srila Prabhupada asked for questions after his talk, a man said in a challenging mood: “Swami So-and-So can do miracles. Can you show me a miracle?” The swami referred to could produce ashes or *rasagulas* (sweets) and give them to people.

“This is my miracle!” Srila Prabhupada said, majestically gesturing towards the hundred and fifty of us Western devotees on the stage. “I made these *mlecchas* \[low-born meat-eaters\] into Vaisnavas!”

We cheered enthusiastically: “Haribol! Jaya, Srila Prabhupada!”

Srila Prabhupada had changed so many people’s hearts and lives—from hippies to “happies.” I felt very happy to be part of Srila Prabhupada’s miracle.

Actually, there was no comparison between Srila Prabhupada’s miracle—the result of his true purity—and the antics of a mystic with either something up his sleeve or some power of *prapti siddhi,* the material ability to produce something. Srila Prabhupada’s miracle was unique: convincing thousands to chant the holy names of God and to turn away from sinful life—illicit sex, meat-eating, intoxication and gambling. Srila Prabhupada succeeded because of his unalloyed devotion to Lord Krishna and his steadfast determination to follow the orders of his guru. Therefore we said in complete truthfulness: “All glories to Srila Prabhupada!”

## A Feast at Mr. Reddy’s Home

Mr. Reddy invited Srila Prabhupada and his followers to a daytime program at his house. First, we did *harinam sankirtan* through the streets of Hyderabad, then we walked to his house. Mr. Reddy’s house was in a hilly area overlooking the city. By the time we got there I was exhausted from walking uphill in the heat, and I rested under a *pandal* tent, thoughtfully erected on the lawn for shade.

Srila Prabhupada arrived by car and was nicely received by Mr. Reddy, his relatives and his invited guests. We all sat under the *pandal* to hear Srila Prabhupada speak.

Then we took a mid-day feast. Srila Prabhupada took *prasadam* inside the house with our hosts. The devotees sat in lines outside. To feed the two hundred or so people, Mr. Reddy had hired professional cooks and set up an outdoor kitchen. Servants brought us wonderful *prasadam*, including hot puris right out of the ghee. We had never been served like this!

After serving Srila Prabhupada inside, Mr. Reddy came out to ask, “Is everyone satisfied? Do you want anything more?”

“We want more sweets,” somebody said.

“Of course! I’ll send my servant down to the sweet shop to get some more.”

Other devotees tried to intervene: “No, no! We’re satisfied! Please don’t go to any trouble for us!”

But our impeccable host insisted: “No, no! It’s no trouble at all!”

The servant was soon biking down the hill to Mr. Reddy’s sweet shop—the best in town. Half an hour later he was back with two big pots strapped to the back of his bike, one pot filled with *rasagulas*, the other with *gulubjamans*. These were unforgettable sweets, unlike anything I’d ever tasted. Compared with these spongy, juicy *gulabjamuns*, American *gulabjamuns* had an inferior texture, probably because they were made from powdered milk. The orange-flavored *rasagulas* were also special. We ate every single sweet.

And not only that, the men had a juice-drinking contest and polished off all the juice in which the sweets had been soaked. We became so intoxicated that some devotees were laughing uncontrollably and rolling on the lawn. There was no way we could walk back down the hill after eating so much. We had to go back by hired buses.

## Our Program at the Hyderabad Farm

One morning Srila Prabhupada traveled by car to the Hyderabad farm, a large piece of undeveloped land that had potential. I reluctantly went with the devotees by bus. I didn’t want to go because I’d had a sleepless night—red ants attacked me. At the same time I did not want to be alone all day at the *dharmasala*.

Local villagers came streaming into our farm on foot from all directions. All the devotees and villagers then went off to an inauguration ceremony for a new railway station nearby.

Getting permission to stay back to rest, I was shown to a room in a narrow building consisting of four rooms in a row with an open verandah.

Once I lay down, I heard voices through the wall. I was right next to Srila Prabhupada! I couldn’t hear the words, but Srila Prabhupada was speaking with Hansaduta dasa and a few others whose voices I couldn’t recognize. I rested, but it was impossible to fall asleep while relishing hearing Srila Prabhupada’s voice.

When all the devotees and villagers came back, Srila Prabhupada sat under a large tree and gave a talk. Afterwards, everyone sat in rows on the ground to take *prasadam*. It was prepared by hired cooks we’d brought from the city.

Later, Palika prabhu asked the ladies, “Does anyone want to go swimming? Vrndavani and I know a great place!” About a dozen ladies wanted to go—the bunch of us from New York, Jagaddhatri dasi from Los Angeles, and others.

We were told to be back on time to catch one of our buses back to the city.

It’s quite possible we were late, or maybe our buses left early, but when we returned, we found the buses had left without us. Just the kitchen crew still there, packing huge woks and other cooking paraphernalia.

“You can return on the back of the kitchen truck,” someone said, “if you sit on top of the paraphernalia in the open.”

“We don’t mind. There is no other way back to Hyderabad.”

While we were waiting for the truck, Srila Prabhupada came out of his room, ready to go back to the city in his car. Seeing us offering our obeisances, he approached us and asked, “Why are you still here?”

“We missed the bus, Srila Prabhupada.”

“What are you going to do? How will you get back?” he asked.

“We’re going to ride in the truck when they finish packing.”

Knowing that most of us were in India for the first time, Srila Prabhupada asked, “How do you like India? What do you think?”

Alarka prabhu, standing closest to Srila Prabhupada, answered, “We like India very much!”

All of us were in ecstasy to have this conversation with our guru. I was touched that he showed fatherly concern about our ride back. And then he presented himself like a gentlemanly host, wondering if we liked India. It was a sweet exchange.

During the whole Hyderabad festival we saw Srila Prabhupada much more often than we did in America. He was more accessible and obviously more relaxed in India than in America. After spending a week in Hyderabad, we devotees traveled by train to Vrindaban, and Srila Prabhupada flew to Delhi and went on to Vrindaban.

## Chapter 10 - The Vrindaban Temple—April, 1975

## The Grand Opening of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir

Our incomplete Krishna-Balaram Mandir in Vrindaban was scheduled to open in 1974, but the grand opening had been postponed. Finally, on Rama Naumi, the auspicious appearance anniversary of Krishna’s incarnation as Lord Ramacandra, the temple opened.

The Vedic style of the pillared temple, with its black-and-white-checkered marble floor and a central courtyard open to the air was architecturally wonderful. Behind the temple, the International Guesthouse also opened—so modern that it featured running water in every room, a first for Vrindaban. Srila Prabhupada’s house was nestled between the two buildings.

The parcel of land was donated to Srila Prabhupada in 1972. It was on the outskirts of the holy city of Vrindaban, where Lord Krishna tended cows five thousand years ago, and there were hardly any ashrams or buildings in any direction. Venturing outside our gate, I saw one or two rickshaw *wallas* (drivers) looking for passengers. There were no shops, no beggars, few people and little traffic.

Because our temple was in Raman Reti, where Krishna played in the sands with His friends, Srila Prabhupada decided to install Krishna-Balaram Deities on the central altar. As he wrote in *Caitanya-caritamrta (Madhya* 16.281), “We are establishing a Krishna-Balaram temple to broadcast to the world that worship of Gaura Nitai is the same as worship of Krishna-Balaram.”

Lord Balaram, Krishna’s older brother, was carved from white marble. His left hand draped over the right shoulder of black-marbled Lord Krishna. Krishna stood jauntily with His left hand on His hip.

The left altar held medium-size marble *murtis* of Srila Prabhupada and Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati sitting cross-legged. Large white-marble Gaura-Nitai (Caitanya-Nityananda) Deities stood behind the gurus.

On the right altar were the absolutely stunning divine couple, Sri Sri Radha-Syamasundara, and Lalita and Visakha, two gopis (cowherd girlfriends), on either side.

Installing the Deities took several days and included ceremonies conducted by several South Indian *brahmanas* of impressive proportions. We witnessed the blindfolding of the Deities, the opening of Their eyes and a bathing ceremony (*abhiseka*). Finally, the Deities were dressed in intricately decorated outfits designed for the occasion by the French *pujari* Omkara dasa. Each piece of the outfit was a different color, and the colors on each altar combined exquisitely.

I watched Srila Prabhupada perform the first *arati* for Sri Sri Krishna-Balaram, along with hundreds of devotees and guests packed into the temple. As he finished offering each article to the Deities, Srila Prabhupada turned around to offer it to the devotees. We saw the most beautiful, satisfied smile on his face. As the *arati* progressed, his smile grew bigger and bigger! Fortunately, Visakha dasi was persistent enough to situate herself in front of the altar with her camera and capture this remarkable *arati*.

After this, we listened to many guest speakers, all speaking Hindi. After each speaker Srila Prabhupada said to us, “Don’t be disappointed! Don’t be disappointed!” I was wondering: “Disappointed about what?” I figured the speakers may have been criticizing us in some way; perhaps they didn’t accept us as Vaisnavas. But Srila Prabhupada didn’t care. He was very proud of his Western disciples, whom he called his “dancing white elephants.”

Actually, Srila Prabhupada adapted the term “white elephants” to his own use. Formerly, he once explained, only kings and rich men maintained elephants, and therefore in India, a hundred years ago, when students began going to England and taking English wives, people would say, “This man is maintaining one white elephant,” because a European wife meant very much expenditure. When Srila Prabhupada brought white, Western devotees to India in the early seventies, he called them his dancing white elephants.

Because Srila Prabhupada preached to us Westerners, who knew nothing about Krishna—but who also had no philosophical misconceptions about Him, we were able to give up our sinful activities and embrace Krishna consciousness wholeheartedly. Previously, Srila Prabhupada had tried to spread Krishna consciousness in India by producing Back to Godhead and by forming the League of Devotees, but he was unsuccessful. Although Lord Krishna’s holy footprints had touched India, by the 1950’s the Indian people were so steeped in philosophical misconceptions and burgeoning materialism that they misunderstood Krishna’s message.

When Srila Prabhupada’s Western disciples danced and chanted Hare Krishna at *pandal* programs in major Indian cities, then and only then was the Indian public impressed with Srila Prabhupada’s offer of Krishna consciousness. His dancing white elephants soon had Indians feeling guilty that Westerners were following their culture better than they were! Here in Vrindaban, the local people were not yet sure about us being bona fide Vaisnavas; however, Srila Prabhupada told us not to be disappointed. He accepted us, and that’s all that mattered.

The next part of the festivities was an initiation ceremony in the sunken courtyard. Srila Prabhupada officiated, and devotees from around the world recited their vows and became his disciples.

Late one afternoon Srila Prabhupada led a *parikrama*. It went out the front gates and turned left. We walked to Vrindaban, the city of five thousand temples—hundreds of chanting, dancing devotees and our beloved guru. We visited the seven main Vaisnava temples and a few others and offered our respects. At dusk I was walking down the narrow streets next to Palika prabhu when suddenly a boy yanked a gold chain off her wrist and ran away so quickly that by the time she cried, “Thief! Thief!” the child had disappeared. She accepted the loss, knowing it was futile to search for the thief.

By the time the *parikrama* returned to Krishna-Balaram Mandir it was dark.

## Group Photograph with Srila Prabhupada

As we’d done in Mayapur, we went on daily tours to major holy places of Krishna’s pastimes: Govardhana Hill, Radha-kunda, Varsana, Nandagram and more.

I requested to do a personal service for Srila Prabhupada, and I got to iron his clothes in the *pujari* room, using the Deities’ iron, the only one available.

One morning an announcement was made that at a certain time—right during my ironing time—everyone would gather in front of the temple for a group photograph with Srila Prabhupada.

I thought I could do both.
