While ironing Srila Prabhupada’s cloth, I would hear a rise in the intensity of the *kirtan* when Srila Prabhupada arrived—and know the photo session was starting—and then I would join them. I was blissfully ironing, listening to the *kirtan*, but not noticing any change. When I finished, I went out front to see what was going on. Alas! I had missed the photo opportunity.

Later, I saw the historic photograph of Srila Prabhupada standing in front of the Vrindaban temple, with the men around him and the women on the roof. “I was there,” I said. “I’m in the *pujari* room ironing Srila Prabhupada’s clothes.”

## Joining the Vrindaban Temple

By the time the Vrindaban festival wound down, I had formed an attachment to the idea of staying in India. Besides loving the atmosphere of the holy *dhamas*, I knew I’d get more of Srila Prabhupada’s personal association. Since I was in Vrindaban, I was content to stay there, even though I liked Mayapur more.

I spoke with Ramesvara prabhu about my composing service with ISKCON Press. Now that he was in charge, not only was the Press moving to Los Angeles but also the name was changing to the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust (BBT).

I asked, “Is it all right if I stay in Vrindaban? You don’t need me any more. I trained Balai prabhu to compose.”

He didn’t know that actually Balai was not totally proficient—but he agreed I could stay.

He reasoned: “They do need help here because—the temple just opened.”

I was so happy to get permission to stay in India!

Srila Prabhupada stayed in Vrindaban for two days after the festival ended, then left.

My service was in the basement *pujari* room. With the help of Rangavati dasi, another American devotee, I took care of the Deities’ four sets of day clothes, each with matching jewelry, and Their night clothes. Arundhuti dasi, a senior devotee, and I shared a guesthouse room, where we passed many a peaceful afternoon singing Hare Krishna. Arundhuti sang melodious tunes and accompanied herself on a dulcimer, a stringed instrument, while I sang along, playing my *kartals*.

Even when the intense summer heat set in, with temperatures above 120 degrees Fahrenheit, and I was on my back for three weeks with jaundice, I still wanted to stay. I was happy there associating with Rangavati and Arundhuti prabhus. And I also had the association of Bala Gopal dasi (Dhananjaya dasa’s wife) and Visalini dasi (Visala dasa’s wife); both these American ladies, pregnant and due in a few months, courageously planned to deliver their babies in Vrindaban.

But my visa was a problem. I could not extend my three-month tourist visa for more than two weeks.

I was trying to figure out what to do when Palika dasi arrived in Vrindaban. “Did you hear about the publishing marathon in Los Angeles?” she asked. “Srila Prabhupada is upset that the BBT is so far behind him in printing his books. He went to Los Angeles and saw the new BBT offices and equipment, and he demanded that they catch up to him, by sending seventeen volumes to the printer in a mere two months.”

Hearing this, I felt really bad. I realized that my walking out on the composing service wasn’t good timing.

Palika said, “They asked me to help. You should come too.”

She was right—I should offer my help. I was one of the few devotees skilled on the Redactron phototypesetter. Instead of endeavoring to extend my visa, I took it as Krishna’s arrangement, and returned to America.

## Chapter 11 - A Short Stay in America

## Sojourn in New York

Arriving in New York, I asked Gopijanavallabha prabhu, the temple president, to call the BBT in Los Angeles. And he asked someone there whether I could help the BBT workers during the two-month marathon.

“They don’t need you,” he reported. “They have enough devotees.”

“Well, I volunteered my help,” I thought. “I don’t mind that they trained others because now I’m free to return to India.” I had promised Gopal Krishna dasa, one of the GBCs for India—who had lived in the Brooklyn temple before he was appointed to the GBC and who knew I was an experienced typesetter—that I would return to Bombay to serve as his secretary.

Once my visa from the Indian Embassy was squared away, I began selling incense at street corners with Alarka prabhu. We usually went across the Manhattan Bridge to a corner with a long red light. Using Alarka’s method, I approached the cars at the light, pointed a stick of incense at the drivers and said, “This is a stick-up!”

Usually the people laughed, and I gave them the incense and asked for something for “our church.” They threw their donations into my brightly decorated collection bucket, and the temple let me keep a percentage for my airfare to India.

I felt very happy in New York, because every morning I bathed and dressed little Radha-Krishna, something I’d always wanted to do. These Deities, with Their curvy hips and big eyes, were my favorites. When I went out with incense, I thought of these Deities. I might not know Radha-Krishna, but I had a relationship with Their Deity forms.

Still recovering from jaundice, for weeks I ate only fruits for breakfast and boiled vegetables for lunch or dinner. At one Sunday feast, however, I couldn’t control my senses, and ate fried potatoes in sour cream. As a result, I doubled over in pain for eight hours straight, and there was nothing the devotees could do to help. I just had to suffer for my lack of restraint, but I didn’t repeat this mistake.

## The Philadelphia Ratha-yatra

Srila Prabhupada was coming to the Philadelphia Ratha-yatra parade and festival, the first large-scale Ratha-yatra on the East Coast of the United States. In 1971, I’d participated in a small Philadelphia Ratha-yatra in which the devotees had carried tiny Jagannatha Deities in a decorated cigar box to a quiet place near the Schuylkill River, where surprised weekend picnickers watched us dance and chant. I wasn’t in Philadelphia for the next year’s Ratha-yatra, but heard that the large Lord Jagannatha Deities had emerged from a closet to grace a small cart, which the devotees pulled through city streets. Afterwards, the devotees expected to put the Jagannatha Deities back in the closet, but their GBC insisted that henceforth they worship the Deities. So they began doing the temple worship.

Srila Prabhupada’s presence made this year’s Ratha-yatra, in July, world-class. I traveled for two hours from New York to Philadelphia with other devotees. We attended the festival and saw Srila Prabhupada, as did devotees from many other temples.

The bright cart was exceptionally beautiful, its huge wheels decorated with many diamond-shaped mirrors. Srila Prabhupada joined the parade through downtown Philadelphia when it was midway along its route. When all of us reached the festival site, Srila Prabhupada sat on a stage to deliver a talk, and his audience sat pleasantly on the park lawn.

## A Tour of Our New Building in Manhattan

For a long time Srila Prabhupada had wanted the New York temple moved back to prestigious Manhattan. At long last the temple authorities were in the process of purchasing a twelve-story skyscraper in mid-Manhattan, at 340 West 55th Street, between 8th and 9th Avenues.

Because I was about to leave for India after my six-week stay, I was allowed to join Mother Silavati and a few others for a tour of the new facility. It was fantastic! Past the front lobby, with its reception desk, was a very large room—perfect for a temple room. The ground floor also had space for a boutique as well as the *pujari* rooms. In the basement we saw a commercial kitchen that needed a thorough cleansing, and a large dining room, slotted for a restaurant.

We rode up in one of two passenger elevators (there was also a service elevator somewhere else), and on the second and third floors we saw several medium-size offices, two rooms big enough for *prasadam* halls and a one-hundred-and-ten-seat theater, complete with lighting and dressing rooms. On the third floor we also gained access to a huge, fenced verandah, the size of the proposed temple room two floors below it.

Off T-shaped hallways on the next seven floors were many small rooms that could house two devotees each. Two hundred devotees could live here comfortably.

The eleventh floor was planned for guestrooms and Srila Prabhupada’s quarters. On the roof we had a bird’s-eye view of midtown Manhattan.

The building felt eerie, and we knew why—many people had died here. It had been a nursing home. When we walked into one office, all of us felt the presence of someone—a spirit soul in a ghost’s body. That problem would be solved by plenty of *kirtan*, which had the potency to dispel ghosts.

## Traveling Back to India

A curious thing happened before I left New York. I phoned Los Angeles to speak with Radhaballabha dasa, the production manager at the BBT, about some money he owed me, and he asked, “Where have you been for the last two months? We could have used your help here during the marathon!”

“But Gopijanavallabha told me the BBT didn’t need me!”

“Anyway, it’s over now,” he said. “We did the impossible! We published seventeen books in just two months, and Srila Prabhupada is very pleased.”

Well, I never figured that one out! I just chalked it up to Krishna’s arrangement. For although I missed the marathon, I was returning to India.

Gopal Krishna prabhu wrote me a letter, asking me to bring sequin trims for the Bombay Deities, film, cassette tapes and my own typewriter. I purchased a manual Olympia typewriter in a hard carrying case—practical for India because the electricity was unreliable. The sequin trims filled a small trunk, along with the film and tapes.

Gopal Krishna prabhu said I could go to Vrindaban first, because Srila Prabhupada was there. My goal was to arrive by his Vyasa-puja day, on August 31st.

I flew to London and spent a few days there, and I bought a cheap ticket to Bombay, with a stopover in Delhi. I stayed at our wonderful Bury Place temple and relished seeing Srila Prabhupada’s rooms, potent with his presence even in his absence.

Arriving at the Delhi airport, I collected my luggage. Going through customs, I was asked to pay import duty for the trunk with the sequin trims.

“See this picture of Radha-Krishna?” I asked, showing them a photograph I had strategically placed on top of the spools of trim. “The trims are for the Deities—they’re not for me! You shouldn’t charge me duty!”

The customs agent was unmoved by my appeal, but another agent appeared out of nowhere and said, “These are for Radha-Krishna? O.K., you may go now. No problem!”

Relieved, I left the airport and caught a train to Mathura. From there, it was a slow yet peaceful journey by bicycle rickshaw to Vrindaban, giving me time to meditate while reading aloud from the Krishna book about Akrura’s realizations upon entering Vrindaban. Because I’d earned my own way to India this time, entering the holy *dhama* seemed more meaningful than on my previous trip. And I was looking forward to seeing Srila Prabhupada on his Vyasa-puja day.

## Chapter 12 - Vrindaban—August-September, 1975

## Srila Prabhupada’s Vyasa-puja Day

After the *guru-puja*, on Vyasa-puja morning, Srila Prabhupada exited the temple via the side door nearest his *vyasasana*. I had waited outside the door, and now I handed him a small photograph and said, “Srila Prabhupada, I’d like to give you a picture of little Radha-Krishna in New York.”

“She just came from New York, Srila Prabhupada,” said Brahmananda Swami, who was at his side.

Srila Prabhupada took interest. He looked at me and asked, “Have they closed on the Manhattan building?”

I answered, “Adi Kesava Swami had the key, and I just had a tour of the building.”

“But have they purchased the building?”

Srila Prabhupada wanted confirmation that the deal on the mid-Manhattan skyscraper was closed—that the title to the property had changed hands, that ISKCON now owned it. All I knew was the devotees had the key. I didn’t know if they had closed the deal. I did not even know the meaning of the term”closed” in relation to buying a building!

The conversation continued briefly, but Srila Prabhupada gave up trying to understand. Photo in hand, he proceeded to his quarters. However, Brahmananda Swami stayed behind, trying to learn more from me, but what I knew wasn’t conclusive.

After he walked on, I was reeling with excitement over this unexpected exchange with Srila Prabhupada! I’d merely handed him a photo, but suddenly I was in the fast track, conversing about an important matter that was dear to his heart. Thrilling as it was to discuss the new temple in mid-town Manhattan with Srila Prabhupada, these affairs were way over my head. So due to my ignorance of the situation, I disappointed him. Obviously, I wasn’t meant to be the bearer of this good news. However, confirmation of the purchase arrived soon after, from New York.

Gopal Krishna prabhu was surprised I returned. Devotees usually left India, claiming they’d return to stay, but rarely did. Once back in the West, India seemed too austere in retrospect, or they took up other engagements. He allowed me to stay in Vrindaban for as long as Srila Prabhupada remained; afterwards, I could go to Bombay to become his secretary. So I settled into the *brahmacarini* ashram, a large room on the top floor of the guesthouse.

## Typing Srila Prabhupada’s Letters

“Is there any secretarial work I can do?” I asked Brahmananda Swami, who was serving as Srila Prabhupada’s secretary. “I have my own typewriter.”

“Yes, you can help type letters,” he said. “Srila Prabhupada tells me what to write. So I’ll dictate the exact words, and you can transcribe the tapes on his letterhead.”

Overjoyed to get this service, almost daily between September 7th and the 21st I conscientiously typed the letters, adding my initials at the bottom: ACBS/bs/mdd. I returned them to Brahmananda Swami, who gave them to Srila Prabhupada to sign. I felt honored to render this service even though I never spoke to Srila Prabhupada himself about it.

I also resumed my service of ironing Srila Prabhupada’s clothes. They smelled so sweet, like sandalwood.

## The Bathroom Lotas

I was one of several devotees helping Daivi-sakti dasi, an American devotee, to clean Srila Prabhupada’s quarters. We cleaned his downstairs rooms during his morning walk, starting at six a.m. A few times, I helped wash the floors, but mostly I polished his brass paraphernalia. I used natural polishers like tamarind and lemons, and tried to maintain the standard he expected.

“Srila Prabhupada is very particular,” Daivi-sakti said. “He does not want to see water spots, especially on his brass drinking glass.”

One day I did the most ridiculous, embarrassing and intolerable thing. Usually, Srila Prabhupada went directly from his morning walk into the temple room, just in time to greet the Deities. This day, Daivi-sakti informed me: “Srila Prabhupada is back early. He is in his quarters, so hurry up and finish.”

I was in Srila Prabhupada’s kitchen, polishing his two bathroom *lotas*, pots with wide lips curved downward. Usually, when I was finished polishing, I put the *lotas* back where they belonged.

I knew Srila Prabhupada had returned, but I assumed he was in his sitting room. I knocked on his bedroom door, which was always closed, and walked in. Then I walked across the bedroom to the bathroom and knocked on that door, also normally closed. Without hesitating, I pushed the door open and started in. I saw Srila Prabhupada walking toward me, his *dhoti* hiked up to his knees!

Shocked to see him there, I fell to my knees to pay my obeisances, and banged the *lotas* loudly on the floor. I usually put the *lotas* in their place, but I just left them right there and ran out as quickly as I could.

I didn’t know what to do. I was thinking, “That’s it. Srila Prabhupada is going to kick me out. I’ve offended him.”

I ran up to my room, frantic to figure out a way to get back in Srila Prabhupada’s good graces after my rudeness and thoughtlessness.

Now I could hear the greeting of the Deities going on in the temple room. I decided to write a short note, begging Srila Prabhupada for forgiveness. Something Visalini prabhu once said flashed through my mind: Srila Prabhupada likes to receive donations. I had only a ten-rupee note—worth about a dollar and twenty cents—but I included it with the letter, sealed the envelope and ran down to the temple room.

The *guru-puja* was going on. When I offered Srila Prabhupada flowers, I put the envelope on his *vyasasana*. Srila Prabhupada’s servant took my letter when Srila Prabhupada got up to leave.

After that I started worrying that he would misunderstand why I enclosed the money: “Maybe he will think I’m trying to buy him.”

Then I worried whether it was enough to be respectable: “It was such a paltry amount!”

I wondered whether I’d been forgiven or not. I was too embarrassed to tell anyone about it. However, the next day, when I was giving Brahmananda Swami a stack of typed letters, I revealed that I had done something to offend Srila Prabhupada.

Maharaja asked, “Did Srila Prabhupada act offended?”

I thought about it: “I took him by surprise and didn’t give him time to react.” So I answered, “No.”

“You probably didn’t offend him.”

I felt a little better, but I still thought it was an inexcusable thing to do. I learned that I’d better be more conscientious and Krishna conscious if I wanted to serve my guru in close proximity.

## Cleaning the Upstairs Rooms

Every afternoon Srila Prabhupada gave *darshan*. Anyone could come to his sitting room to converse, ask questions or just listen. Back in America I would have given my eye tooth to go to his *darshans*, and I had lamented like anything when I was stuck on the BQE in New York in 1973 and missed what I thought was my one-and-only chance. But now that I could freely go to his *darshans* every day if I wanted, I hesitated, feeling it was better to do some service for him. I asked Daivi-sakti prabhu for some afternoon service.

“You can help clean Srila Prabhupada’s room upstairs,” she said.

His upstairs quarters consisted of one enclosed room and a back verandah. On the partially covered verandah, overlooking his garden, Srila Prabhupada took an early-afternoon massage. After *darshan*, he relaxed on the open portion of the verandah and slept about two hours a night, on a bed in the open air, under the roofed section. Rising at one a.m. every morning to translate the *Srimad-Bhagavatam*, he sat at a low desk in the enclosed room.

Evening *darshan* time was the best time to clean this translating room. We started with Srila Prabhupada’s translating desk. Rolling up the mosquito net that hung over it, we cleaned systematically in sections. We moved aside his Grundig dictaphone, Sanskrit *Srimad-Bhagavatam*, and other books and paraphernalia. After cleaning that area, we replaced the items in the exact same spots we’d found them. Then we rolled down the mosquito net and tucked it under his sheet-covered asana.

After wringing out a wet cloth until it was very dry, I washed the floor on my hands and knees. I appreciated all these opportunities to render menial service to Srila Prabhupada.

A couple of times, *darshan* finished, Srila Prabhupada came into the room while we were still cleaning. One time he told Daivi-sakti: “Please climb under the mosquito net and shoo out the mosquitoes trapped inside. Some were caught in the net last night.”

## Early Mornings

After *mangal-arati*, I liked to chant *japa* while circumambulating the temple. Even though Srila Prabhupada was in his upstairs room, on many mornings I heard him dictating while I walked on the narrow path between the back of the temple and his quarters.

When Srila Prabhupada left his quarters for his morning walk, Visala dasa, an American devotee, stood where he’d be noticed, folded his hands in *pranams* and loudly chanted mantras glorifying Srila Prabhupada. I thought this was nice, and I started doing the same thing myself, but unobtrusively—whispering “*Guruvastaka*” prayers when Srila Prabhupada walked by.

One day as Visala was chanting *slokas*, Srila Prabhupada admonished him: “Do something useful!”

It seemed Srila Prabhupada was unimpressed by his display of devotion, and preferred that he do something practical.

Evenings on the Upstairs Verandah

Evenings on the Upstairs Verandah

The temple managers made announcements about giving Srila Prabhupada his privacy in the late evenings while he was on his upstairs verandah, which could be viewed from some guest house rooms. But I confess that we used to spy on Srila Prabhupada from the window of the *brahmacarini* ashram. He was such an attractive personality that we couldn’t resist, even at the risk of getting in trouble!

To not be noticed, we turned off the lights in our room. Sometimes we’d watch Srila Prabhupada chant *japa* alone, pacing slowly back and forth across the verandah. Sometimes he chanted while sitting on an ordinary wooden chair, sometimes with one leg draped casually over an arm of the chair. He was quite relaxed.

We saw Nava-yogendra dasa, an Indian *brahmacari*, bring Srila Prabhupada hot, dry-roasted *pappadams*, one at a time. Nava-yogendra was extraordinarily respectful, offering obeisances both coming and going—no matter that he had done it two seconds before.

Other times we saw Bhagaji, Srila Prabhupada’s elderly neighbor and friend. He’dcome visit, usually with Bhakti Kisori, Kisori dasi’s cute four-year-old daughter, in tow.

## Srila Prabhupada’s Mirror Compact

“If you don’t do something with this photograph of little Radha-Krishna in New York that you gave Srila Prabhupada, he’s going to leave it behind when he goes,” Daivi-sakti prabhu said one morning as we cleaned.

She had an idea. Srila Prabhupada had a small, round compact with a mirror, which he used to put on his *tilaka*. In the dish where face powder would normally be kept was a picture of Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakur.

“The picture has been completely ruined by water. Why don’t you replace that picture with the photo of Radha-Krishna?” she suggested.

Using the water-damaged picture as a template, I cut the photograph to the right shape and put it the compact.

Srila Prabhupada said nothing about the new photo, as far as I know. He used that compact for several months, until Hari Sauri prabhu, who became Srila Prabhupada’s new personal servant, arranged for an improved model: a solid-silver compact with Krishna-Balaram embossed in gold relief on the cover. Srila Prabhupada gave Hari Sauri the old compact, which he gave to Bhavananda Maharaja.

## Radhastami Day

A pious family donated to Srila Prabhupada a parcel of land adjacent to the temple lot. He decided to use the land for an international *gurukula* for boys. Thus a section of the brick wall there was knocked down to create the *gurukula*’s side entrance. On Radhastami day, September 13, 1975, Srila Prabhupada presided over the cornerstone-laying ceremony.

Photographers snapped away, catching Srila Prabhupada standing by the engraved marble cornerstone, surrounded by guests. Devotees, including me, were also standing nearby, witnessing this historic event.

## Plans for the Ahmedabad Pandal Program

Jasomatinandana dasa, a Gujarati devotee, wanted to open a temple in Ahmedabad, so he made arrangements for a *pandal* program there, at the end of September, to generate publicity. Srila Prabhupada’s lectures at the previous Ahmedabad *pandal* program, in the early seventies, had been very popular, and now he again agreed to preach in Gujarat. Devotees from Bombay would be going, too.

Brahmananda Swami told me about the travel plans: “We need an extra person flying with us, to help carry the bags. You see, on international flights there’s a larger luggage allowance than on domestic flights.”

He explained that their flight was from Delhi to Bombay, with stops in Jaipur and Ahmedabad.

“Maybe I could go with you, using my Delhi-Bombay ticket,” I said. “I’ll get off the plane in Ahmedabad. After the *pandal* program, I can go to Bombay by train with the Bombay devotees.”

Because Brahmananda Swami encouraged me, I decided to go for it. However, there was no question of phoning the airline to check on the feasibility of this plan. The Indian phone system was such a joke that the sum and substance of a normal phone conversation was “I can’t hear you!” I would have to work it out at the airport.

Although I was convinced I should fly with Srila Prabhupada’s party to help with the luggage, Brahmananda Swami somehow worked it out so that my help wasn’t needed to carry extra bags, which was good, because I already had too many pieces of my own. Two days before Srila Prabhupada left Vrindaban, I hopped on a train to Delhi and awaited their arrival at our Delhi temple.

## Chapter 13 - To Ahmedabad with Srila Prabhupada

## Pressed for Time in the Delhi Temple

Arriving at our small, rented temple in Delhi, behind Bengali Market, I saw the gorgeous Deities, Sri Sri Radha–Partha-sarathi. Tejiyas dasa, the temple president, didn’t want to host visiting devotees because Srila Prabhupada—and Brahmananda Swami, Harikesa dasa and Navayogendra dasa—would be spending twenty-four hours there before flying out, but he relented when he heard I was going with Srila Prabhupada’s group.

Madira dasi, Tejiyas’s wife, and Tejasini, her infant daughter, as well as the other ladies—Sitala dasi and Aditi dasi—and I would sleep on the roof to free the *brahmacarini* ashram for Srila Prabhupada and his entourage. On the morning of his arrival we were so busy moving our things upstairs and getting everything ready that I didn’t have time to chant my morning *gayatri*.

Very few people—maybe ten devotees and some guests—were on hand to greet Srila Prabhupada when he arrived at eight a.m. We followed Srila Prabhupada into his room and sat down. I decided I’d better chant *gayatri* before it was too late.

Almost immediately someone brought Srila Prabhupada’s breakfast *prasadam* and said, “It’s time to go. Srila Prabhupada’s breakfast is here.”

I was chanting *gayatri* as fast as I could. The devotees were filing out, one by one, past Srila Prabhupada, who was placing pieces of cut fruit from his plate into their hands. Although purposely last, I was still hurriedly chanting when I held out my hand to Srila Prabhupada for my share of fruit, which he pushed into my hand. I appreciated Srila Prabhupada’s touch, but at the same time my mind was absorbed in my frenzied chanting. I wondered if Srila Prabhupada noticed a strange expression on my face. This was a good lesson to chant *gayatri* on time.

## Checking In and Waiting for the Plane

Srila Prabhupada gave an evening class in the Delhi temple. Early the next morning he was driven to the airport for his six a.m. flight. Tejiyas, Madira, some other devotees and I also went in a car.

Srila Prabhupada and his group checked in and walked to the passenger lounge. I went to the counter with my ticket and explained that I wanted to disembark in Ahmedabad. The agent was at a loss what to do. He wasn’t bothered in the least that I was a month late making my connection or that I didn’t have a reservation. No, it was my wanting to get off the plane in Ahmedabad that he couldn’t handle. Because the ticket said that Bombay was my destination, he expected me to fly all the way there. He saw this as something unusual, a problem.

The agent said, “Look, if everyone checks in and there’s an empty seat, we’ll allow you to go.”

I sat there anxiously till everyone checked in. Finally, he said, “O.K., you can board the flight.”

By the time I got to the waiting lounge, it was full. Plopping down my bags—three shoulder bags and my heavy typewriter—I offered my obeisances to Srila Prabhupada. Not seeing any place to sit on the long benches, I sat on the floor, some feet away from him. I looked up at Srila Prabhupada. He made a slight gesture with his hand a couple of times, and immediately—two feet away from Srila Prabhupada’s entourage—some passengers slid down the bench to make room for me.

After being seated, I looked around carefully. The lounge was filled with businessmen. Then I realized: “I made a mistake!” This lounge had no carpeted floor like the nice American airports, and I wasn’t with a group of devotees who were also sitting on the floor. I was embarrassed and probably turned beet-red. But I was grateful that those men were so receptive to Srila Prabhupada’s slight gesture. Srila Prabhupada had saved me, in more ways than one.

## The Flight

When it was time to board, Srila Prabhupada went first. He sat in the front row next to the door. Everybody boarding the plane passed him. Because of his *sannyasa* dress, most knew he was a *sadhu*, but many were unaware that he was a pure devotee of Lord Krishna. I sat six or seven rows back and spoke to the people on either side of me about Srila Prabhupada’s books. The only book I had in my hand luggage was The Nectar of Devotion.

I said, “The person who wrote this book is sitting up there. He is my guru.”

They were curious about Srila Prabhupada, so I glorified him. I offered to sell them my book, but they declined.

The flight attendants served breakfast and offered me fruitcake. I refused.

“Oh? They’re eating it up there,” she said, nodding in Srila Prabhupada’s direction.

“Well, if they’re eating it,” I said, “I can, too.”

The same stewardess came back with a pitcher of hot, sweet milk: “They’re drinking this.”

A few minutes later a steward came to my seat with a coffee cup full of *dahi-wadas*, deep-fried balls of ground *dahl* soaked in yoghurt.

“They sent this back to you,” he said.

The *dahi-wadas* were from Srila Prabhupada’s tiffin, which the Delhi devotees had packed full of *prasadam*. I knew Brahmananda Swami was responsible for sending this *prasadam* back to me. He was like a big brother, allowing me to type Srila Prabhupada’s letters, encouraging me to come on this flight and now sending me some remnants from Srila Prabhupada’s plate.

When Srila Prabhupada rose to wash his hands, Brahmananda Swami stood in the aisle. I caught his eye and smiled my thanks. I appreciated so much getting the *maha-maha-prasadam*, the remnants of the Lord’s pure devotee.

## Our Arrival in Ahmedabad

Landing in Ahmedabad, we could hear a *kirtan* as soon as the airplane door opened. Srila Prabhupada was the first out. He walked down the stairs, but didn’t go into the terminal. Instead, he walked across the tarmac toward the devotees, who poured onto the airfield, swarmed around him and swept him away with them.

I was way behind Srila Prabhupada. Afraid of being left behind, especially because I still had to claim my luggage inside the terminal, I was glad to catch up with some ladies: Radha-kunda dasi, Jasomatinandana’s wife, who was carrying Sundari, her year-and-a-half-old daughter, and Aditya dasi, from Bombay. They were surprised to see me getting off the plane with Srila Prabhupada.

Radha-kunda prabhu said that the devotees had been riding on a flatbed truck all over downtown Ahmedabad to advertise the *pandal* program. They had also ridden on the truck out to the airport and now were going to ride in it as they followed Srila Prabhupada’s car to his quarters. Before they left, Radha-kunda arranged for a cousin of Kartikeya Mahadevia, a life member and a main organizer of the *pandal* program, to wait for me and drive me to where the ladies were staying.

The problem was that the cousin didn’t know where that was. He took me to where the men were staying—someone’s car dealership offices—but no one there knew either. He gave up and said, “Let’s just go to Srila Prabhupada.”

Bhogilal Patel, an affluent older man and ISKCON life member, was Srila Prabhupada’s host. His house reminded me of a governor’s mansion in America. Although it was situated in the middle of the city, it stood inside such a large park that there was no evidence of the city beyond the trees. The long, winding driveway ended in a circular drive around a large fountain. The outside entrance was very impressive, as was the front vestibule, dominated by a double curved, marble stairway.

The living-room floor was covered in white sheets, for Srila Prabhupada’s use. Leaving my luggage there, we entered the next room and found Srila Prabhupada sitting in a big chair in the library—a real library, with bookshelves up to the ceiling. I offered my obeisances and sat down.

Srila Prabhupada was talking with the devotees, Mr. Patel and his family members and friends. I recognized some devotees: Acyutananda Swami, Giriraja dasa, Sridhara dasa and others.

Srila Prabhupada asked some of the young Indian men: “What do you do?”

“We are going to the university, studying technology.”

“Why are you bothering to study technology? They have already perfected it in the West,” Srila Prabhupada admonished. “You are just wasting your time, doing what has already been done. Take Germany. The Germans have made so many technological objects—watches, cameras, tape recorders, dictaphones. My dictaphone is made in Germany, by Grundig.”

Some devotees pitched in more items, adding to the list of advanced products made in the West.

Srila Prabhupada said, “Stop wasting your time studying technology and instead study Krishna consciousness. I want one hundred young men from Ahmedabad to join the Hare Krishna movement and be trained up as devotees of Krishna.”

Unfortunately, for them, no one surrendered on the spot.

Srila Prabhupada continued speaking and at one point asked for a *Bhagavad-gita*, to look up a verse. None of the devotees had one, which was understandable because they’d been riding around the city on a truck. But I had all my belongings with me, including a *Bhagavad-gita*.

However, I was reluctant to get it. Why? Well, because I had left New York with too much luggage, including many of Srila Prabhupada’s books, and the devotees in London had said, “Why carry so much? You can get more books in India.” Agreeing, I renounced most of my books and donated them to temple. Then in Vrindaban I had asked to purchase a new *Bhagavad-gita*. After paying the full retail price to the temple president, he said, “Get one out of the temple library.” The library books were typically worn out. I chose the best *Bhagavad-gita*, but along the outer edge of the pages was clearly written: “Temple Copy Number 4.” This was the *Bhagavad-gita* in my luggage.

How could I hand this book to Srila Prabhupada? It would look like I had stolen the temple copy!

Remembering the saying “Do the needful,” I fetched the book andpassed it forward. Someone looked up the verse for Srila Prabhupada.

For the second time that day, I was embarrassed and expected Srila Prabhupada to ask, “Whose book is this? Why did you take it from the temple?” But he said nothing.

After the *darshan*, I went with the ladies to their room in the house of Mr. Sony, another life member. I was surprised to find that little Radha-Rasabihari, the small-size brass Deities from the Bombay temple, were also staying in our room. Mother Aditya was taking care of Them. They would be the presiding Deities at the *pandal* program.

## The Ahmedabad Pandal Programs

Banners stretched across many busy roads in Ahmedabad, advertising the five-night *pandal* program and its exalted guest speaker, Srila Prabhupada. As a result, many thousands of people packed the *pandal* each night.

When Srila Prabhupada would arrive on stage, he’d stand with folded hands before little Radha-Rasabihari, gazing at Them for a long time before sitting down. Mother Aditya had pointed out to me that Srila Prabhupada had a special attraction for these Deities, and observing him proved her right. These quite small, beautiful Deities enchanted Srila Prabhupada.

Mr. Mahadevia and the twenty or so devotees sat onstage with Srila Prabhupada. Srila Prabhupada’s *vyasasana* was definitely not up to the proper standard for a world-renowned representative of Lord Sri Krishna, but he accepted it humbly.

After the initial *kirtan* and Srila Prabhupada’s lecture, we showed Yadubara dasa’s film, The Hare Krishna People, with dubbed-in Hindi. The screen was a white sheet hung from the front of the stage, between the audience and us. We could see through the sheet and watch the film in reverse.

We could tell from the audience’s reactions that they loved the entire film, but their favorite segment was the one on *gurukula*. When Balai and Advaita prabhus’ curly-haired, five-year-old daughter Nandini wrinkled her cute little nose, and when Saudamani and Rabindra Swarupa prabhus’ beautiful blonde, six-year-old Emily (later Radhikaika) chanted a *Bhagavad-gita* verse, the audience oohed and aahed.

One night, someone put a gift on the stage for us—a case of cold Limca, the Indian equivalent of 7-Up, considered a Western luxury. The bottles sat on the stage behind us, opened and getting warm, through the first part of the program. Once it was dark enough to show the film, each of us, even Srila Prabhupada, was handed a lukewarm Limca. Suddenly the lights came on, and there we were, caught drinking Limcas! Maybe this was someone’s idea of a practical joke, to make people think we liked expensive cold drinks. But who could enjoy a lukewarm Limca?

During the final *kirtan* Srila Prabhupada would leave the *pandal* stage. Devotees respectfully saw him to a car. From this point, Acyutananda Swami and Sridhara prabhu took over the program and did some fund-raising.

They sent devotees into the crowd to pass out envelopes on the men’s side first, while giving their spiel: “We are rich men’s sons! We don’t need your money. No! Rather, you need to give your money to Krishna for your sake! Whatever you donate to Srila Prabhupada for spreading Krishna consciousness, you will get back one-millionfold, guaranteed.”

Like this, the crowds were cajoled into filling the envelopes, which the *brahmacaris* returned to the stage. Acyutananda Swami and Sridhara held a contest of sorts. Whoever placed a one hundred rupee note—worth about twelve dollars—in an envelope would receive a framed photograph of Radha-Krishna Deities. The person’s name was read out as he came forward to receive his prize, and the crowd applauded appreciatively, encouraging others to do the same. These two devotees also appealed to the audience to become life members.

After the men finished passing up their envelopes, Acyutananda Swami, knowing that the ladies would donate only coins, would say to me, “Go give envelopes to the ladies and get their *paisa* \[coins\].” But they all gave something and thus were given the opportunity to serve Lord Krishna and his representative, Srila Prabhupada.

## Engagements with Life Members

During the day Srila Prabhupada accepted invitations to visit people’s homes. The friends and relatives of the hosts came as well. After Srila Prabhupada spoke about Krishna consciousness, the Bombay devotees asked everyone, if they weren’t already, to become life members. For a one-time donation, life members received some of Srila Prabhupada’s books and the privilege of staying in any of our centers worldwide for three days a year.

Our hosts, always extremely gracious, prepared wonderful *prasadam*. Srila Prabhupada usually ate in a separate room with the men, while the ladies took *prasadam* in the living room. One time Srila Prabhupada requested a dry-roasted *pappadam* to conclude his meal. I saw the ladies of the house quickly jump up to make it for him.

At someone’s house Srila Prabhupada told an anecdote about Krishna devotees from India, preaching in Burma, who were cooking butter to make ghee. Their native neighbors complained that the ghee smelled bad. They disliked its wonderful, sweet aroma. In the same village was a big vat. When animals died, they were placed inside this vat, fermenting for months. And it smelled so bad! The same people who did this complained that the ghee smelled bad. We laughed with Srila Prabhupada as he told this outrageous story, especially when he added that the people bottled the liquid from the vat to make perfume!

After the scheduled five days of *pandal* programs, Srila Prabhupada had to leave for Bombay to catch a flight to Africa and Mauritius. However, because the programs were so popular, he asked Acyutananda Swami and Sridhara prabhu to continue them for another five days. It was impossible to stay at that location, so another one was quickly found. Even without prior advertising, however, the next five nights were also well attended. Life members continued to invite us for lunch *prasadam* at their homes.

Many devotees left with Srila Prabhupada, including Giriraja prabhu, the Bombay temple president, and his secretary, Mother Aditya, who left me in charge of bathing and dressing little Radha-Rasabihari and bringing Them to the nightly programs. After the *pandal* programs ended, I took Them back to Bombay on the train.

## Chapter 14 - Bombay—October-November, 1975

## Srila Prabhupada’s Bruised Knee

By the day I arrived at our Bombay temple, in posh Juhu Beach, an hour north of the city, Srila Prabhupada had departed for Africa and Mauritius, but he was expected back soon.

I started my service as Gopal Krishna prabhu’s secretary, but he changed his mind when he learned I didn’t know shorthand. Therefore, after organizing his filing system, I took up Deity worship—cooking, sewing outfits and dressing the Deities.

A telegram arrived from Srila Prabhupada, saying he would arrive on such-and-such a date, at midnight. Waiting at the airport in the middle of the night, many Bombay devotees, including me, watched the passengers disembark, but no Srila Prabhupada. Disappointed, we figured the date was confused because of the midnight timing. The next night we traipsed back to the airport, and this time we joyfully greeted Srila Prabhupada with *kirtan*.

Back at the temple, everyone followed Srila Prabhupada up the stairs to his top-floor flat in a Hare Krishna Land apartment building. Although it was around one a.m., Srila Prabhupada invited us into his *darshan* room.

He sat behind his desk and informed us: “I was in a car accident in Mauritius.” We were upset to hear this.

“Are you all right, Srila Prabhupada?” a devotee asked.

“I hurt my knee,” he said.

He lifted his *dhoti* to show us his knee. From where I sat, I could not see his scar clearly. But just the act of displaying his knee to share his experience with us was intimate. It was something you do with your family, and I was touched that he considered us his family.

“Just before the accident, I put my cane in front of me,” Srila Prabhupada told us. “Krishna saved me. He told me what to do.”

He added that the worst part was the shock—not knowing what was going on during the collision.

A few days later Brahmananda Swami asked me to type his report about the accident.

The prime minister of Mauritius had agreed to meet Srila Prabhupada, but couldn’t see him for several days. Therefore Srila Prabhupada visited South Africa, then returned to Mauritius, but still the time for the appointment was not fixed.

“Let Srila Prabhupada have the use of my car,” the prime minister said, “and I will meet with him later.”

Srila Prabhupada and Brahmananda and Pusta Krishna Swamis toured the island in the prime minister’s chauffeur-driven car. The sugar-cane fields were grown so high that they obstructed the view on the winding roads. Going around a curve, a Volkswagen bug drifting into the wrong lane collided head-on with Srila Prabhupada’s car.

Moments before the accident, Srila Prabhupada, sitting cross-legged in the back seat, had picked up his cane and placed it firmly in front of him, just to brace himself. At the moment of impact, the cane gave him the necessary support to avoid being thrown excessively forward. Still, he was bleeding in several places, and his knee was badly bruised. Both the driver and Pusta Krishna Swami, sitting in the front seat, were injured, as was the couple in the other car. Both cars were totaled.

Flagging down a passing car, Brahmananda Swami pleaded with the driver to give Srila Prabhupada a ride back to his quarters. There, Harikesa prabhu followed Srila Prabhupada’s calm instructions how to make a poultice for the injured knee. Only then did Srila Prabhupada speak about the accident. He revealed: “Krishna saved me. He told me what to do.”

## The Bombay Temple

Srila Prabhupada had struggled long and hard to successfully purchase Hare Krishna Land—several acres of land that included six apartment buildings. Large, white-marble Deities of Sri Sri Radha-Rasabihari stood in a temporary structure. The new temple complex would include “The ISKCON,” a high-class hotel, with round, twin towers. Excavation for the project was proceeding at the front of the property. But a large hole, dug for the foundation, had for far too long been filled with water, much to the frustration of architect Saurabha dasa, whom Srila Prabhupada pushed to complete the project.

The new temple would be the project’s crowning glory, a wonderful home for Sri Sri Radha-Rasabhihari. And additional Deities would be installed: Gaura-Nitai and Sita-Rama-Laksman-Hanuman, for Bombayites were well-known lovers of Lord Rama. New outfits were being sewn for all the Deities.

Srila Prabhupada always came to greet the Deities in the temporary temple room, a simple structure like a shed. When the curtains opened and the “*Govindam*” prayers boomed forth from the sound system, Srila Prabhupada offered his full *dandavats* to the beautiful Deities. I watched him stand with folded hands before the altar, and from the tilt of his head I knew that little Radha-Rasabihari held his attention for a long time. Then, raising his head, he admired the big Deities.

During *guru-puja*, Srila Prabhupada sat on a red-orange *vyasasana—more* like a high-backed chair—and we came forward one by one to offer him flowers. The *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class followed. There weren’t many devotees or guests, so the temple room wasn’t crowded.

One day Srila Prabhupada instructed that a fire sacrifice be set up in the temple room. Then I and the other devotees watched him give *sannyasa* to Nava-yogendra and Nanda Kumara prabhus.

I asked Brahmananda Swami if I could again type Srila Prabhupada’s letters, and he agreed. From November 9th to the 23rd, I typed numerous letters for Srila Prabhupada. Just like in Vrindaban, I dealt with Brahmananda Swami, not Srila Prabhupada directly. Nevertheless, I felt connected to my guru by doing such vital, personal service.

Daivi-sakti dasi, who’d eventually joined us in Ahmedabad, was now in Bombay, and was given charge of Srila Prabhupada’s flat. I volunteered to help clean—this time polishing two big brass buckets that Srila Prabhupada used for bathing. His servant heated the water in them with an electric coil. Just as you find out how big a room is by washing a floor on your hands and knees, similarly, I discovered just how much surface area brass buckets have when I polished them, inside and out. It was a labor of love.

A few days a week, I went on *sankirtan* to distribute books at the Bombay airport. I wasn’t a big distributor, so often I tagged along to watch Radha-kunda prabhu in action. When the first person she approached gave the hackneyed excuse, “I don’t have any money,” she’d laugh. After all, there they were in the airport about to take an expensive flight—and they had no money?

When the next few persons gave the same or an equally unlikely excuse, she’d laugh harder. No matter what they said, she kept laughing nonstop! Somehow her laughter rubbed off on people, and they’d start taking the books and giving donations. She had such an unusual method of book distribution, but it was natural because she had a good sense of humor—and it worked!

## “Purify Your Existence!”

A wealthy life member invited Srila Prabhupada and all the devotees to a program in his home in a high-rise in downtown Bombay. Srila Prabhupada sat on the large apartment’s open verandah, which filled with a big turnout of our host’s friends and relatives, as well as twenty-five or thirty devotees.

Usually, at these engagements, Srila Prabhupada spoke in English for the benefit of his disciples, but this time he was requested to speak in Hindi, much to our chagrin. During the lecture, I chanted *japa* softly and tried to stay alert, but it was difficult when you couldn’t understand a word. Suddenly, Srila Prabhupada said three words in English: “Purify your existence!” He continued in Hindi. These three words—a Srila Prabhupada sutra (code)—so succinctly summed up the whole lecture for us.

## Srimati Morarji

Srila Prabhupada had first seen Hare Krishna Land in 1965, while visiting the home of Srimati Sumati Morarji to request free passage to America on her company line, Scindia Steamship. Srimati Morarji’s house was only a block from Hare Krishna Land, so when several ladies heard of her annual, week-long party in honor of Bala Gopala, her Deity, we decided to distribute Srila Prabhupada’s books there.

I went along with Daivi-sakti and Radha-kunda. Long lines of people waited outside her backyard, which was covered by a *pandal*. Guards controlled the flow by shutting the gates periodically, whenever it became too crowded inside. When the doors reopened, everyone pushed forward, in typical Indian style, allowing us to slip in without revealing that we didn’t have an invitation.

After having *darshan* of Bala Gopal, I tried, rather unsuccessfully, to distribute books. Suddenly, I was face to face with Srimati Morarji herself, an energetic older lady. She was about my height, but appeared bigger and menacing, considering the henchmen at her sides. She boomed, “Do you know who I am? If it wasn’t for me, you wouldn’t have met your spiritual master!”

Even though she was yelling at me, I was glad to meet this lady, who had generously granted Srila Prabhupada free passage on her steamship Jaladuta, but it stuck me as odd that she was taking the credit for Srila Prabhupada’s success. I saw her as his instrument, just as I was Srila Prabhupada’s instrument; not that he was dependent upon us for anything. If we didn’t help him in his mission, he would accomplish it with the help of others. But if we did help him, we’d get the credit and go back to Godhead, as Srila Prabhupada promised me once by letter.

Srimati Morarji had a different perspective: “What are you doing here? You weren’t invited! Now get out!”

Her assistants made sure I found my way out fast.

## My First Darshan

In the early evenings Srila Prabhupada had open *darshans*, just as in Vrindaban. Still feeling unqualified, I didn’t go. One day Sarvamangala dasi, from England, tried to knock some sense into my head: “You’re not always going to have this opportunity. You’d better take advantage of it now, because it won’t last forever!”

I realized she was right. Giving up all my excuses—like feeling that I should do some service instead or that the *darshan* was just for guests—I finally decided to go with her.

We walked into Srila Prabhupada’s room, offered obeisances and sat down. Haridasa, an Bengali *brahmacari*, was there with some guests. Within a short time they all left, leaving just the two of us with Srila Prabhupada. Sarvamangala prabhu said, “Should we leave, Srila Prabhupada?”

“Yes, unless you have some questions,” he answered.

Both of us politely said, “No,” and offered our obeisances to leave, though actually we both had questions. Rising to a sitting position again, we simultaneously started to ask our questions. I deferred to Sarvamangala.

“Srila Prabhupada,” she said, “I heard thatYour Divine Grace said yesterday, during *darshan*, that the *sannyasis*’ business is to preach, the *brahmacaris*’ business is to assist the *sannyasis* and the *grhasthas*’ business is Deity worship. What about the *brahmacarinis*? Can we also worship the Deity?”

“Yes, *brahmacarinis* can do.”

Most of the Deity worship in Bombay was performed by single ladies—Cintamoni, Moksa-laksmi, Ambika, Kanta, Sarvamangala, me and others. Sarvamangala wanted to ensure that the temple authorities here, and elsewhere, wouldn’t use Srila Prabhupada’s words—”Deity worship is the *grhasthas*’ business”—as an excuse to kick *brahmacarinis* off the altar. Srila Prabhupada confirmed that we could continue our Deity service.

It was my turn, and I knew exactly what I wanted to ask: “Can I have my own Deities?” Here was my golden opportunity, but I felt too embarrassed to ask this in the first person: “Can I . . .?” I’d never spoken to Srila Prabhupada before like this. How could I ask such a personal question? I couldn’t.

My motivation flashed through my mind. I knew two ladies worshiping their own Deities, with Srila Prabhupada’s blessings. One was my friend Cintamoni dasi; Srila Prabhupada told her to worship Lord Jagannatha, after her husband took *sannyasa*. The other was Nandulala dasi, whom I met in India. When she’d spoke to Srila Prabhupada in his Los Angeles quarters about her situation—unmarried and older than most ladies (around thirty-two)—he had handed her a silver Krishna Deity off his desk, saying, “Worship this Deity.”

Comparing myself with these ladies, I was thinking, “I’m also alone, and I’m getting old—I’m twenty-nine. My marriage before I joined the movement doesn’t count, and the New Vrindaban marriage by a flower-ceremony was a joke. I don’t want to get married again. Besides, I heard that Srila Prabhupada said marriage after thirty isn’t pleasing because one is set in his or her ways.”

Thinking like this, I wanted Srila Prabhupada’s permission to worship my own Deities. Because I was too inhibited to ask directly about myself, I generalized: “Can devotees have their own Deities?”

Srila Prabhupada answered, “No. They will distract from the temple Deities.”

I felt defeated, but I was comparing myself with those two other ladies, thinking we should all be treated alike. Srila Prabhupada got to the root of my deep-seated impersonalism. I was forgetting that we are all unique, spiritual individuals. Therefore I learned: “What is good for others may not necessarily help me.”

Srila Prabhupada answered me according to the time and circumstance—I was a *brahmacarini*. I knew that householders are supposed to make their homes into temples; but the basic principle is to serve the temple Deity and not allow worship of personal Deities to detract from that service.

## Leaving Bombay

When Srila Prabhupada left Bombay, most of the devotees found rides to the nearby airport, just ten minutes away. From the vantagepoint of the terminal roof we watched Srila Prabhupada’s plane take off—our *kirtan* in full swing.

When I returned to the temple, I was completely perturbed to find that I’d left my *kartals* on the airport roof. I jumped in a taxi and rushed back to the terminal, but they were gone. I was devastated. Srila Prabhupada had played them three times—the last time during *guru-puja* in Bombay. They were the most valuable thing I’d ever lost.

Jayapataka Swami came to Bombay shortly after Srila Prabhupada left. He asked for help in Mayapur. He needed teachers, secretaries, and so on. I wanted to fill the post of secretary because I loved Mayapur, but Ambika prabhu from Australia wanted to go there to teach, and she had first choice, having served in Bombay longer than me.

At the last minute she changed her mind, and I was selected instead. Krishna kindly fulfilled my desire! Jayapataka Swami paid for my train ticket, and I traveled alone to Mayapur in late November, 1975.

## Part Four: Śrīla Prabhupāda in Mayapur

## Chapter 15 - Mayapur—November, 1975-March, 1976

## Living in Mayapur

Mayapur was quiet when I arrived, compared with festival time. So few devotees lived there: a few *brahmacaris*, about two dozen Westerners and several families, most refugees from Bangladesh. All the men—even *grhasthas*—occupied rooms along the boundary wall. Homes for the Bengali ladies and their daughters and pre-school sons were in the boundary-wall rooms nearest the Big Kitchen; their older sons lived in the *gurukula* rooms, also in the boundary wall, near the front gate. The school included a few local nondevotee boys, and classes took place outdoors on the grass.

Living above the Big Kitchen were the Western ladies: Vrindaban Biharini dasi, from South Africa; Krishna-rupa dasi, from Australia; Svati dasi and her three-year-old son Sivajvara, from England; Kamadhuk dasi, from Scotland; Khandabasi dasi, with her eight-year-old daughter Susasita, from Germany; and Australian Rasamandala and her two-year-old son Damodara. A round bathroom building accommodated everyone: men in the outer ring and ladies in the inner circle. Two hand-pumps provided water.

The Deities—Sri Sri Radha-Madhava, Lord Caitanya and a saligram-sila (a stone Deity of Krishna)—were nicely looked after by only three *pujaris*: Jananivasa dasa brahmacari, from England, the dedicated head *pujari* since Day One of ISKCON Mayapur; Pankajanghri dasa brahmacari, his identical twin brother; and Anakadundubhi dasa, another Englishman, whose wife was Vrindaban Biharini prabhu. The Bengali men maintained a twenty-four-hour *kirtan* in the temple room, and many of them sang like Gandharvas (angels).

The Lotus Building rooms were reserved for guests. Huge *tulasi* bushes, some nearly five feet high, grew at the back of the building, and more distant was a small vegetable garden. The Deities’ flower gardens, all along one side of the path from the front gate, provided many fragrant varieties and even imported American roses.

Muslim guards played *sahnai* music in a small room above the front gate during every sunrise. No shops or rickshaws, except one or two, were outside the front gate, and guests were rare.

The weather was hot and humid most of the year, with perhaps eight weeks of cold in mid-winter. The storm season was most exciting. The cyclones approached so fast that, if I was in my room, I had only enough time to close the wooden shutters before the high winds blasted. The incredible beauty of the rainy-season skies proved that Krishna is the supreme artist. At every sunrise and sunset there was a twenty-minute light show.

Bhavananda Maharaja told us that once he asked Srila Prabhupada: “Is it wrong to enjoy the beauty of Mayapur?”

“No,” Srila Prabhupada said. “I’ve given you Mayapur to enjoy.”

And we did enjoy Mayapur. Here we could appreciate the wonderful sunrises and sunsets and not be in *maya*—Srila Prabhupada said! Mayapur only seemed to be in the material world, but actually it was entirely spiritual.

Communication with the outside world practically didn’t exist. Letters delivered through the Muslim-run post office near the Yogapitha—if they did arrive—were already opened. Mayapur had one telephone in the Lotus Building, on the back verandah behind the stairs. If someone phoned, everyone in the vicinity could hear the person on our end yelling into the receiver. Hardly anyone called.

The lack of communication really didn’t matter, because we were in the holy *dhama*, Lord Caitanya’s home, and He is especially merciful to fallen souls. I could feel His presence often. Even the banana trees reminded me of Him. Our real, important business was chanting Hare Krishna. Although quiet, Mayapur was the world headquarters of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. The *sankirtan* movement started here, which made it the center of the universe.

I noted that when large groups of pilgrims came, they invariably consisted of all women, with two or three men chaperones. So I understood that women formed the bulk of the Krishna devotees in India. Even though to stay in India we Western women were more or less forced to accept being treated as lower-class citizens, I understood that having a woman’s body was conducive to spiritual life. Women were naturally in a subservient position, which is necessary for maintaining a proper, humble relationship with guru and Lord Krishna.

The back verandah of the Lotus Building was my outdoor office. To type, I sat cross-legged on the floor and perched my typewriter on its hard case. Jayapataka Maharaja dictated letters on tapes, but I didn’t have a transcriber; so I nearly wore out my tape player transcribing his tapes.

As the temple secretary, I had a few other duties, such as keeping a current list of the foreign devotees’ names and passport numbers, for the Foreign Registration Office (FRO), in nearby Krishnanagar.

I also helped Krishna-rupa dasi sew for the Deities. Navadvipa was the nearest town. I hardly ever went shopping there. However, a few times I traveled by train to Calcutta with Krishna-rupa to shop for cloth, trims and decorations for Radha-Madhava’s outfits.

## The Bengali Devotees

The Bengali ladies were Krishna devotees at heart, but they had a problem chanting *japa*. When given *japa* beads, they’d just sit around and talk. So Srila Prabhupada told them to do service instead of *japa*, and thus directly after *mangal-arati* they went to the Big Kitchen to hull rice or to cut fruits and vegetables.

Srila Prabhupada was merciful in giving first initiation to all the Bengali devotees, forgiving the ladies’ their lack of discipline in chanting *japa*. However, only the three ladies who did chant sixteen rounds were later offered second initiation.

The mother of Haridasa prabhu from Bombay temple came to live in Mayapur. Being a cultured lady who could read and write, she didn’t live with the Bengali ladies, but shared a room with me above the Big Kitchen.

We called her “Haridasa Ma” (Haridasa’s mother), as was the custom—the ladies were known by their child’s name. She lived in Mayapur for quite some time before we discovered she was a fabulous cook. After that, she cooked for Radha-Madhava. At some point, the devotees started calling her Didi Ma, meaning “older sister.”

The Bengali men made saris on looms. This cottage industry provided income for the temple when the saris were sold during the Gaura Purnima festival. Once or twice a year each female devotee living in Mayapur received one of the colorful handloom saris.

The Bengali ladies wrapped their saris differently than we Western ladies—only city slickers in India wore their saris like us—so we adopted their style, which was faster to wrap and cooler to wear, though harder to keep over the head.

I liked to watch the easily pleased Bengali children. The toddlers were satisfied playing with a small stick or some leaves. They were not suffering for lack of store-bought toys. Kids in America were less peaceful, though perhaps more intelligent because of being brought up on cows’ milk.

## Prasadam

Everyone ate in the *prasadam* pavilion behind the Big Kitchen. The *prasadam* was austere by Western standards, but it was “either eat there or starve.” Except for a few bites of *maha-prasadam* each morning, we had no other *prasadam*, not even for a price—and no means to buy *bhoga* (unoffered foodstuffs), nor any facilities for cooking. There was one concession for Western devotees: we each received a piece of fruit at four p.m. daily.

I looked forward to the twice-monthly Dvadasi day—the day after Ekadasi, when we fasted from grains and beans—for this was the only time we got a few mouthfuls of sweet *prasadam*. That small amount of delicious *atta* (wheat flour) *halava*, however, was insufficient to satisfy my sweet tooth. I never realized how many naturally sweet things I was accustomed to eating until I was deprived of them—hardly any fruit, no fruit juices, no Sunday feast.

Desperate, during my daily stint at the temple book table, I’d sneak some *gur-badam*—peanuts and *gur*—meant for the infrequent guests. I also asked the purchaser, or “kitchen in charge,” to buy me a supply of rock candy, which I kept in a jar in my room.

Instead of a Sunday Love Feast we had two “feasts” per week, on Saturday and Sunday: *kittri* cooked with mustard oil-distributed to two thousand people besides us. I no longer looked forward to the weekends as I used to in America. However, when a feast day came around, Radha-Madhava ate sumptuously, and so did we. I felt very satisfied. The Bengali ladies really knew the art of cooking!

We drank water from the hand pumps, none of which were deep wells. I suffered from stomach ailments caused by the lack of pure water but bottled water and filters were unknown to us.

## “Better Not to Mix”

We did not visit other temples in Mayapur except as a group, once a year, during the Gaura Purnima festival, because Srila Prabhupada didn’t want us to associate with his Godbrothers. He wrote to Rupanuga prabhu, on April 28, 1974, in a letter that circulated all over ISKCON:

. . . Actually amongst my Godbrothers no one is qualified to become acarya. So it is better not to mix with my Godbrothers very intimately because instead of inspiring our students and disciples they may sometimes pollute them. This attempt was made previously by them, especially Madhava Maharaja and Tirtha Maharaja and Bon Maharaja but somehow or other I saved the situation. This is going on. We shall be very careful about them and not mix with them. This is my instruction to you all. They cannot help us in our movement, but they are very competent to harm our natural progress. So we must be very careful about them. . . .

Because we lived so near to Srila Prabhupada’s Godbrothers in Mayapur, our local leaders stressed this point even more: “Don’t go to their temples; don’t buy their books; don’t take *prasadam* there. Be respectful if you meet them, but don’t mingle.”

Other Vaisnavas were bound to have perspectives different than Srila Prabhupada’s and to say things differently, and the result would be confusion. Besides that, Srila Prabhupada told us many times that some of his Godbrothers envied his success. Therefore Srila Prabhupada wrote, “We shall be very careful about them and not mix with them. This is my instruction to you all.” Not just Srila Prabhupada’s Godbrothers, but many camps and philosophies were represented in India. Our spiritual lives depended on our chastity in following Srila Prabhupada strictly, without deviating—no adding, no subtracting, no interpreting.

And why should we neglect his pure teachings to hear from others? Obviously, Srila Prabhupada was the only one of his Godbrothers to fully understand and fulfill the desires of their guru, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, to preach Krishna consciousness worldwide. Lord Caitanya’s mercy was flowing through Srila Prabhupada, enabling him to succeed in spreading Krishna consciousness on an unprecedented scale. Our spiritual father was the greatest empowered personality—the *jagat-guru*, or guru of the universe.

I was glad to live in India, because I clearly understood the urgency of Srila Prabhupada’s instructions regarding his Godbrothers. Practicing spiritual life, we were on the razor’s edge—any inattention and we could be cut. Eventually I saw devotees who paid no heed to his warnings and wandered off the path. Srila Prabhupada rectified some of them, but others he couldn’t retrieve.

## Srila Prabhupada’s Arrival

On January 17, 1976, all the Mayapur devotees—Bengalis and Westerners—had a rousing *kirtan* outside the now completed front gate. We were waiting for Srila Prabhupada to arrive and expecting him to stay through the Gaura Purnima festival in March. Finally, his car came! “Jaya Srila Prabhupada! Haribol!” We paid obeisances to him in the street.

Bhavananda Maharaja handed Srila Prabhupada a pair of scissors to cut a wide, inaugural ribbon hanging on the gate. With *kirtan* going full blast we all followed him under the arch and through the metal gates. We were so happy to see Srila Prabhupada! We were not identifying with our bodies, but were truly an international family, exuberantly celebrating the arrival of our glorious spiritual master.

Srila Prabhupada walked the main path to the Lotus Building. Just as he approached the templedoor, a flower shower that looked like an offering from the demigods fell on Srila Prabhupada, thanks to Svati and Kamadhuk prabhus, one flight up.

After taking *darshan* of Radha-Madhava, Srila Prabhupada sat on his *vyasasana*. In his arrival address, he expressed exactly what I was feeling that day:

It was Bhaktivinoda Thakur’s aspiration that the Europeans, Americans, and Indians all together dance jubilantly and chant “Gaura Hari.” So this temple, Mayapur Candrodaya temple, is meant for a transcendental United Nations. What the United Nations has failed to achieve, that will be achieved here by the process recommended by Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu: *prthivita ache yata nagaradi grama*, *sarvatra pracara haibe mora nama*. So you have come from all parts of the world and are living together in this temple.

So train these small boys. I am very glad, especially, to see that the small children from all other countries, and Indian, Bengalis, all together, forgetting their bodily consciousness. That is the greatest achievement in this movement, that everyone forgets the bodily conception of life. Nobody thinks themselves here as European, American, Indian, Hindu, Muslim, Christian. They forget all these designations, and simply they are ecstatic in chanting the Hare Krishna mantra. So kindly what you have begun, do not break it. Continue it very jubilantly. And Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the master of Mayapur, He will be very much pleased upon you, and ultimately you will go back to home, back to Godhead. Thank you very much.

## The Long Building

Whenever I saw Srila Prabhupada, I hardly knew of his activities as the manager of his worldwide organization, even if I was right there in the same temple. But occasionally news trickled down. For instance, the day after his arrival he toured the grounds and suggested that a new building be constructed along the north boundary line, parallel to the Lotus Building. He wanted it finished in time to house the visiting devotees during the Gaura Purnima festival.

“But, Srila Prabhupada, the festival is only six weeks away,” the leaders protested. “How can we get it finished on time?”

The task was impossible, but Srila Prabhupada asked that they try. He often pushed his disciples to increase their service, sometimes quipping that “impossible” was a word found in the fool’s dictionary. The new building did go up—quickly, too.

Srila Prabhupada himself got involved with some details of the construction, and he kept Jayapataka Swami, Pancaratna prabhu and many other devotees busy. Almost a thousand feet long—five times the length of the Lotus Building, and similarly designed—everyone called it “the Long Building” and the nickname stuck.

Since the Ganga flooded Mayapur every eight or nine years, the plans called for two floors of rooms above the hollow space around the plinths on the ground level. Realizing that this was a waste of space, Srila Prabhupada later told the workers to enclose the ground floor to make basement rooms. Srila Prabhupada mentioned it at an evening *darshan*, “For a few more rupees you get thousands of square feet of useable space, so I told them to finish it off.”

## Morning Walks

Women were not invited on Srila Prabhupada’s morning walks, but I was always outside chanting *japa* during that time. Spotting His Divine Grace, I would usually offer obeisances three times: when I first saw him from a distance, when I could see him more clearly, and when he was very close.

The *pukkur* near the front gate was his favorite destination. It was a man-made pond, crudely dug, more or less square. Srila Prabhupada walked along and around its raised sides, his entourage following in single file. The raised sides were fifteen-foot-high piles of dirt blocking the sight of the pond itself. Patches of wild *subjees*—okra and portals—grew on these small hills. I relished watching Srila Prabhupada’s stately form, even from a distance. He walked majestically, using his cane to balance himself as he went around the *pukkur*.

One morning I picked a beautiful, newly opened rose from the flower garden and handed it to Srila Prabhupada as he passed. I felt so good about this—until Bhavananda Maharaja, who hung back, heavily criticized me for picking the rose.

“Roses have to be fully bloomed before you can pick them,” he yelled. He angrily banned me from picking roses.

I was aware of his rule about picking only fully bloomed roses, but what he didn’t consider was that a fully bloomed rose, whose petals are wide open, is on the verge of falling apart, as the garland-makers experienced daily, with great frustration. The pleasure of giving Srila Prabhupada that “contraband” newly opened rose outweighed the aggravation of being scolded for picking it.

One time I was walking on the construction site of the Long Building, so absorbed in my chanting that I didn’t notice Srila Prabhupada and his entourage approaching from behind. I was caught by surprise! For a minute I was part of the morning walk as they encircled me. They continued walking on without me, of course.

## The Srimad-Bhagavatam Classes

The Mayapur temple schedule that we followed throughout the year differed, in some details, from the schedule of the annual international festival. Normally, during the year—after *mangal-arati*, *tulasi-puja* and some *japa—we’d* sing “*Udilo Aruna*” at sunrise. This song celebrates the rising of the sun in Mayapur, a time of day when Lord Caitanya would perform *sankirtan* through the village. And usually we held the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class before we greeted the Deities. So now Srila Prabhupada followed this normal schedule.

Srila Prabhupada gave classes on the Seventh Canto, Chapter Nine. After his first class was over, he turned to Subhaga dasa and said, “Now you translate it into Bengali.” Subhaga prabhu was stunned. Srila Prabhupada had not asked him to translate sentence by sentence. Rather, without warning, Srila Prabhupada expected him to remember the class and summarize it. So difficult! He was on the spot and could say hardly anything. Srila Prabhupada then asked Nitaicand dasa, and he made a passable attempt.

After that, it became a regular feature that one of the bilingual devotees would give a synopsis of the class in Bengali. Now they knew what to expect and listened carefully to the class.

## Guru-puja

After the Deity curtains opened at seven a.m., we greeted the Deities with the “*Govindam*” prayers, followed by Srila Prabhupada sitting on his marble *vyasasana*, with its brightly colored cushions, for *guru-puja*. Then Srila Prabhupada circumambulated the temple room three times, stopping to ring the bells on either side of the altar. Surrounded by dancing *gurukula* boys, Srila Prabhupada gestured to encourage them to dance and jump higher. We all had just as much fun dancing for Srila Prabhupada’s pleasure as we did with the festival crowds.

One morning Srila Prabhupada had gotten up after *guru-puja* and was walking towards the Deities when Jananivasa prabhu stopped him, holding out a plateful of *mangal-arati* sweets. Srila Prabhupada took a piece and popped it into his mouth. Then he distributed the remainder of the sweets to those lucky devotees nearby.

The next day Jananivasa prabhu brought the plate of *mangal-arati* sweets earlier, while Srila Prabhupada was still on the *vyasasana* and the *kirtan* was still going on. Srila Prabhupada again popped a piece in his mouth and indicated that the *gurukula* boys should come forward to receive pieces. The area right in front of the *vyasasana* was very congested, but the boys pushed and shoved to line up, and approached Srila Prabhupada through the small opening in a marble barrier that separated the *vyasasana* area from the temple room. They looked so happy to receive their sweets from his hand.

This was the start of a daily routine. If Srila Prabhupada broke up the sweets into small enough pieces, there were enough for the men to also receive sweets. Some days there were still some leftover after all the boys and men had received theirs, so the little girls—there were only five or six of them—would get sweets.

Last came the women. Oh, those rare and wonderful days when the ladies got the mercy of receiving pieces of *mangal-arati* sweets from Srila Prabhupada’s hand! He wouldn’t just drop the sweet into my outstretched palm—he would push it into my hand, touching it.

When the sweets didn’t stretch to include us, however, the little girls were so generous that they would share their tiny pieces with the ladies, so no one missed out.

The blond-haired boys, Sivajvara and Damodara, loved getting sweets from Srila Prabhupada. They stationed themselves in front of his *vyasasana* and would not budge. They kept their hands out constantly. Srila Prabhupada reciprocated by giving them sweets three or four times. Srila Prabhupada often laughed at their antics. Each time one of them got a piece, he ate it, wiped his hand on his *sikha* (the tuft of hair left on the shaved head of male Vaisnavas) and held out his hand for more—all in one motion. The boys must have been imitating the guests who received in their right hands a tiny spoonful of *carinamrta*—the Deities’ bath water mixed with yogurt and sugar—and sipped it, then wiped their hands on the back of their heads.

Srila Prabhupada’s distributing *prasadam* to all of us was a sweet and intimate affair.

## Flower Pastimes

The little nine- and ten-year-old Bengali girls picked flowers from the gardens for the Deities’ garlands every morning after *mangal-arati*. It was dark at that time of the morning in the winter, but that didn’t deter them. These girls were quite responsible and made all the garlands.

Now they increased the amount of flowers so as to make a garland daily for Srila Prabhupada. They made a huge garland, four inches in diameter, with spirally strung, super-fragrant flowers called *rajani-gandha* (“the queen of scents”), which resemble small white bugles. The girls included in the garland both sections of red and pink roses and variously colored marigolds. It was a masterpiece every day, often reaching Srila Prabhupada’s knees. Srila Prabhupada looked aristocratic wearing these garlands.

Usually there were extra flowers and petals that the little girls sprinkled on top of Srila Prabhupada shoes, outside the door. Srila Prabhupada put on his shoes with a devotee’s assistance, and when he started walking, the flowers would fall off.

One day they didn’t fall off. I watched Srila Prabhupada take several steps and then look down. Noticing that the flowers were still there, he lifted one foot and gently shook it, then the other, but the flowers didn’t move! He looked puzzled.

The little girls were in stitches watching this, for they had played a joke and tied small garlands of flower petals around the toe of each shoe. They had let the ladies in on their trick, and we were also cracking up. Then Srila Prabhupada figured it out and laughed, too, enjoying the joke very much! He had a great sense of humor and didn’t mind the girls playing a joke.

## Evening Darshans

Bhavananda Maharaja renounced his room on the Lotus Building roof for Srila Prabhupada’s use, particularly for his afternoon nap. Coming down from the roof after four o’clock, Srila Prabhupada started evening *darshans* at five. Anyone could come. From the day he arrived, I was inspired to go.

I really liked these evening *darshans* and kicked myself for not going to them when I could in Vrindaban and Bombay.
