Because his health was deteriorating, Srila Prabhupada asked Palika to cook his meals without ghee, not even using it in the *chaunces*, where it is the source of flavor for the *subjees* or *dahl*. To accommodate Srila Prabhupada’s strict regimen, Palika avoided *chaunces* by dry roasting the whole spices instead—cumin, coriander and so on—and then grinding them and adding water to make a wet paste.

Pisima had her own idea how Srila Prabhupada was going to get better: if she were to cook for Srila Prabhupada using mustard oil. Naturally, this led to a conflict between Palika and Pisima, the result being that Palika, trying to protect Srila Prabhupada, barred Pisima from the kitchen. Yet a good-sized jar of mustard oil sat in Pisima’s room, and she wanted to use it to cook for him.

Pisima was so disturbed about the situation that one day she had to speak to Srila Prabhupada immediately. He was on the roof having his massage. It was difficult for Pisima to climb stairs. She needed support on both sides—one arm on the railing, the other arm on me—yet she slowly but surely ascended three flights of stairs that day.

I went only as far as the landing, and from there watched Pisima walk onto the roof. I could see Srila Prabhupada sitting on a straw mat in the sun, wearing his *gamsha*. His body was beautiful to behold, and I meditated upon his transcendental form.

My service to Pisima had advantages, like seeing my spiritual master during his massage.

After they spoke, I helped her down the stairs. Srila Prabhupada must have explained that right now he wanted Palika to continue cooking for him without ghee. But he must have also filled her heart with hope, because after that Pisima was just awaiting the day when Srila Prabhupada would ask her to cook.

## The Plantain Subjee

Weeks later, Pisima sent me to our room to get the jar of mustard oil. Her long-anticipated day had arrived! Srila Prabhupada was hungry and had asked her to cook an evening snack, she explained.

Pisima cooked a plantain *subjee* and sent up a small bowlful to Srila Prabhupada. When his plate came back down to the transfer room, we couldn’t tell whether Srila Prabhupada had tasted this *subjee* because the bowl still looked full. Was Srila Prabhupada actually hungry? Or did he just want to satisfy Pisima’s desire to cook for him?

Surprisingly, no devotees were around to take his remnants. Maybe this was because he didn’t often eat evening *prasadam*, and it was quite late, perhaps nine-thirty or ten p.m. Whatever the reason, Vrindaban Biharini and I were the only ones there, just finishing the kitchen clean-up, and we could have shared all the remnants if we’d wanted to. However, this plantain *subjee* reeked of so much mustard oil that we couldn’t eat it. Here was our golden opportunity to eat Srila Prabhupada’s remnants to our heart’s content, but we couldn’t do it. We brought the *subjee* to the Bengali men doing the twenty-four-hour *kirtan* in the temple. They got to honor Srila Prabhupada’s remnants that night, and they loved it!

## Watching Pisima Cook

On a Vaisnava holiday Pisima went into the Deity kitchen to cook a *subjee* for the Deities. I was also busy cooking in the kitchen, but I assisted her. Measuring out the spices in my palm for her approval, I tossed them into the hot ghee in a wok. After they snapped and crackled, we added chopped potatoes, cauliflower flowerets and a whole, uncut tomato. She told me to add salt and a lot of water.

Pisima sat near the coal stove, watching the uncovered wok. By the time most of the water boiled away, all the vegetables were nicely cooked into a wet stew, and the tomato had steamed in its own skin. Then Pisima broke the tomato into pieces by jabbing it with a *kunti*, a utensil especially shaped for stirring woks.

I was amazed to see such an unusual cooking method. Keeping the tomato whole instead of cutting it into pieces had prevented it from merging with the other vegetables; and even after it was cut up, it retained its juiciness, individual taste and red color.

## Lemon Pickles

Vrindaban Biharini prabhu liked making homemade pickles. She experimented with many different vegetables and fruits, pickling them in glass jars that she placed in the intense Indian sunlight. After a few weeks, they were ready to offer to Radha-Madhava.

Her specialty was whole lemon pickles, which she said were best after two years. Knowing that Srila Prabhupada liked lemon pickles, she had stashed one jar of them in her room at the ashram, awaiting his arrival. Now that he was in Mayapur, she brought it over to the Deity kitchen. Besides offering pickles to Srila Prabhupada with his lunch *prasadam*, we occasionally offered Radha-Madhava a whole lemon cut into quarters with their evening offering. I got to taste a *maha-prasadam* pickle one day. It was delicious—the entire lemon was eatable, even the skin and pits.

## A Cultured Young Man

One day an unusual, young Bengali man came to Mayapur. Named Kisore, he was a Vaisnava from birth and obviously intelligent. He spoke impeccable English and was also fluent in German because he was a student in a German university. With simply a little coaxing, he surrendered to Srila Prabhupada and quickly got initiated, receiving the name Ksira-cora-gopinatha dasa. On several occasions he kindly translated conversations between Pisima and me. Several months later, Srila Prabhupada awarded this cultured young man *sannyasa*, changing his name to Bhakti Caru Swami.

## Fiji Leaders Visit

Vasudeva dasa and his wife, Bhagavati dasi, from Fiji, came to visit the holy *dhama*. A few years earlier they had briefly met Srila Prabhupada as he passed through a Fiji airport, and they became so inspired by his association that shortly afterward everyone in their family was chanting Hare Krishna. Gradually the Fiji temple developed, and Srila Prabhupada had made Vasudeva the GBC for Fiji.

Construction of their new temple was nearing completion, and Deities of Kaliya-Krishna and the Nagapatnis, created by devotee artisans in Los Angeles, were to be installed. These unique Deities were chosen after a discussion in which Vasudeva said there were no snakes in Fiji, to which Srila Prabhupada had replied, “Oh? Kaliya must be there.” Kaliya, a multi-headed water serpent, was banished from Vrindaban by Lord Krishna because he had poisoned the Yamuna. Chastising Kaliya, Krishna danced on his heads and overpowered him to the point of death. The Nagapatnis, Kaliya’s wives, begged Lord Krishna to forgive their husband, and the Lord spared Kaliya’s life, but ordered him to leave Vrindaban. Kaliya returned to Nagalaya, his original home, which is said by some to correspond to the Fiji Islands. Srila Prabhupada indicated that Kaliya still resides in Fiji, and this led to the decision to install Kaliya-Krishna Deities.

Srila Prabhupada allowed Bhagavati prabhu, who was cooking fantastic Gujarati preparations for him, to go on the altar to get training in Deity worship from Jananivasa prabhu. Several mornings in a row she watched the *pujaris*, learning the art so she could return to Fiji and train the other devotees. Bhagavati was privileged to be allowed on the altar. Never before had I seen a lady go on the altars in Mayapur or Vrindaban. Srila Prabhupada broke the rule out of practicality—Bhagavati needed the knowledge of Deity worship. I loved that quality in Srila Prabhupada—the ability to adjust according to time, place and circumstance.

## Pisima Using the Men’s Bathroom

Temporarily, Bhavananda Maharaja was living a few doors down from Pisima’s room, while a room for him on the roof of the Long Building was being completed. Although many *brahmacaris* had moved into rooms in the Long Building even before the electricity and water were working, Bhavananda Maharaja was waiting for everything to be finished before he moved in.

Living down the hall from Bhavananda Maharaja—and Acyutananda Swami, who was visiting Mayapur—wouldn’t have been a problem if there hadn’t been a conflict over the bathrooms. The first floor had two bathrooms, back to back, at the opposite end of the building from the Deity kitchen. All the *brahmacaris* living in the unfinished Long Building used the men’s bathroom, off the front verandah. Pisima and I wanted to use the ladies’ bathroom, off the back verandah, but Bhavananda Swami commandeered use of that bathroom for himself and Acyutananda Swami.

The problem had started many moons before. The Western ladies objected to the. Bengali ladies and their kids using the ladies’ bathroom during the morning program. In our regular bathroom near the Big Kitchen, we had separate toilet stalls. The Westerners kept their stalls clean and locked. The Bengalis-used to taking care of their bodily functions in the fields-didn’t care to throw a bucket of water into the toilets to flush them. Not only that, the kids didn’t necessarily pass stool inside the toilet, but anywhere on the bathroom floor.

Revolted by the same lack of cleanliness in the shared Lotus Building bathroom, the Western ladies requested it be locked, and Bhavananda Maharaja agreed. I was in charge of the key and keeping the bathroom clean. When the Bengali ladies complained to Bhavananda Maharaja that the bathroom was always locked, he felt pressured to tell me to unlock it for them. It was a vicious cycle and in the end Bhavananda Maharaja blamed me for the mess. He took the key away from me as “a punishment.”

When Pisima had arrived, Bhavananda wouldn’t give back the key. Instead he kept the bathroom reserved exclusively for himself and Acyutananda Swami.

“What bathroom should Pisima and I use?” I asked him.

“Go up to the third floor,” he replied. That was feasible for me, but impossible for Pisima.

“She can’t walk up all those stairs,” I told him. “Then she can use the men’s bathroom.”

Pisima didn’t mind at all walking around to the front verandah in her *gamsha*. Of course, the men were more than surprised to see Pisima using their bathroom, and soon the *brahmacaris* were approaching me, sheepishly saying, “Excuse me, *mataji*, but Pisima is using the men’s bathroom. Could you tell her to use the women’s bathroom instead?”

“I know Pisima s using the men’s bathroom, but this is Bhavananda Maharaja’s arrangement until his room in the Long Building is ready.”

Enough *brahmacaris* were upset about Pisima’s using their bathroom to convince Satsvarupa Maharaja, who had recently arrived to be the monthly GBC secretary, to speak to Srila Prabhupada about it on their behalf. He explained to Srila Prabhupada that the men felt bad that Pisima had to share their bathroom. Srila Prabhupada saw right through their presentation. “She is an old lady,” he said. “She does not care at all about her body. The *brahmacaris* are the ones who are inconvenienced and embarrassed, not her!”

Not long afterward, Bhavananda Maharaja’s room was finished, and we got our bathroom back.

## The Activities of Pisima

Normally I appreciated serving Pisima, and I encouraged devotees visiting Mayapur for the festivals to get her mercy, too, by massaging her feet or whatever they felt inspired to do for her. She was always in a jovial mood and enjoyed the company of the foreign devotees.

Sometimes some of Pisima’s sons visited her. She had eight sons and one daughter. All the sons and their wives and children lived with her in the family house in Calcutta. One son, Govinda, resembled her (and therefore Srila Prabhupada) more than the others.

Although Pisima loved her family, she preferred the association of her elevated brother’s disciples in the holy *dhama*. Srila Prabhupada had told her, “Choose where you want to live—Mayapur or Vrindaban.”

She chose Mayapur.

## Srila Bhaktisiddhanta's Appearance Anniversary

Early one morning Srila Prabhupada told Bhavananda Maharaja, “Today is Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakur’s appearance day. You should offer one hundred and eight preparations.”

We didn’t have anything planned. Bhavananda Maharaja found me chanting *japa* at six a.m. and said, “Go into the kitchen and cook a hundred and eight preps by eleven a.m.”

I replied, “I am off the altar. Madri prabhu is my substitute, and she’s off, too. Vrindaban Biharini prabhu is in Calcutta recovering from an operation. Anirdesavapu dasa is cooking the evening offering because we can’t go in the kitchen.”

“Oh, yes, you can!” he said. “March yourself right into the kitchen and start cooking.”

“All right, but we don’t have any ingredients because we didn’t plan to cook a feast.”

He ordered, “Go to the Big Kitchen and get the *bhoga* you need.” Madri and I followed his mandate and immediately went to get *bhoga*. The Bengali ladies in the Big Kitchen, who always knew when we were *balo na* (“not well”), grumpily told us to get lost. Blaming Bhavananda Maharaja for our presence, we went ahead and gathered the necessary items. The ladies were ready to give Bhavananda Maharaja an earful about this!

By eleven a.m. Madri and I had cooked twenty-five elaborate preparations, and the Bengali ladies in the Big Kitchen had cooked many more, but the total number was short of a hundred and eight. We had done what we could, considering the last-minute notice and so on.

After the offering, we had a quiet festival—especially compared with last year’s, when Srila Prabhupada delivered that fiery speech. Then Srila Prabhupada tasted the *maha-prasadam* and was very satisfied.

“Who cooked?” Srila Prabhupada asked.

“The Bengali ladies,” Bhavananda Maharaja answered, not giving Madri and I one smidgen of credit because he was peeved that we had not made enough preps.

“These Bengali ladies really know how to cook,” Srila Prabhupada conceded. “The Western ladies should learn how to cook from them. They know the art of cooking.”

“You are right, Srila Prabhupada.”

“I really liked the bindi *subjee*—that was the best.”

Wanting to find out who cooked the *bindi* (okra) *subjee,* Bhavananda Maharaja walked over to the Big Kitchen. As soon as he entered, the ladies barraged him with complaints for allowing Madri and I to cook when we were off the altar.

After agreeing to never do that again, he asked, “Who cooked the *bindi subjee?*”

“No one—we didn’t have any *bindi.*”

Later he asked Madri and I the same question, but we said, “Not us. We didn’t have any either.”

We were curious which *subjee* Srila Prabhupada had liked so much. Talking it over with Palika, we learned that once Srila Prabhupada referred to portals as *"bindi."* And there were a few *portal subjees,* one of which I had cooked. But we couldn’t really figure out who had cooked Srila Prabhupada’s favorite *subjee* that day.

Even in the unlikely event it was the one I cooked, still what Srila Prabhupada said held true—the Bengali ladies were superior cooks and could make a feast out of nothing. Practically without a variety of ingredients, they could make a feast with many varieties of flavors. I saw that illustrated one season when there were only potatoes, sweet potatoes and pumpkins available—and nevertheless they made an assorted feast.

## The Gaura Purnima Festival

Once the festival officially started, Srila Prabhupada’s health declined noticeably. He became so ill that he gave permission for the devotees worldwide to chant a special prayer to Lord Nrsimha-deva for His protection and to start a twenty-four-hour *kirtan****,*** just as we had done in August, 1974, in light of Srila Prabhupada’s poor health. Despite his poor physical condition, Srila Prabhupada gave the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class on four mornings. But even when he didn’t, he sat on the *vyasasana* during class and listened to his disciples’ discourses. He didn’t look at all well, but he obviously didn’t want to disappoint the devotees who had traveled so far to see their cherished spiritual master.

I heard about his Ayurvedic medicine—so bitter, he told one devotee, that if he were younger, three or four strong men would have to hold him down and force him to take it. Nothing would make it less bitter, either. But he took it.

Seeing Srila Prabhupada getting weaker daily was quite disturbing, but I wasn’t thinking he might leave his body in the near future. An astrologer had predicted he would live to be one hundred, and I earnestly believed that would happen.

The festival *parikramas* went out as usual during the day. One night we were thrilled to see Srila Prabhupada attend the evening entertainment. Sitting on a raised platform, he watched American devotees perform a drama. I had a hard time choosing which to watch-the drama or Srila Prabhupada. Many others had the same difficulty, and eyes were going back and forth to catch Srila Prabhupada’s expressions in reaction to the dramatic scenes. He enjoyed the Krishna conscious play.

After the Mayapur portion of the Gaura Purnima festival, Srila Prabhupada stayed in Mayapur, too ill to travel. The visiting devotees went to Vrindaban without him, which left Mayapur very quiet once again.

## Cleaning Srila Prabhupada's Quarters

Madri prabhu and I became the crew cleaning Srila Prabhupada’s rooms when the festival ended. Although being a maid or a janitor is a job with little esteem in worldly terms, we found cleaning the floors for a pure devotee, the representative of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, to be enlivening and enjoyable. It made us happy—just the two of us cleaning the rooms and seeing Srila Prabhupada nearby—even though we were disturbed to see him unwell.

The normal time to clean Srila Prabhupada’s quarters was during his morning walk, but these days Srila Prabhupada never left the second floor of the Lotus Building. Thus we had to clean while Srila Prabhupada was in his rooms, which presented the problem of how to clean without inconveniencing him. We solved this by playing a variety of “musical chairs” with Srila Prabhupada: whichever room he was in, we would clean the other room, and when he went to the bathroom or onto the verandah, we would scoot into the room he just left and start cleaning it. Returning, he would notice our activity and accommodate us by going into the clean room.

One day he was so sick that he did not get out of bed while we were cleaning his *darshan* room. We waited a while to see if he would rise, but we had to forego cleaning his bedroom that day.

Another complication was that Madri prabhu didn’t want to be seen by Tamal Krishna Maharaja, who was now Srila Prabhupada’s GBC servant, since etiquette called for a widow to avoid her former husband.

Understanding that Maharaja’s widow was cleaning his rooms, one day Srila Prabhupada came looking for her. Madri and I were on either side of his bed, making it up. He surprised us by walking in through the door from the back verandah. I was standing right by the door, only a few feet away from him. Although he was saying something, I couldn’t understand it, and I reacted to his entrance impulsively by hitting the floor to offer obeisances.

Getting up, I asked, “What, Srila Prabhupada?”

Looking at me, Srila Prabhupada repeated, “Madri? Madri?” Madri was on the other side of the bed, hiding shyly behind the canopy that held the mosquito net.

Pointing, I said, “Madri is over there.” He walked around the bed and saw her. “Yes, Srila Prabhupada?” she asked with folded hands. “Madri, how are you? Is everything all right?”

“Yes, Srila Prabhupada, I’m fine.”

They talked briefly, and later Madri prabhu said it really touched her heart that Srila Prabhupada cared so much about her. Feeling privileged to see this display of Srila Prabhupada’s love, his reaching out to his female disciple, I also felt touched by his concern for Madri’s widowed situation, especially since I was wearing white and had a shaved head, identifying with being a widow.

## Leaving Mayapur

At the slightest sign of getting better, Srila Prabhupada talked to his servants about going to Bombay for a *pandal* program scheduled around Rama Naumi. Enlivened by the prospect of preaching, he improved daily. Although still weak, Srila Prabhupada completed his plans to leave Mayapur. Preaching opportunities awaited him in Bombay.

Hearing that I wanted to leave Mayapur because my health was poor (too many digestive problems), Palika prabhu asked me to come to Bombay to assist her in her new service. She was setting up a filing system for the huge stack of paperwork generated by Gargamuni Maharaja’s traveling “library party.” For years they had distributed sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books to Indian college and university libraries.

“It should take about two months to complete an efficient filing system so that as new volumes are published, books can be sent out to those libraries now holding standing orders,” she said.

I agreed to go to Bombay.

Although this time no one stopped my plans to leave Mayapur, Bhavananda Maharaja gave me a hard time when I asked for my passport. He had my passport because some time back a Western devotee living in India had blooped and Srila Prabhupada said that if the authorities had been holding his passport, he couldn’t have left so easily. After that, all the Western devotees had to hand over their passports to the temple authorities.

I was used to being treated like a lower-class citizen, but the treatment became extremely unfair when, in front of a group of men at his daily business meeting, Bhavananda Maharaja called me “a widow on the loose.” Not only that, he accused me of wanting to leave Mayapur to “have sex in the bushes.” The words burned my ears, and I felt so humiliated. Some of the men, feeling my pain, looked at me with pity in their eyes. Ultimately, Bhavananda Maharaja gave me my passport.

Cars were arranged to take Srila Prabhupada and his group to Calcutta. He asked his servants to bring with them a large metal tin of locally grown Govinda Bhog rice. His tiffin was filled with *prasadam*. Pisima was a bit apprehensive about leaving Mayapur because she was afraid Bhavananda Maharaja wouldn’t allow her to return there to live. Still, she agreed to come with me to Calcutta. We left Mayapur in a separate car shortly after Srila Prabhupada’s party departed.

## Chapter 17 - Calcutta/Bombay—March-May, 1977

## Making Sandesa in Calcutta

After depositing Pisima at her house, I went to the Calcutta temple on Albert Road. Srila Prabhupada was there in his room. I wanted to cook *sandesa* for Srila Prabhupada because I knew he liked this snack early in the morning when he translated. I gathered ingredients and proceeded to curdle the milk, press the curds to make *panir*, knead it nicely, add the sugar and slowly cook it to just the right dryness—following the exact science Vrindaban Biharini prabhu had taught me. She learned the art of *sandesa*-making from American-born Sumukhi dasi, who’d previously lived in the Calcutta temple and was dubbed, unbeknownst to her, “the *sandesa acarya*.”

Finished, I left the *sandesa* in the kitchen.

A few hours later I returned and was absolutely horrified to find it gone. Someone took it! I was devastated.

Sumana dasi, a Calcutta devotee, was in the Deity kitchen cooking the evening offering. She begged me, “Please make it again!”

“But there’s no time—it’s too late!” I lamented.

“No, no! It’s not too late! I have some *panir* that I made for the Deities’ offering,” Sumana prabhu said. “Please, take this *panir* and make the *sandesa* again for Srila Prabhupada!”

I accepted her kind offering and started kneading the *panir*, making it again. This time I immediately gave the *sandesa* to Srila Prabhupada’s servant, Nava-yogendra prabhu.

Early the next morning I was in the temple room chanting *japa* before *mangal-arati*. Suddenly Nava-yogendra prabhu, a *brahmacari* who never spoke unnecessarily with women, came right up to me with a wonderful message.

“Srila Prabhupada said your *sandesa* was very good.”

The perfection of my life! I pleased Srila Prabhupada.

## Arrival in Bombay

Srila Prabhupada had already arrived in Bombay when I got there, but I soon heard about his arrival. Srila Prabhupada’s car was headed toward his flat at the back of the property; however, he demanded that the car stop sooner, because he wanted to stay in his new quarters on a top floor of the unfinished twin towers.

“Your rooms aren’t ready, Srila Prabhupada,” he was informed.

But Srila Prabhupada insisted. He said he never wanted to see his old flat again. Although the devotees couldn’t quite understand why his insistence, they submitted to his desire and carried Srila Prabhupada up seven flights of stairs, because the elevators weren’t ready, either.

The “rooms” were a construction site. Workers were everywhere. Some workers were making a lot of noise and a big mess in the main *darshan* room by wet-polishing the marble floors. Devotees scurried around, cleaning the bedroom floor and moving in furniture to make it somewhat livable. Srila Prabhupada was happy there, despite the inconveniences.

However, after only one night, he agreed to move to Kartikeya Mahadevia’s Bombay home—conveniently located near the *pandal* site—for the duration of the preaching program. Thus he avoided the one-hour drive back and forth to the Juhu Beach temple. And this gave Saurabha prabhu and his crew five or six days’ time to complete Srila Prabhupada’s quarters.

I hadn’t been in Bombay since November, 1975. What a pleasant surprise it was to see that the new temple had manifested. The water-filled holes were a thing of the past, and the shed-like temple room was nearly obsolete.

One day I saw laborers remove a large tree—from where Radha-Rasabihari’s altar would eventually be—and replant it in the new temple courtyard. This was no small feat and required many hands. The brains behind this operation was Srila Prabhupada, who not only saved the tree but also provided the new courtyard with a beautiful, shady tree, just like in our Vrindaban temple courtyard.

## The Bombay Pandal Program

During the five-day *pandal* program in Azad Maidan, a Bombay park, we would be chanting Hare Krishna on the stage in the evening when Srila Prabhupada arrived. He didn’t look well, but he sat regally on his *vyasasana*, brought from the temple. When he spoke, first his voice sounded weak, but gradually it grew in power, and at all times I could feel the conviction of his words. However, some nights he was too sick to speak. Instead, he listened to his disciples’ discourses on Krishna consciousness.

The programs were attended each evening by about a thousand pious people, drawn to see and enjoy Srila Prabhupada’s white elephants dancing and chanting.

Rama Naumi day was slotted for initiations—live onstage. Nine of the ten devotees received names related to Lord Ramacandra.

Once the whole *pandal* program ended, Srila Prabhupada returned to the Juhu Beach temple to live in his now completed rooms, so wonderfully designed and appointed that they were fit for royalty.

“If I hadn’t insisted in staying in my new rooms, you never would have finished them!” he told Saurabha prabhu.

## Guru-puja

The life-membership preachers could bring guests to see Srila Prabhupada, but the rest of us rarely saw him, because of his poor health. One morning at *guru-puja* Srila Prabhupada made a surprise appearance. After we sang “*Guru-vandana*” and the *arati* was complete, he gave a spontaneous talk. Asking us if we knew the meaning of the words we’d just sung, he spoke in particular about the words “*divya-jnana,*” in the third verse.

He said, “‘*Divya-jnana*’ means transcendental knowledge coming from a pure devotee.”

Because Srila Prabhupada emphasized that idea, my understanding was that it was the most important point in the prayer. We worship the guru because he enlightens us with knowledge—not ordinary knowledge, but transcendental knowledge.

## Gargamuni and the Almirah

Palika prabhu and I set up an office in Srila Prabhupada’s old rooms and absorbed ourselves in sorting through years of accumulated paperwork for Gargamuni Swami’s library party.

One day while we were busy working, Gargamuni Swami told us that he had just asked Srila Prabhupada if he could have the almirah, a movable wardrobe, that got left behind in the old quarters. Srila Prabhupada had challenged him to distribute ten standing orders for his books—then he could have the almirah. After telling us this, Gargamuni Maharaja headed off to take a nap.

He woke up to find a telegram had arrived, saying that his traveling parties had distributed ten sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books! Triumphantly he brought the telegram to Srila Prabhupada, who gladly gave Gargamuni Swami his reward.

## The Initiation Name Book

Tamal Krishna Maharaja, continuing on as Srila Prabhupada’s GBC secretary, sent a message for me to come to Srila Prabhupada’s quarters. I trekked up the stairs to the seventh floor and spoke with Maharaja in the outer office. He requested me to retype the initiation name book, the list of Srila Prabhupada’s disciples maintained by his secretary. So many additional handwritten names were tightly packed in between the originally typed names that each page required re-alphabetization before being retyped.

Tamal Krishna Maharaja wanted it done in a month—by the time Srila Prabhupada was to leave Bombay. Palika didn’t want to give me time from my work on Gargamuni Swami’s filing system, but I did it anyway, by sneaking in an hour a day until it was finished. Without the convenience of having a phone on which to call Tamal Krishna Maharaja, I had to traipse upstairs sometimes to confer with him; that was as close as I got to Srila Prabhupada in his quarters.

## The Twenty-Four-Hour Bhajans

Mr. Sethi was a friend of both Srila Prabhupada and the Bombay devotees. For years he had regularly been inviting all the temple devotees to his house for *kirtan* and *prasadam*, highlighted by his wife’s famous, hot sweet rice.

He belonged to a *bhajan* group that met once a month, and the entire group chanted the Hare Krishna maha-mantra for twenty-four hours nonstop. He arranged that the group did their monthly *bhajan* at our temple one Sunday. During their chanting, Srila Prabhupada came out to his bedroom balcony, overlooking the old temple room.

Observing the scene, he sent down word: “Where are our devotees?”

The word spread fast: “Srila Prabhupada is on the balcony and wants us to join the *bhajan* group.”

Some of us joined in, but just for an hour or two. Later, we heard that Srila Prabhupada asked Giriraja dasa, our temple president: “Can our devotees do this every Sunday—chant all day? Our chanters can’t do that, can they?”

To please Srila Prabhupada, the devotees did have *kirtan* all day the following Sunday, but we did it in shifts. The ladies’ shift was in the early afternoon, when there was no *darshan* because the Deities were resting. We were almost resting ourselves because it was quite hot in the temple room and we’d just eaten lunch.

Srila Prabhupada was right: Our twenty-four-hour *bhajans* lasted only a few weeks.

## Seeing Srila Prabhupada for the Last Time

Srila Prabhupada decided to leave Bombay to try to regain his health in Hrisikesh, a holy city in the foothills of the Himalayas. I was glad to be able to see Srila Prabhupada, even though he was leaving. Gargamuni Maharaja drove Srila Prabhupada to the airport in one of the three Ambassadors—complete with ISKCON lotus-flower logos on the doors—that he bought for Srila Prabhupada’s use. (The other two cars were used in Calutta/Mayapur and Delhi/Vrindaban.) Several ladies and I grabbed a ride with a life member. On the way to the airport, we followed directly behind Srila Prabhupada’s car.

We were told that someone had arranged for permission to drive Srila Prabhupada onto the airfield, right to the plane’s stairway. Therefore we weren’t surprised when Srila Prabhupada’s car went off in the opposite direction of the parking lot. We ran inside the terminal and upstairs to the observation tower on the roof—the same place I’d lost my *kartals* two years before. We looked for Srila Prabhupada’s car, but couldn’t see it.

“We missed him,” someone said. “He must be inside the airplane already.”

We stayed around, watching several planes take off. We assumed that one of those planes must be Srila Prabhupada’s.

“He’s gone,” I thought, “and we never figured out which plane was his.”

We left the terminal. I was walking a bit ahead of the other ladies, meandering through the parking lot towards the car we came in. In the distance I noticed what looked like Gargamuni Maharaja’s Ambassador at the other end of the lot. I walked quickly in that direction—the other ladies were now following behind—and realized it was Srila Prabhupada’s car. Both ends of the car—the hood and the trunk—were up and Srila Prabhupada was still inside! He hadn’t left yet!

Close to the car I offered my obeisances. As I was getting up, simultaneously one devotee shut the trunk, while another shut the hood. I stood next to the car with folded hands, smiling at Srila Prabhupada. Inside the car Srila Prabhupada smiled back and raised his arms in the air, signifying “Victory!” That instant, his car started moving. The other ladies arrived and were offering their obeisances just as the car pulled away. That was the last time I saw Srila Prabhupada.

## Chapter 18 - Departures

## Engagement in New York

Srila Prabhupada had left Bombay in mid-May. Two weeks later, my service for the library party was over, and I also left Bombay. Flying back to New York, I again got a service of typesetting—this time in the graphics department of the new Manhattan temple. My boss was Jnanagamya dasa. A week later he asked me to marry him. He disregarded the vast difference in our heights—more than fifteen inches—which was easy, because he was sitting when he proposed! We became engaged.

Besides giving me a ring that he produced from his desk drawer, Jnanagamya gave me a tiny, decorated silver box and instructed me to present it to the presiding Deities, Sri Sri Radha-Govinda, while praying to Them, “If You desire that we marry, please make it possible.”

The next morning, while praying, I slid the box across the altar floor during the Deity greeting. A *pujari* noticed it and picked it up. From then on, I left the matter in Their hands.

It was humiliating to renounce wearing white. Anyway, several ladies in the New York temple who came to the movement with children but without their husbands had also succumbed to the fad of widowhood by donning white; then they had given it up. They had been undermining my widowhood by questioning why I had voluntarily put on white in the first place. To them, it seemed silly, especially since I was free to remarry because I wasn’t a mother; and it was fine to be a widow in India—but in New York?

Within a couple of days of our engagement, somehow Palika prabhu heard about it in India. “Mahamaya is making a mockery of the widow ashram,” Palika complained to Tamal Krishna Maharaja, who quickly contacted Adi Kesava Swami, the GBC for New York.

Adi Kesava Maharaja summoned Jnanagamya prabhu into his office and admonished him: “You can marry anyone you like, but not Mahamaya!”

Besides being pressured from India, we had to contend with Adi Kesava Swami preaching against divorce in the Krsna consciousness movement, from a booklet just published on that topic! Assuming that my previous marriage was properly performed with a fire sacrifice, he wanted to make a case against my remarrying as an example to others. But neither he nor anyone else involved bothered to learn that my previous, so-called marriage was simply New Vrindaban’s flower ceremony.

To dissuade Jnanagamya from marrying me, Adi Kesava instructed him to phone Radhaballabha prabhu, one of my previous authorities. Radhaballabha told him, “Don’t marry her! She’s independent and unsubmissive! But maybe she changed while she was in India.”

Jnanagamya concluded, and rightly so, that I had changed in India. Absorbed in his service, he kept thinking of one of Srila Prabhupada’s mottos, “Utility is the principle”; he wanted to retain me as a typesetter. Besides, he’d already made a commitment to marry me. Soon the temple authorities accepted his decision. Radha-Govinda had made it possible!

Besides the typesetting, I also accepted another service—assisting Visvatomukha dasa in compiling *The Sankirtan Newsletter*. I found it exciting to phone the various centers to gather the reports of their book distribution and then to type up the scores. The results were listed in four categories according to the size of the temples. The names of the book distributors became quite familiar to me. This weekly publication circulated all over the movement, to more than a hundred leaders and centers, but mostly importantly was mailed to Srila Prabhupada, who loved to hear the worldwide results of book distribution.

## A Letter from Pisima

Before leaving Bombay, I’d written a letter to Pisima, but hadn’t received a reply. As soon as I arrived in New York, I’d written to her again. Now someone—a friend or relative—had helped her reply in English:

Sri Sri Guru Gauranga Jaita:-  
Calcutta  
22.6.77  
Srimatya Vaisnavi Charaney Asankha Dandabat,

My most affectionate Mahamaya,

Three days back I have received your letter dated May 31, 1977 which was redirected from Albert Road Temple, Calcutta. I am so much glad on receiving your letter that I could not express in my own language. It is the grace of Krishna “Who” inspired you to write to me such a beautiful letter and also it upgrades my devotion to Lord Krishna. It is also my bad luck that I could not meet you in India and most probably you know it is due to my bad health. You and the devotees in your place want to know about the health of Prabhupada. So far it comes to my knowledge that the health of Srila Prabhupada is little better. Prabhupada advises us to chant “Mahamantra” as much as we can and that is the only medicine which keeps him (Prabhupada) well.

I come to know from your letter that the Deities in the New York Mandir are named Radha-Govinda like ours in Calcutta. You are serving there in various ways on account of recent “Rathayatra.” Please try to keep your health in good condition so that you may serve better with your better health. I am not well here. Please keep me in touch with all the best with you. I have not received your letter written from Bombay. Please write to my own address.

My address:-

Smt. Bhaba Tarini Devi,  
76, Durga Charan Doctor Road  
Calcutta, 700 014

With my heartiest blessings to all of you and you.

Yours “Pisima”

## Srila Prabhupada’s Departure—November, 1977

On Govardhana-puja day, Jnanagamya prabhu and I got married. A wedding fire sacrifice was performed in front of Sri Sri Radha-Govinda, witnessed by a few devotees and some of my family members. Most of the devotees were not in the temple celebrating, but were out on Maha-Saturday *Sankirtan*, because the leaders decided Govardhana-puja would be celebrated the following day, during the Sunday feast.

Two days later, on November 14, 1977, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada left this mortal world and returned back home, back to Godhead. In Vrindaban-*dhama* surrounded by loving disciples who were chanting Hare Krishna, Srila Prabhupada uttered his last “Hare Krishna,” leaving us spiritual orphans, who cried in agony at losing his personal association. Our only comfort was that he lives forever in his vani, his instructions, as Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakur expressed:

*He reasons ill who tells that Vaisnavas die*  
*When thou are living still in sound.*  
*The Vaisnavas die to live and living try*  
*To spread a holy life around!*

As his eternal follower, I prayed to always serve His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada.

## More Association with Pisima

My husband and I attended the Gaura Purnima festival in March, 1979. Afterwards we visited Pisima at her family home in Calcutta. She was overjoyed to see me and to meet my husband. Candra-mukhi, Dayananda’s twelve-year-old daughter, had come with us, and she understood quite a lot of Bengali, so we could communicate well with Pisima.

As Srila Prabhupada’s younger sister, now eighty years old, held my hands, twirling my new conchshell-and-red bangles, which were equal to a wedding ring for Bengalis, she repeatedly instructed my husband to buy me gold bangles.

Pisima reminisced about our days together in Mayapur, thanking me for all my service, especially for shooing the mosquitoes out of her net. Then she got upset—and was even weeping—because she wasn’t being allowed to live in Mayapur, which she fervently wanted to do.

Months later, in January, 1980, Pisima wrote again saying that now she was allowed to live at our Mayapur temple. Her health was deteriorating, but true to form, she was praising Srila Prabhupada and all the devotees:

Room 128  
Sri Mayapur Chandrodaya Temple  
P.O. Sri Mayapur  
Dist. Nadia, West Bengal  
INDIA

D/- 19.1.80

My dear Mahamaya,

Your letter of 18.12.79 reached me through my son as you remitted same to my home address, contents of which has pleased me very much.

However, first of all you take the blessings of your Srila Prabhupada as well of myself. Surely you and your husband have got the divine grace of Sri Sri Chaitanya dev and by dint of which both of you have proceeded so much to the divine instruction of your Srila Prabhupada. I am so glad, which is inexplicable in a letter, that you all are thinking of me so much. You will understand from the address of this letter that now I am in Sri Mayapur and if you come you will see me here.

Although my days are numbered now, yet I think that the services of worship, of you, all the disciples and the devotees of the ISKCON are all through being guided by your Srila Prabhupada from behind and thus it is presuming that HE is still with you alive and such sense seems to be continued.

I am simply a poor lady, but you are all respecting and loving me so much, taking me as your PISIMA, by which my life has become blessed.

Services to Baishnava means to serve the LORD KRISHNA. So that I hope you and your husband’s work will make to proceed more to the divine feet of the LORD KRISHNA. It is also the liberal blessings of HIM that you are engaged in the works to make HIS thought developed in the magazine of BACK TO GODHEAD. I also trust that such method of work of you and your husband will show this path of having the LORD KRISHNA’s direct blessings. Further, I believe that both of your good works for this next world (i.e. divinity) apart from this worldly life have materialized to the mind of yours through the blessings of SREMATI RADHARANI.

I am very much happy of living here amidst so much disciples and devotees of your Prabhupada. It is needless to mention that all the Maharajas and Baishnabs are always trying to their best to make me joyous through the uncommon respect as to their PISIMA, which is making me guilty for paying so much respect to me, as I am simply a mere lady and I can’t do anything for them. But I am sure they are proceeding to their adoration as for blessings of their Srila Prabhupada.

In the conclusion I bless you all by the grace of my Gurudev and as well as by the grace of my brother your Srila Prabhupada to go ahead to the performance of the truth which is my utmost desire.

Your Pisima

The only truth in this world is:-

“HARE KRISHNA HARE KRISHNA  
KRISHNA KRISHNA HARE HARE  
HARE RAMA HARE RAMA  
RAMA RAMA HARE HARE

Which is the only chanting of Kali-yuga.

PISIMA

## Pisima’s Departure—March, 1980

During the Gaura Purnima festival of 1980 many devotees visited Pisima on her deathbed, thinking she would leave her body on Gaura Purnima, but she didn’t. The next day, the visiting devotees left for Vrindaban.

Several days later, Pisima asked Ananga-manjari prabhu, her personal servant, “When is Ekadasi?”

“Tomorrow, Pisima,” was the reply.

The next morning she again asked: “When is Ekadasi?”

“Today is Ekadasi,” Ananga-manjari replied.

That evening, during evening *arati*, amid devotees chanting Hare Krishna, Pisima left her body, following her exalted brother back home, back to Godhead. Lord Caitanya fulfilled Pisima’s wish to leave her body in Mayapur.

Amazingly enough, my son, Rasacarya dasa, was born four years later on the Ekadasi after Gaura Purnima.

## Epilogue

## A Vyasa-puja Offering to Srila Prabhupada

Dear Srila Prabhupada,

A few years ago I had a very realistic dream about you. Devotees were saying those nectarean words: “Srila Prabhupada is coming! Srila Prabhupada is coming!” I was skeptical, because I knew you’d left the material world. Some devotees went to the airport to pick you up, and I was still skeptical. I was in the Los Angeles temple room, helping with the final details of decorating, when I heard the *kirtan* party heralding your arrival—the devotees were returning with you from the airport. I was watching the door when, to my great surprise, Your Divine Grace walked in! All my skepticism vanished. I was so satisfied to be in your presence again.

Then, in the dream, you sat on the *vyasasana*. Although hundreds of devotees were packed into the temple room, you looked right at me, singling me out of the crowd by speaking just to me.

You said something like: “I hope you are not upset with me because I left you.”

I tried to answer: “No, Srila Prabhupada, please don’t apologize for anything!”—but I could not speak. My throat was completely choked, and it was all I could do to hold back my tears. I felt overwhelmed by your love and compassion and by your humble apology for any sorrow I’d had to feel in your physical absence.

I can understand now that you were fulfilling my desire to be noticed by you, even in a crowd of devotees. You were also showing me that you care deeply about us. Most importantly, you were telling me that you are indeed present. I should never doubt it. You are here, in your Hare Krishna movement, and, I hope and pray, always in my heart.

Your servant,

Mahamaya-devi dasi

Thank you to Nanda-devi dasi, who listened to and directed my ramblings as I taped my memories, thus forming the foundation of this book. Both Raghunandini-devi dasi and Kanya-kumari-devi dasi helped transcribe those tapes.

I am very grateful for the excellent editorial advice of Tattvavit dasa brahmacari.

I would like to thank the many devotees who helped with the photos: Gangamayi-devi dasi and Annada-devi dasi, who lent the cover photo of Srila Prabhupada; Dulal-candra dasa, who lent the rare Road Show photos; Ranjit dasa, who helped select the Bhaktivedanta Archives photos; and especially Nitya-trpta-devi dasi, who designed the covers and prepared all the photos for printing

My son, Rasacarya dasa, deserves a big thanks for tolerating me during the many months I was absorbed in writing and re-writing. Also he shared his knowledge of desktop publishing with me and cooperated in numerous other ways in book production.

I wish to thank my husband, Jnanagamya dasa, for all his help and support over the years.

And finally thanks to all the readers of *Ganga Safari* who gave me such wonderful feedback that I had the strength to stop procrastinating, get this book off the back-burner, and turn it into a reality.

All glories to Srila Prabhupada!
